A Recursive Domain- and Objective -Adaptive Frank-Wolfe Algorithm


Abstract




Marcel Kaiser

marcel.kaiser@uni-konstanz.de

Department of Computer Science, University of Konstanz, Germany

Tobias Sutter

tobias.sutter@unisg.ch

Department of Economics, University of St.Gallen, Switzerland

Abstract

We investigate a recursive variant of the classical Frank-Wolfe algorithm for minimizing a convex differentiable function over a convex compact domain.
Unlike the traditional setting, we assume that both the problem domain and the objective function are initially unknown and must be learned from data.
To address this, we integrate estimators into the optimization process, allowing the algorithm to iteratively refine approximations of the problem domain and the objective function.
Our approach maintains the projection-free nature of the classical Frank-Wolfe algorithm while adapting to the uncertainty inherent in data-driven settings.
We establish convergence guarantees for the recursive method, showing that the optimization error scales with the accuracy of the learned estimators.
Two experiments support our theoretical findings, demonstrating that the proposed method achieves convergence behavior comparable to that of the classical Frank-Wolfe algorithm under exact knowledge of the problem domain and objective function, while offering significant computational savings.

Keywords.
Frank-Wolfe Algorithm
\cdot
Conditional Gradient Method
\cdot
Projection-Free Optimization
\cdot

Data-Driven
Optimization
\cdot
Convergence Analysis

1.  Introduction

Convex optimization lies at the core of modern machine learning, statistics, operations research, and control.
Among the many algorithms developed for dealing with structured constrained convex minimization problems, the Frank-Wolfe algorithm (also known as the conditional gradient method) stands out for its elegance and efficiency and has become an essential part of the algorithmic toolbox in convex optimization, see
[
12
]
for a recent comprehensive textbook.
Consider a general convex minimization problem

\text{minimize }\,f(x)\,\text{ subject to }\,x\in D\hskip 1.00006pt,

(P)

where the problem domain
D
is a compact and convex subset of a finite -dimensional Hilbert space
H
, equipped with the inner product
\langle\hskip 0.50003pt\cdot\mid\hskip-0.50003pt\cdot\hskip 0.50003pt\rangle
, and the objective function
f\colon\dom(f)\to\mathbb{R}
, with
\dom(f)\subseteq H
closed, is a continuously differentiable and convex function.
We denote the optimal value of the problem (
P
) by
\Popt
.
The Frank-Wolfe algorithm iteratively linearizes the objective function
f
at the current iterate
x_{n}\in D
and solves the linear minimization problem

s_{n}\in\argmin\,\{\langle\hskip 0.50003pts\,|\,\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x_{n})\hskip 0.50003pt\rangle\,\colon s\in D\}

(1.1)

at each step
n\in\mathbb{N}
using a linear minimization oracle (LMO), consequently updating along directions that remain feasible without requiring costly projections onto the problem domain
D
.
Indeed, for many structured sets solving the linearized subproblem (
1.1
) is significantly simpler than projecting onto it.
The iterates are then updated using a given step-size

x_{n+1}\,=\,x_{n}+\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}(s_{n}-x_{n}),\quad\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}=\frac{2}{n+2}\hskip 1.00006pt.

(1.2)

This algorithm was introduced in 1956 by Marguerite Frank and Philip Wolfe
[
16
]
and yields a sequence
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
with convergence guarantees of
\mathcal{O}(1/n)
under standard smoothness assumptions on the objective function.
More specifically, it can be shown
[
21
, Theorem 1]
that the generated sequence
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfies

0\,\leq\,f(x_{n})-\Popt\,\leq\,\frac{2\hskip 0.50003ptC_{\hskip-0.59789ptf}}{n+2}\hskip 1.00006pt,

(2)

where
C_{\hskip-0.61455ptf}
is the curvature constant of
f
as defined in
[
21
]
.
The assumption of a bounded curvature constant
C_{\hskip-0.61455ptf}
closely corresponds to the smoothness of the objective function
f
.

Despite its elegant geometry and strong guarantees, the classical Frank-Wolfe algorithm (
1
) presumes
full access
to both the objective function
f
(more precisely its gradient) and the problem domain
D
.
However, this assumption breaks down in many data-driven, online, or interactive settings, where the problem domain and objective function may not be
explicitly known, but can incrementally be learned from data.
That is, the objective function
f
and the problem domain
D
are only accessible via data-dependent sequences of estimators
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(D_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
which are constructed from incoming samples.
These estimators are typically noisy, biased, and evolving, raising significant challenges for optimization algorithms that must simultaneously learn and optimize.
Moreover, the approximation of the domain
D
introduces additional complexity: Linear minimization steps, defined via (
1.1
), are no longer exact and must be executed on a surrogate set.

Our Setting.

We propose and analyze a recursive domain- and objective -adaptive Frank-Wolfe algorithm, where both the objective function
f
and the problem domain
D
are initially unknown and must be approximated from data.
A rigorous formulation of this setting is provided in Section
2
.
In particular, we consider the setting where at each iteration
n\in\mathbb{N}
we only have access to estimators
f_{n}
of the objective function
f
and
D_{n}
of the problem domain
D
.
A naive way to obtain a sequence of approximations on the optimal value
\Popt
of the problem (
P
) would be to construct a sequence
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
defined as the minimizers of the optimization problems

\text{minimize }\,f_{n}(x)\,\text{ subject to }\,x\in D_{n}

(3)

for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
However, since the minimization problem (
P
) and hence also the approximation problems (
3
) may in general be time -consuming to solve, we introduce a computationally cheaper approach.
Motivated by the Frank-Wolfe algorithm (
1
), we define its recursive and adaptive counterpart as follows.

Algorithm 1  –
(Recursive Adaptive Frank-Wolfe Algorithm)

1:

Approximation sequences
(\hskip-0.50003ptD_{n}\hskip-0.50003pt)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of the problem domain
D
and the objective function
f
, respectively. Initial iterate
x_{0}\in\dom(f_{1}\hskip-0.50003pt)
.

2:

for

n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}

do

3:

if

x_{n}\in\dom(f_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}\hskip-0.50003pt)

then

4:

compute
s_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}\hskip-1.49994pt\in\argmin\hskip 1.99997pt\{\langle\hskip 1.00006pts\,|\,\nabla\hskip-1.99997ptf_{n+\hskip-0.39864pt1}(x_{n}\hskip-0.50003pt)\hskip 1.00006pt\rangle\,\colon s\in D_{n+\hskip-0.39864pt1}\}

\triangleright
requires LMOs

5:

set
\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}=2\hskip 0.24994pt/(2+n)

6:

update
x_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}=\hskip 1.99997ptx_{n}+\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}(s_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}-x_{n}\hskip-0.50003pt)

7:

else

\triangleright
may happen only with small probability

8:

set
x_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}=x_{n}

Clearly, the quality of a sequence
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
generated by Algorithm
1
for approximating
\Popt
depends on the approximation quality of two estimator sequences
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(D_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
.
In this paper, we analyze this approximation quality.
We consider a setting in which the sequences of estimators
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(D_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
are modeled as stochastic processes.
These estimators are viewed as functions derived from an increasingly large set of training data.
As the sample size grows, they tend to concentrate around the true underlying objects
f
and
D
, although convergence to these targets is not guaranteed.
For example, we consider a scenario in which the estimators concentrate, with high probability
1-\beta
, within an
\eta
-neighborhood of their respective nominal models, for some small constants
\beta,\eta>0
, as the amount of training data increases.

A motivating example arises in
distributionally robust estimation
, which we illustrate in a simplified setting and discuss it in much greater detail in Section
5
.
Let
\{\mathbb{P}_{\hskip-0.2048pt\theta}\,\colon\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\}
denote a parametric family of probability measures, and let
\mathbf{X}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
be a random variable distributed according to the (unknown) ground-truth distribution
\mathbb{P}_{\hskip-0.2048pt\theta^{\star}}
.
We observe independent and identically distributed samples
\mathbf{X}_{1},\dots,\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.40974ptn}\sim\mathbb{P}_{\hskip-0.2048pt\theta^{\star}}
and denote by
\hat{\mathbb{P}}^{(n)}
the associated empirical probability measure.
For some abstract loss function
\ell:\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}
, we define the objective function
f\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
via

f(\theta)\,=\,\mathbb{E}_{\hskip 0.40974pt\mathbb{P}_{\hskip-0.18398pt\theta}}[\hskip 0.24994pt\ell(\mathbf{X})\hskip 0.24994pt]

for all
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
The feasible domain of interest is characterized by proximity to the true (unknown) distribution,

D\,=\,\bigl\{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:d\hskip 0.24994pt(\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbb{P}_{\hskip-0.2048pt\theta},\mathbb{P}_{\hskip-0.2048pt\theta^{\star}}\hskip-1.00006pt)\leq r\bigr\}\hskip 1.00006pt,

where
r\geq 0
is a robustness radius and
d
is a distance (or divergence) on probability measures.
Since
\mathbb{P}_{\hskip-0.2048pt\theta^{\star}}
is unknown, the set
D
cannot be computed directly.
Instead, one works with the empirical, data-driven approximations

D_{n}\,=\,\bigl\{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:d\hskip 0.24994pt(\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbb{P}_{\hskip-0.2048pt\theta},\hat{\mathbb{P}}^{(n)})\leq r\bigr\}\hskip 1.00006pt,

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
A distributionally robust estimation (or optimization) problem is then given by

\text{minimize }\,f(\theta)\,\text{ subject to }\,\theta\in D,

which is intractable in practice due to the unknown domain
D
but of the form (
P
).
In applications, this motivates replacing
D
with its data-adaptive surrogates
(D_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, a principle that underlies many modern distributionally robust optimization models
[
23
]
.

This paper develops the resulting approximation bounds for the sequence
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
generated by Algorithm
1
.
More specifically, we show how the concentration rates of the approximation sequences
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(D_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
around their respective targets
f
and
D
translate into concentration properties of the sequence
(f(x_{n}))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
towards the optimal value
\Popt
.

Contributions.

The main contributions of this work are as follows.

•

We propose a novel projection-free and recursive optimization algorithm that accommodates noisy,
data-driven
approximations of both the problem domain and the objective function.
The algorithm is formulated within a general probabilistic framework that rigorously captures the interaction between estimator convergence and optimization progress, thereby extending classical Frank-Wolfe theory to dynamic and uncertain settings.

•

We establish asymptotic convergence guarantees, showing that the error
f(x_{n})-\Popt
is governed by the statistical convergence rates of the underlying estimators under standard smoothness and convexity assumptions.
In addition, we derive explicit convergence rates under slightly stronger structural assumptions.

•

We demonstrate how the established convergence rates can be further improved by exploiting strong convexity properties and by assuming that optimizers lie in the relative interior of the feasible sets, features that are well-known to accelerate the classical (non-adaptive) Frank-Wolfe algorithm.

•

Empirical validation on two experiments, including data-driven robust linear-quadratic control, confirms that the theoretical convergence rates accurately reflect practical performance and that the algorithm remains robust even when the estimators are initially inaccurate.

Related Work.

Much of the literature on Frank-Wolfe algorithms has focused on improving their convergence rates.
It is well-known that, under the standard assumptions relevant to our setting, namely, a smooth convex objective function
f
and a compact convex problem domain
D
, the convergence rate stated in (
2
) cannot generally be improved beyond constants
[
21
,
27
]
.
Under stronger regularity conditions, however, such as strong convexity of the problem domain
D
, or gradient dominance properties of
f
, combined with the assumption that the optimal solution lies in the relative interior of the feasible region, the convergence rate (
2
) can be improved to a linear rate, see
[
12
, Section 2.2]
for details.
Recent results reveal that convergence rates of the Frank-Wolfe algorithm are fundamentally governed by geometric properties of the feasible set via the LMO, rather than solely by properties of the objective function
[
33
]
.
Modern Frank-Wolfe methods encompass a broad variety of algorithmic variants beyond the original formulation (
1
), primarily motivated by the goal of accelerating convergence or relaxing assumptions.
Examples include adaptive step-size strategies
[
32
]
, or structural modifications such as away-step Frank-Wolfe methods
[
25
]
, which aim to mitigate “zig-zag” behavior.
A comprehensive treatment of these variants is provided in
[
12
, Section 3]
.
These works, however, all operate in a setting where the optimization problem is fully specified and static, that is, both the objective function
f
and the problem domain
D
are known.

A large body of work extends the Frank-Wolfe framework to settings where the objective function is only partially accessible.
One line of research studies Frank-Wolfe algorithms with inexact gradient information, where the true gradient is replaced by an approximation with bounded error.
As shown in
[
21
, Theorem 2]
, the convergence rate (
2
) is unaffected when the gradient is approximated up to some fixed accuracy
\delta
, resulting in the bound

f(x_{n})-\Popt\,\leq\,\frac{2\hskip 0.50003ptC_{\hskip-0.59789ptf}}{n+2}(1+\delta)\hskip 1.00006pt,

where
\delta\geq 0
measures the deviation between the exact and the inexact gradient.

A similar challenge arises in stochastic optimization problems, where the objective function, or its gradient, is not directly accessible.
In such cases, the true gradient
\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x_{n})
is replaced by an unbiased stochastic estimator
\tilde{\nabla}\hskip-1.00006ptf(x_{n})
, giving rise to stochastic Frank-Wolfe algorithms and their variance-reduced variants
[
19
]
. Under standard assumptions, such as bounded variance of the stochastic gradient, one obtains the expected convergence guarantee

\mathbb{E}[\hskip 0.24994ptf(x_{n})-\Popt]\in\mathcal{O}(1/n)\hskip 1.00006pt,

see
[
12
, Section 4.1]
for an extensive discussion and further references.
Importantly, these methods still assume that the problem domain
D
is fixed and exactly known, such that each linear minimization step is carried out over the true problem domain.

Beyond classical stochastic Frank-Wolfe methods, a number of recent developments have focused on improving the efficiency of projection-free optimization by carefully balancing the use of gradient information and LMO calls.
A prominent example is the Conditional Gradient Sliding (CGS) algorithm
[
26
]
, which introduces a multi-level scheme that intermittently
skips
gradient evaluations while continuing to perform LMO calls. This allows CGS to achieve the optimal convergence rate
\mathcal{O}(1/n)
for smooth convex problems, while simultaneously attaining improved complexity bounds in terms of gradient evaluations. In particular, CGS decouples the computational roles of gradient computations and LMO calls, and can substantially reduce the total number of gradient evaluations compared to the classical Frank-Wolfe algorithm, without sacrificing the optimal order of LMO complexity.

A complementary line of work aims at closing the gap between stochastic and deterministic conditional gradient methods in terms of LMO complexity. The Generalized Stochastic Frank-Wolfe (GSFW) algorithm
[
28
]
achieves this goal in the context of empirical risk minimization with linear prediction by carefully combining stochastic gradient estimates with variance reduction techniques. Their results show that GSFW matches the optimal
\mathcal{O}(1/n)
convergence rate while simultaneously achieving optimal dependence on both stochastic gradient evaluations and LMO calls, thereby resolving the classical “complexity gap” present in earlier stochastic Frank-Wolfe methods.

More broadly, these methods are part of a large and rapidly growing literature on stochastic and large-scale variants of conditional gradient methods.
This includes variance-reduced methods
[
19
,
35
]
, coordinate-randomized Frank-Wolfe schemes
[
24
]
, and distributed projection-free algorithms designed for high-dimensional or decentralized settings
[
6
,
41
]
. Recent unified frameworks
[
30
]
further synthesize these developments by providing general stochastic formulations that recover many of the existing algorithms as special cases and clarify their underlying structural similarities.

Despite their methodological differences, all of the approaches described above share a common modeling assumption: While the objective function (or its gradient) may be stochastic, noisy, or only partially observable, the problem domain
D
is assumed to be fixed and exactly known, such that each linear minimization step is performed over the true problem domain.

In contrast, uncertainty in the constraint set has been studied extensively in the context of data-driven and distributionally robust optimization (DRO), see
[
23
,
38
]
.
In these settings, the feasible region is typically defined via statistical estimators, for instance through ambiguity sets constructed from empirical data.
Such approaches are widely used in operations research, control, and machine learning, where constraints are often derived from uncertain or partially observed systems.
However, the predominant paradigm in this literature is to separate estimation and optimization:
One first constructs a surrogate feasible set based on data, and subsequently solves a deterministic optimization problem over this set.
The interaction between the statistical error of the domain approximation and the optimization dynamics is typically not analyzed at the algorithmic level, and projection-free methods such as the Frank-Wolfe algorithm have received comparatively little attention in this context.

Another related line of work is online learning
[
20
]
, where the objective function may change over time and is revealed sequentially. The goal in this setting is to minimize regret relative to the best fixed decision in hindsight. While this framework shares the feature of evolving objective functions, it differs fundamentally from our setting in both modeling and objectives:
Online learning is typically adversarial and focuses on regret minimization, whereas our approach is statistical in nature and aims at convergence to the true optimum of an underlying problem. Moreover, online learning algorithms generally assume a fixed and known decision set, and thus do not address uncertainty in the feasible domain.

In summary, while the literature on Frank-Wolfe algorithms with unknown, noisy or evolving objective functions is extensive, the domain
D
is typically assumed to be known and fixed.
However, in many data-driven optimization problems, common in operations research, control, and machine learning, the constraint set
D
is derived from data and evolves as new information sequentially becomes available.
To the best of our knowledge, this is the first work to propose and analyze a Frank-Wolfe algorithm for problems in which both the objective function and the constraint set change adaptively over time modeled as statistical estimators.
The class of problems addressed by our approach is broad, encompassing many important settings in distributionally robust and data-driven optimization.

Structure.

The remainder of the paper is organized as follows.
Section
2
formally introduces the stochastic setting of Algorithm
1
.
It constructs the stochastic approximation processes for modeling the approximation sequences
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(D_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of the objective function
f
and the problem domain
D
, creating a stochastic counterpart (Algorithm
2.2
), whose iterates are random variables.
It also introduces some underlying structural assumptions required later for our convergence analysis.
Section
3
develops the theoretical results: First establishing an asymptotic convergence, and then analyzing a convergence rate under slightly stronger structural assumptions.
In Section
4
, we study under which additional assumptions a convergence acceleration can be established, similar to the setting of the classical (non-adaptive) Frank-Wolfe methods.
Finally, Section
5
reports two numerical experiments: (i) a simple academic example illustrating the theoretical bounds and highlighting the impact of domain constructions and (ii) a more challenging, data-driven distributionally robust formulation of the linear quadratic regulator problem.

Notation.

We denote
\mathbb{N}=\{1,2,3,\ldots\}
and
\mathbb{N}_{0}=\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
for the set of natural numbers and nonnegative integers, respectively.
For
n\in\mathbb{N}
we abbreviate
[n]=\{1,\ldots,n\}
and
[n]_{0}=[n]\cup\{0\}
.
For each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we denote
I_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
for the identity matrix
\mathbb{S}_{+}^{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
for the cone of positive semidefinite and
\mathbb{S}_{++}^{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
for the cone of positive definite real matrices.
For a topological space
X
we denote the (relative) interior, (relative) boundary, and closure of a subset
A\subseteq X
by
(\operatorname{rel})\interior(A)
,
(\operatorname{rel})\bd(A)
, and
\cl(A)
, respectively, and the Borel
\sigma
-algebra with
\mathscr{B}(X)
.
For a metric space
(\hskip-0.50003ptX,d\hskip 0.50003pt)
, a point
x\in X
and radius
\rho\geq 0
we denote by
\mathbb{B}(x,\rho)
the
closed
ball centered around
x
, that is,
\mathbb{B}(x,\rho)\,=\,\{y\in X\,\colon d(x,y)\leq\rho\}
.
We denote

\lVert\,\cdot\,\rVert\colon H\to\mathbb{R},\;x\,\mapsto\,\sqrt{\langle\hskip 0.50003ptx\mid x\hskip 0.50003pt\rangle}

for the induced norm and

h\colon H\times H\to\mathbb{R},\;(x,y)\,\mapsto\,\lVert\hskip 0.50003ptx-y\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert

for the induced metric on
H
.
For any point
x\in H
and any
Y\subseteq H
we write
h(x,Y)=\inf\hskip 1.00006pt\{h(x,y)\,\colon y\in Y\}
for the minimal distance of
x
to
Y
,
\diam(Y)=\sup\hskip 1.00006pt\{\lVert\hskip 0.50003pty_{1}-y_{2}\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert\,\colon y_{1},y_{2}\in Y\}
for the diameter of the set
Y
, and
\convex(Y)
for its convex hull in
H
.
Lastly, to distinguish clearly between deterministic and stochastic objects, we will write any random object in
bold
case.

2.  Stochastic Formulation and Algorithm

In this section, we formalize the stochastic framework required to establish rigorous probabilistic convergence guarantees for Algorithm
1
.
To this end, we model the sequences of data-driven approximations
(f_{n}\hskip-0.35004pt)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(\hskip-0.35004ptD_{n}\hskip-0.35004pt)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
which are used as inputs of Algorithm
1
as stochastic processes
\mathbf{F}
and
\mathbf{D}
, respectively.
We introduce the assumptions and regularity properties needed to ensure that this stochastic formulation is well-defined.
Building on these constructions, we then define an abstract stochastic version of Algorithm
1
, which serves as a theoretical foundation for the subsequent convergence analysis.
The presentation is developed for the general case in which
both
the objective function
f
and the domain
D
are unknown and must be approximated.
If either of them is known, the results can typically be adapted by replacing the corresponding stochastic approximation with its exact counterpart.
Simplified or less restrictive versions of the definitions and results that arise in such cases will be pointed out in the accompanying remarks.

2.1.  Stochastic Approximation Processes

Let
(\Omega,\Sigma,\mathbb{P})
be a complete probability space.
To model the data-driven approximation sequences
(f_{n}\hskip-0.35004pt)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of the objective function
f
and
(\hskip-0.35004ptD_{n}\hskip-0.35004pt)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of the domain
D
used in Algorithm
1
, we consider stochastic processes

\mathbf{F}\colon\mathbb{N}\times\Omega\to\mathscr{F}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbf{D}\colon\mathbb{N}\times\Omega\to\mathscr{D}\hskip 1.00006pt,

where
\mathscr{F}
and
\mathscr{D}
denote suitable measurable spaces to be defined below.

Definition of the Domain Space.

Since the problem domain
D
is nonempty, compact, and convex, it is natural to assume that the same holds for all its approximations.
Therefore, we set
\mathscr{D}
to be the set of all nonempty, compact and convex subsets of
H
and define a corresponding metric on it.

Definition 1  –
(Hausdorff Distance)

Let
X,Y\subseteq H
be nonempty and compact sets.
The
Hausdorff distance
between
X
and
Y
is defined as

d_{H}(X,Y)\,\coloneqq\,\max\hskip 1.00006pt\{\hskip 0.50003pt\sup\hskip 1.00006pt\{h(x,Y)\,\colon x\in X\},\hskip 0.50003pt\sup\hskip 1.00006pt\{h(y,X)\,\colon y\in Y\}\}\hskip 1.00006pt.

Alternatively, it can be shown that for
X,Y\subseteq H
nonempty and compact the Hausdorff distance
d_{H}(X,Y)
can be written as

d_{H}(X,Y)\,=\,\inf\hskip 1.00006pt\{\hskip 0.50003pt\rho\geq 0\,\colon X\subseteq Y+\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbb{B}(0,\rho),Y\subseteq X+\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbb{B}(0,\rho)\}\hskip 1.00006pt,

(4)

where in this case
+
stands for the Minkowski addition of sets
[
37
, Chapter 3]
.
It is well-known that
d
defines a metric on the family
\mathscr{K}
of nonempty and compact subsets of
H
and that the resulting metric space
(\mathscr{K}\hskip-1.00006pt,d\hskip 0.50003pt)
is Polish, that is, complete and separable, since
(\hskip-0.50003ptH,h)
itself is Polish
[
5
, Chapter 3]
.
As
\mathscr{D}
is a closed subset of
\mathscr{K}
, completeness and separability are inherited, making
(\hskip-0.35004pt\mathscr{D},d\hskip 0.50003pt)
itself a Polish metric space.
Thus, we obtain the measurable space
(\hskip-0.35004pt\mathscr{D},\mathscr{B}(\hskip-0.50003pt\mathscr{D}))
and can formally consider a stochastic domain approximation process
\mathbf{D}\colon\mathbb{N}\times\Omega\to\mathscr{D}
, which can be interpreted as a sequence
(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.35004pt)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of
\Sigma
-measurable compact and convex random sets in the sense of
[
29
]
.
A natural goal would be to hope that for at least some sample points
\omega\in\Omega
it holds that

\lim_{n\hskip 0.40974pt\to\hskip 0.40974pt\infty}d_{H}(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega),D)\,=\,0\hskip 1.00006pt,

that is, as the number of samples increases, we would like to obtain better approximations of the domain
D
.
However, in general, it may not be feasible to extract perfect approximations, even with an infinite amount of data, let alone for all sample points
\omega\in\Omega
.
Thus, we have to make the following assumption on the asymptotic convergence behavior of the stochastic process
\mathbf{D}
.

Assumption 1

The domain approximation process
\mathbf{D}
is such that there exist constants
\beta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}\in[\hskip 0.24994pt0,1]
and
\eta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}\geq 0
with

\mathbb{P}\hskip-1.99997pt\left[\hskip 0.50003pt\limsup_{n\hskip 0.40974pt\to\hskip 0.40974pt\infty}\hskip 1.00006ptd_{H}(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\hskip 0.50003pt,D)\,\leq\,\eta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}\right]\,\geq\,1-\beta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}\hskip 1.00006pt.

The constant
\beta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}
represents an approximation failure tolerance (for example,
\beta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}=0.05
), while the constant
\eta_{\hskip 0.40974pt1}
quantifies the limiting approximation quality (for example,
\eta_{\hskip 0.40974pt1}=0.1
) that can be obtained with a probability of at least
1-\beta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}
.
If
\eta_{\hskip 0.40974pt1}=0
, the limes superior can be replaced by a usual limes since
d_{H}(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\hskip 0.50003pt,D)\geq 0
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
If additionally
\beta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}=0
, then Assumption
2.1
implies almost sure convergence of the sequence
(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.35004pt)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
to the problem domain
D
in the sense of
[
29
]
.

Definition of the Function Space.

Having established the stochastic framework for the domain process
\mathbf{D}
,
we now turn to the construction of an appropriate function space
\mathscr{F}
for the objective approximation process
\mathbf{F}
.
While conceptually similar, the function setting requires additional care, as the effective domain on which the approximations are defined must remain compatible across iterations.
Since the objective function
f
is convex and continuously differentiable, it again is natural to assume the same for all its approximations.
The main concern is now to find a common domain for these approximations.
Indeed, one might first assume that
D_{n}\subseteq\dom(f_{n}\hskip-0.35004pt)
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
would suffice to run Algorithm
1
.
However, starting from
x_{0}\in\dom(f_{1}\hskip-0.35004pt)
, we have
x_{1}=s_{1}\in D_{1}
since
\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048pt0}=1
, but already
x_{2}
could be located anywhere in
\convex(D_{1}\cup D_{2})
.
In general, for
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we only know that it holds

x_{n}\in\convex\hskip-1.99997pt\left(\hskip 0.74997pt\bigcup\hskip 1.99997pt\{\hskip-0.35004ptD_{k}\colon k\in[n]\hskip 0.24994pt\}\hskip-0.50003pt\right)\hskip-1.00006pt,

such that for
\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}\hskip-0.50003pt(x_{n})
to be well-defined we have to make sure that at least

\convex\hskip-1.99997pt\left(\hskip 0.74997pt\bigcup\,\{\hskip-0.35004ptD_{k}\colon k\in[n]\hskip 0.24994pt\}\hskip-0.50003pt\right)\,\subseteq\,\dom(f_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}\hskip-0.35004pt)\hskip 1.00006pt.

Moreover, the common domain used for the convergence analysis must not depend on a specific sample point.
This motivates the following assumption on the existence of a uniform extension domain.

Assumption 2

The domain approximation process
\mathbf{D}
is such that there exists a constant
\varepsilon\in[\hskip 0.24994pt0,1]
and a uniform extension domain
E\in\mathscr{D}
with
D\subseteq E\subseteq\dom(f)
satisfying

\mathbb{P}\hskip-1.99997pt\left[\hskip 0.74997pt\bigcup\hskip 1.99997pt\{\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\hskip 0.50003pt\colon n\in\mathbb{N}\}\,\subseteq\,E\hskip 0.50003pt\right]\,\geq\,1-\varepsilon\hskip 1.00006pt.

Note that the set

N^{+}\,\coloneqq\,\left\{\hskip 0.50003pt\bigcup\hskip 1.99997pt\{\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\hskip 0.50003pt\colon n\in\mathbb{N}\}\,\subseteq\,E\right\}\,=\,\bigcap\hskip 1.99997pt\{\{\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\,\subseteq\,E\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,\colon n\in\mathbb{N}\hskip 0.24994pt\}\,\subseteq\,\Omega

(5)

is indeed
\Sigma
-measurable due to the measurability of
\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
(see Appendix
B
), such that Assumption
2.1
is well-posed.
The probabilistic constant
\varepsilon
in Assumption
2.1
represents a tolerance for ill-behaved approximation processes
\mathbf{D}
reaching out farther than acceptable (for example,
\varepsilon=0.05
).
By Assumption
2.1
, we know that

\mathbb{P}\hskip-1.99997pt\left[\hskip 0.74997pt\bigcup\hskip 1.99997pt\{\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\hskip 0.50003pt\colon n\in\mathbb{N}\hskip 0.24994pt\}\text{ is bounded}\hskip 0.50003pt\right]\,\geq\,\mathbb{P}\hskip-1.99997pt\left[\hskip 0.50003pt\limsup_{n\hskip 0.40974pt\to\hskip 0.40974pt\infty}\hskip 1.00006ptd_{H}(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\hskip 0.50003pt,D)\,\leq\,\eta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}\right]\,\geq\,1-\beta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}\hskip 1.00006pt,

such that the most restrictive aspect of Assumption
2.1
is the existence of a deterministic extension domain
E
that is independent of the actual sample points.
Furthermore, the domain
\dom(f)
must be large enough to contain such an extension
E
.
Note that, under additional regularity conditions of the objective function
f
, or more specifically its gradient
\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf
,
\dom(f)
may be extended to the whole space
H
, rendering this part redundant
[
4
, Theorem 1.8]
.
When the data originates from bounded or light-tailed distributions, the tolerance
\varepsilon
can often be chosen as zero or at least arbitrarily small, influencing the size of
E
.
We denote
\Delta\coloneqq\diam(E)
for the diameter of the uniform extension domain
E
, a quantity that will be used later.

Remark 1

If the objective function
f
is known, then Assumption
2.1
can be weakened: Instead of a fixed uniform extension domain
E\in\mathscr{D}
, it suffices to assume the existence of a
\Sigma
-measurable map
\mathbf{E}\colon\Omega\to\mathscr{D}
and a constant
\varepsilon_{0}\in[\hskip 0.24994pt0,1]
satisfying

\mathbb{P}\hskip-1.99997pt\left[\hskip 0.74997pt\bigcup\hskip 1.99997pt\{\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\hskip 0.50003pt\colon n\in\mathbb{N}\}\hskip 1.00006pt\cup\hskip 0.50003ptD\,\subseteq\,\mathbf{E}\,\subseteq\,\dom(f)\hskip 0.50003pt\right]\,\geq\,1-\varepsilon_{0}\hskip 1.00006pt.

Here, the set

N_{0}^{+}\,\coloneqq\,\left\{\hskip 0.50003pt\bigcup\hskip 1.99997pt\{\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\hskip 0.50003pt\colon n\in\mathbb{N}\}\cup D\hskip 0.50003pt\,\subseteq\,\mathbf{E}\,\subseteq\,\dom(f)\right\}

(6)

is
\Sigma
-measurable due to the measurability of
\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and the completeness of
(\Omega,\Sigma,\mathbb{P})
.
Note that under Assumption
2.1
the existence of a
\Sigma
-measurable map
\mathbf{E}
satisfying

\mathbb{P}\hskip-1.99997pt\left[\hskip 0.74997pt\bigcup\hskip 1.99997pt\{\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\hskip 0.50003pt\colon n\in\mathbb{N}\}\hskip 1.00006pt\cup\hskip 0.50003ptD\,\subseteq\,\mathbf{E}\hskip 0.50003pt\right]\,\geq\,1-\beta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}

(7)

is guaranteed, such that the only remaining requirement is that
f
is well-defined on this random extension domain
\mathbf{E}
with high probability.
Furthermore, we can guarantee that
\varepsilon_{0}\leq\varepsilon
and for many cases it even holds that
\varepsilon_{0}
can be chosen as zero.
A detailed proof of this remark is provided in Appendix
B.1
.

Considering a uniform extension domain
E
as in Assumption
2.1
, we define

\mathscr{F}\coloneqq\{f\in C^{\hskip 0.2048pt1}\hskip-0.50003pt(E,\mathbb{R})\,\colon f\text{ is convex}\}

and endow
C^{\hskip 0.2048pt1}\hskip-0.50003pt(E,\mathbb{R})
with the supremum norm

\lVert\hskip 0.50003ptf\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert_{\infty}\,\coloneqq\,\sup\hskip 1.00006pt\{\hskip 0.50003pt\lvert\hskip 0.50003ptf(x)\hskip 0.50003pt\rvert\,\colon x\in E\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,+\,\sup\hskip 1.00006pt\{\hskip 0.50003pt\lVert\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x)\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert\,\colon x\in E\hskip 0.50003pt\}\hskip 1.00006pt.

(8)

Since
C^{\hskip 0.2048pt1}\hskip-0.50003pt(E,\mathbb{R})
together with the supremum norm in (
8
) forms a separable Banach space and, therefore, a Polish metric space, the same holds for
\mathscr{F}
as closed subspace
[
1
,
10
]
.
Thus, we obtain another measurable space
(\hskip-0.50003pt\mathscr{F},\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{F}))
and can consider a stochastic objective approximation process
\mathbf{F}\colon\mathbb{N}\times\Omega\to\mathscr{F}
.
Similar to before, we can interpret the stochastic process
\mathbf{F}
as a sequence
(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{F}_{\hskip-0.61455ptn}\hskip-0.35004pt)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of
\Sigma
-measurable convex and continuously differentiable random functions.
Again, rather than assuming exact convergence, we describe its limiting behavior probabilistically.

Assumption 3

The objective approximation process
\mathbf{F}
is such that there exist constants
\beta_{\hskip 0.2048pt2}\in[\hskip 0.24994pt0,1]
and
\eta_{\hskip 0.40974pt2}\geq 0
with

\mathbb{P}\hskip-1.99997pt\left[\hskip 0.50003pt\limsup_{n\hskip 0.40974pt\to\hskip 0.40974pt\infty}\hskip 1.99997pt\lVert\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{F}_{\hskip-0.61455ptn}-f\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert\,\leq\,\eta_{\hskip 0.40974pt2}\hskip 0.50003pt\right]\,\geq\,1-\beta_{\hskip 0.2048pt2}\hskip 1.00006pt.

The constant
\beta_{\hskip 0.2048pt2}
represents an approximation failure tolerance (for example,
\beta_{\hskip 0.2048pt2}=0.05
), while the constant
\eta_{\hskip 0.40974pt2}
quantifies the limiting approximation quality (for example,
\eta_{\hskip 0.40974pt2}=0.1
) that can be obtained with a probability of at least
1-\beta_{\hskip 0.2048pt2}
.
If
\eta_{\hskip 0.40974pt2}=0
, then the limes superior can be replaced with the usual limes since
\lVert\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{F}_{\hskip-0.61455ptn}-f\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert\geq 0
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
If additionally
\beta_{\hskip 0.2048pt2}=0
, then Assumption
2.1
implies almost sure uniform convergence of the sequences
(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{F}_{\hskip-0.61455ptn}\hskip-0.35004pt)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(\nabla\mathbf{F}_{\hskip-0.61455ptn}\hskip-0.35004pt)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
to the objective function
f
and its gradient
\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf
, respectively.

Together, Assumption
2.1
and Assumption
2.1
ensure that both stochastic approximation processes
\mathbf{D}
and
\mathbf{F}
, interpreted as approximation sequences, concentrate around their true counterparts
D
and
f

with joint probability at least
1-(\beta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}+\beta_{\hskip 0.2048pt2})
, while Assumption
2.1
ensures the existence of the approximation process
\mathbf{F}
on a common, deterministic state space
E
.
With these measurable structures in place, we are now prepared to formulate the abstract stochastic version of Algorithm
1
, which we introduce next and which will serve as the main object of the convergence analysis.

2.2.  Stochastic Algorithm Definition and Interpretation

Having established the stochastic approximation spaces
\mathscr{D}
and
\mathscr{F}
and the uniform extension domain
E
, we can now extend Algorithm
1
to a fully stochastic setting in which all iterates and search directions are modeled as random variables on the underlying probability space
(\Omega,\Sigma,\mathbb{P})
.
As a randomized version of an initial iterate, we assume to be given a
\Sigma
-measurable map
\mathbf{X}_{0}\colon\Omega\to H
.
Since the only role of
\mathbf{X}_{0}
is to enable the computation of the initial search direction
\nabla\mathbf{F}_{\hskip-0.81949pt1}\hskip-0.50003pt(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{0}\hskip-0.50003pt)
, it suffices to assume that
\mathbf{X}_{0}\in E
almost surely.
In most cases, the random initial iterate
\mathbf{X}_{0}
may be chosen as a constant
x_{0}\in E
or uniformly distributed on some sensible subset of
E
, such that this part is trivially satisfied.
For the sake of formality, in the following and for the rest of this work, we have to restrict the set
N^{+}
from (
5
) to the set

N\,\coloneqq\,N^{+}\hskip-0.24994pt\cap\hskip 0.50003pt\{\mathbf{X}_{0}\in E\}\in\Sigma\hskip 1.00006pt,

(9)

which is no restriction since if
\mathbf{X}_{0}\in E
almost surely we have
\mathbb{P}[N]\geq 1-\varepsilon
by Assumption
2.1
.

Remark 2

If the objective function
f
is known, then in the setting of Remark
2.1
, the initialization assumption that
\mathbf{X}_{0}\in E
almost surely can be reduced to assume that
\mathbf{X}_{0}\in\mathbf{E}
almost surely, where the event
\{\mathbf{X}_{0}\in\mathbf{E}\hskip 0.24994pt\}
is measurable due to the completeness of
(\Omega,\Sigma,\mathbb{P})
.
Here, as in (
9
) we restrict the set
N_{0}^{+}
from (
6
) to the set

N_{0}\coloneqq N_{0}^{+}\cap\{\mathbf{X}_{0}\in\mathbf{E}\}\,\subseteq\,\Omega\hskip 1.00006pt,

which is
\Sigma
-measurable and satisfies
\mathbb{P}[N_{0}]\geq 1-\varepsilon_{0}
as well.
The following constructions and results in this section then apply with
E
replaced by
\mathbf{E}
,
N
replaced by
N_{0}
, and
\varepsilon
replaced by
\varepsilon_{0}
.
A proof of this remark is provided in Appendix
B.1
.

Algorithmic Processes.

From the random initial iterate
\mathbf{X}_{0}
onward, we can now aim to construct stochastic processes
\mathbf{S}
, modeling the solutions to the linear subproblems in Algorithm
1
, and
\mathbf{X}
, modeling the iterates of Algorithm
1
.
To this end, we first have to analyze how the computation of a solution to the linear subproblems, that is, line
4
in Algorithm
1
, can be modeled in a probabilistic way.
Indeed, since we are dealing with an
\argmin
problem that possibly attains multiple solutions, it is important to abstractly model the selection of such a solution in a way that preserves
\Sigma
-measurability of the resulting search direction.
If the reader is not yet familiar with correspondences (also known as multifunctions or set-valued maps) and the corresponding definitions of measurability, we suggest a look at Appendix
A
for a brief introduction or a study of the corresponding chapters in the textbooks
[
1
,
36
,
13
]
.

Lemma 1

Let
\mathbf{Y}\colon\Omega\to H
be a
\Sigma
-measurable map and let
A\in\Sigma
with
A\subseteq\{\mathbf{Y}\in E\hskip 0.50003pt\}
.
Then, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, the correspondence

\Omega\,\rightrightarrows\,H,\;\omega\,\mapsto\,\argmin\hskip 1.00006pt\{\mathbf{1}_{\hskip-0.39864ptA}\hskip-0.35004pt(\omega)\hskip 1.00006pt\langle\hskip 0.50003pts\,|\,\nabla\mathbf{F}_{\hskip-0.59789ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)(\mathbf{Y}(\omega))\hskip 0.50003pt\rangle\,\colon s\in\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.19925ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)\}

is measurable and admits a
\Sigma
-measurable selector, that is, a map
\sigma\colon\Omega\to H
satisfying

\sigma(\omega)\in\argmin\hskip 1.00006pt\{\mathbf{1}_{\hskip-0.39864ptA}\hskip-0.35004pt(\omega)\hskip 1.00006pt\langle s\,|\,\nabla\mathbf{F}_{\hskip-0.39864ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)(\mathbf{Y}(\omega))\rangle\,\colon s\in\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.19925ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)\}

for all
\omega\in\Omega
.

Since by assumption we have that
\mathbf{X}_{0}
is
\Sigma
-measurable, we can apply Lemma
2.2
to
\mathbf{Y}=\mathbf{X}_{0}
for
A=N
and
n=1
to obtain a
\Sigma
-measurable selector
\mathbf{S}_{1}\colon\Omega\to H
satisfying

\mathbf{S}_{1}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)\in\argmin\hskip 1.00006pt\{\mathbf{1}_{\hskip-0.39864ptN}\hskip-0.35004pt(\omega)\hskip 1.00006pt\langle s\,|\,\nabla\mathbf{F}_{\hskip-0.59789pt1}\hskip-0.50003pt(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{0}(\omega))\rangle\,\colon s\in\mathbf{D}_{1}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)\hskip 0.50003pt\}

for all
\omega\in\Omega
.
Then, we can construct an abstract new iterate
\mathbf{X}_{1}\colon\Omega\to H
by setting

\mathbf{X}_{1}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)\,=\,(1-\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048pt0})\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{X}_{0}(\omega)+\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048pt0}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{S}_{1}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)

for all
\omega\in\Omega
, where
\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048pt0}=2/(2+0)=1
is the step-size chosen as in Algorithm
1
.
The new random iterate
\mathbf{X}_{1}
is now
\Sigma
-measurable as convex combination of
\Sigma
-measurable maps.
Furthermore, it holds

\mathbf{X}_{1}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)\,=\,\mathbf{S}_{1}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)\in\mathbf{D}_{1}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)\,\subseteq\,E

for all sample points
\omega\in N
.
Therefore, we can proceed inductively and apply Lemma
2.2
to
\mathbf{Y}=\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn-\hskip-0.40974pt1}
for
A=N
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
to construct the subsolution process
\mathbf{S}\colon\mathbb{N}\times\Omega\to H
satisfying

\mathbf{S}_{n}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)\in\argmin\hskip 1.00006pt\{\mathbf{1}_{\hskip-0.39864ptN}\hskip-0.35004pt(\omega)\hskip 1.00006pt\langle\hskip 0.50003pts\,|\,\nabla\mathbf{F}_{\hskip-0.59789ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.19925ptn-\hskip-0.39864pt1}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega))\hskip 0.50003pt\rangle\,\colon s\in\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.19925ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)\}

(10)

for all
\omega\in\Omega
and the iterate process
\mathbf{X}\colon\mathbb{N}\times\Omega\to H
satisfying

\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)\,=\,(1-\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn-\hskip-0.40974pt1})\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn-\hskip-0.40974pt1}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)+\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn-\hskip-0.40974pt1}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{S}_{n}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)

(11)

for all
\omega\in\Omega
, where
\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn-\hskip-0.40974pt1}=2/(2+n-1)
as in Algorithm
1
.
Note that defining
\mathbf{S}
as in (
10
) and
\mathbf{X}
as in (
11
), we have
\mathbf{S}_{n}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)\in\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)\subseteq E
as well as
\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)\in E
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and sample points
\omega\in N
, such that under Assumption
2.1
we have

\mathbb{P}[\hskip 1.00006pt\mathbf{S}_{n}\in E\text{ and }\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\in E\text{ for all }n\in\mathbb{N}\hskip 1.00006pt]\,\geq\,1-\varepsilon\hskip 1.00006pt.

In other words, we can guarantee that both, the subsolution process
\mathbf{S}
, and the iterate process
\mathbf{X}
stay inside the uniform extension domain
E
with high probability, such that all evaluations of
f
and
\mathbf{F}_{\hskip-0.61455ptn}
or their gradients are well-defined for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, that is, along the entire stochastic trajectory.

Abstract Stochastic Algorithm.

Now that we have defined the domain approximation processes
\mathbf{D}
and objective approximation process
\mathbf{F}
and derived the resulting subsolution process
\mathbf{S}
and iterate process
\mathbf{X}
, we have formally taken Algorithm
1
to an abstract stochastic level.
A stochastic version of Algorithm
1
is therefore given by Algorithm
2.2
below, which can be read as a compact summary of the construction in (
10
) and (
11
).

Algorithm 2  –
(Recursive Adaptive Stochastic Frank-Wolfe Algorithm)

1:

Domain approximation process
\mathbf{D}
, objective approximation process
\mathbf{F}
, uniform extension domain
E
and initial iterate
\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048pt0}

2:

set
N=\left\{\hskip 0.74997pt\bigcup\hskip 1.00006pt\{\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\hskip 0.50003pt\colon n\in\mathbb{N}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\hskip 1.00006pt\subseteq\,E\hskip 0.24994pt\right\}\cap\{\hskip 0.24994pt\mathbf{X}_{0}\in E\hskip 0.24994pt\}

3:

for

n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}

do

4:

select
\mathbf{S}_{\hskip-0.0819ptn+\hskip-0.40974pt1}\hskip-0.50003pt\in\argmin\hskip 1.00006pt\{\mathbf{1}_{\hskip-0.39864ptN}\langle\hskip 0.50003pts\,|\,\nabla\mathbf{F}_{\hskip-0.59789ptn+\hskip-0.39864pt1}\hskip-0.50003pt(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.19925ptn})\hskip 0.50003pt\rangle\,\colon s\in\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.27905ptn+\hskip-0.39864pt1}\}

5:

set
\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}=2\hskip 0.24994pt/(2+n)

6:

update
\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn+\hskip-0.40974pt1}=\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}+\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip 0.50003pt(\mathbf{S}_{\hskip-0.0819ptn+\hskip-0.40974pt1}-\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn})

We stress that it is important to understand Algorithm
2.2
not as an actual programmable algorithm, but as an entirely abstract algorithm which has the purpose of deriving rigorous probabilistic convergence results that can later be restricted to the deterministic case of Algorithm
1
.
Considering the returned stochastic iterate process
\mathbf{X}
of Algorithm
2.2
, for any sample point
\omega\in\Omega
, we can interpret the corresponding sample sequence
(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq H
as the return of Algorithm
1
.
Thus, any result stating probabilistic properties of
\mathbf{X}
can be understood to hold for the deterministic counterpart
(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
by viewing the stochastic analysis as providing high-probability guarantees for individual realizations of Algorithm
1
fed with random data.
In particular, given an iteration
n\in\mathbb{N}
and a sample point
\omega\in\Omega
, we can interpret

•

\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)
as an
n
-th realized approximation of the problem domain
D
,

•

\mathbf{F}_{\hskip-0.61455ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)
as an
n
-th realized approximation of the objective function
f
,

•

\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)
as the
n
-th iterate of Algorithm
1
if run using the sequences
(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{F}_{\hskip-0.61455ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and the starting point
\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048pt0}(\omega)\in E=\dom(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{F}_{\hskip-0.61455pt1}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega))
,

•

\mathbf{S}_{n}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)
as a solution to the linear subproblem with direction
\nabla\mathbf{F}_{\hskip-0.61455ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn-\hskip-0.40974pt1}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega))
as given in (
10
), considered at the
n
-th iteration.

Therefore, for simplicity in notation, when fixing an iteration
n\in\mathbb{N}
and a sample point
\omega\in\Omega
, we will sometimes use the abbreviations

D_{n}\,=\,\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega),\quad f_{n}\,=\;\mathbf{F}_{\hskip-0.61455ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega),\quad x_{n}\,=\,\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)\quad\text{and}\quad s_{n}\,=\,\mathbf{S}_{n}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)\hskip 1.00006pt,

(12)

reminding of a deterministic setting.

\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.20605ptn}\hskip-0.42502pt(\omega)
\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.20605ptn-\hskip-0.41225pt1}\hskip-0.42502pt(\omega)
\nabla\mathbf{F}_{\hskip-0.6183ptn}\hskip-0.42502pt(\omega)\hskip-0.42502pt(\hskip 0.29753pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.20605ptn-\hskip-0.41225pt1}\hskip-0.42502pt(\omega))
\mathbf{S}_{n}\hskip-0.42502pt(\omega)
\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.20605ptn}\hskip-0.42502pt(\omega)
E
D

Figure 1:

Conceptual illustration of a step (
n-\hskip-0.50003pt1\to n
) of Algorithm
2.2
for some fixed sample point
\omega\in N
, ensuring that everything is contained in the uniform extension domain
E
.
By the previous analysis and using the notation (
12
) this can also be identified with a corresponding step of Algorithm
1
.

3.  Theoretical Analysis and Convergence Results

In this section, we develop a convergence theory for the Algorithm
2.2
.
We start by recalling and tailoring standard tools from deterministic Frank -Wolfe analysis to our stochastic setting.
Then, we derive some baseline
one -step
error bounds that are extended to asymptotic convergence bounds and explicit convergence rates under quantitative assumptions on the approximation processes.
Finally, we briefly comment on how actual domain approximations may be generated via Carathéodory functions and sublevel sets.

3.1.  Stochastic Convergence Analysis Tools

Before turning our attention to the probabilistic convergence analysis, we must introduce some commonly used definitions already used in the deterministic convergence analysis of Frank -Wolfe methods, however, in a slightly more complex environment, since we are dealing with uncertainty.

Curvature Constant.

First, we consider an extension of the so -called curvature constant, which is a tool for measuring the nonlinearity of a function over a given domain by measuring the deviation from its linear approximations.
The curvature constant was originally introduced in
[
14
]
and later revisited in
[
21
]
and has since become a standard smoothness surrogate in several Frank -Wolfe methods
[
17
,
15
]
.

Definition 2  –
(Uniform Curvature Constant)

The
uniform curvature constant
of the objective function
f
on the uniform extension domain
E
is defined as

C\,=\,\sup\hskip-1.00006pt\left\{\hskip-1.00006pt\frac{2\,}{\,\lambda^{\hskip-0.40974pt2}}(f(x+\lambda(s-x))-\hskip-0.50003ptf(x)-\lambda\langle\hskip 0.50003pts-x\,|\,\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x)\hskip 0.50003pt\rangle)\hskip 1.00006pt\,\colon x,s\in E,\lambda\in[\hskip 0.24994pt0,1]\right\}\hskip-0.24994pt.

In general, the uniform curvature constant
C
need not be finite.
However, as already discussed in
[
21
]
, if the objective function
f
is smooth, that is, if
\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf
is Lipschitz continuous with Lipschitz constant
L_{\nabla}\geq 0
, then it holds

C\,\leq\,\diam(E)^{2}L_{\nabla}\hskip 1.00006pt.

(13)

This inequality is a cornerstone of most Frank-Wolfe method convergence analyses, since it allows all nonlinear error terms to be controlled uniformly over the problem domain, independent of the iterates.
In our stochastic setting this plays an even more important role, because the feasible region or objective approximations may change sample -wise.

Remark 3

If the objective function
f
is known, then Definition
3.1
can be adapted to the setting of Remark
2.1
.
In this case, the uniform curvature constant
C
has to be extended to the
\Sigma
-measurable
random curvature constant

\mathbf{C}\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
defined by

\mathbf{C}(\omega)\,=\,\sup\hskip-1.00006pt\left\{\hskip-1.00006pt\mathbf{1}_{\hskip-0.28683ptN_{0}}\hskip-1.00006pt(\omega)\frac{2\,}{\,\lambda^{\hskip-0.40974pt2}}(f(x+\lambda(s-x))-f(x)-\lambda\langle\hskip 0.50003pts-x\,|\,\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x)\hskip 0.50003pt\rangle)\,\colon x,s\in\mathbf{E}(\omega),\lambda\in(0,1]\hskip-0.50003pt\right\}

for all
\omega\in\Omega
.
Here, the use of
(\hskip-0.24994pt0,1]
instead of
[\hskip 0.24994pt0,1]
as in Definition
3.1
has pure measurability reasons but does not affect any of the following results.
Similar to (
13
), we obtain that

\mathbf{C}\,\leq\,\diam(\mathbf{E})^{\hskip-0.2048pt2}L_{\nabla}

if the objective function
f
is smooth.
The random curvature constant plays the same structural role sample -wise as
C
does deterministically.
A detailed proof of this remark can be found in Appendix
B.2
.

Suboptimality Gap.

We consider a quantity originally used for certifying the closeness of
f(x)
and
\Popt
for some given point
x\in D
, called the suboptimality gap.
Given a point
x\in D
it is defined as

g(x)\,=\,\max\hskip 1.00006pt\{\hskip 0.24994pt\langle\hskip 0.50003ptx-s\,|\,\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x)\hskip 0.50003pt\rangle\,\colon s\in D\hskip 0.24994pt\}

and is of particular interest since it can be computed on the fly in the Frank -Wolfe algorithm as can be seen writing

g(x)\,=\,\max\hskip 1.00006pt\{\langle\hskip 0.50003ptx-s\,|\,\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x)\hskip 0.50003pt\rangle\,\colon s\in D\}\,=\,\langle\hskip 0.50003ptx\,|\,\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x)\hskip 0.50003pt\rangle-\min\hskip 1.00006pt\{\langle\hskip 0.50003pts\,|\,\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x)\hskip 0.50003pt\rangle\,\colon s\in D\}\hskip 1.00006pt,

since the second term is exactly the optimal value of the linear subproblem.
Furthermore, the suboptimality gap satisfies
g(x)\geq f(x)-\Popt\geq 0
such that it acts as a natural upper bound for the error of any point
x\in D
.
This tool is widely used in the Frank -Wolfe literature as a computable certificate
[
21
,
25
,
17
]
and, in the following, we consider an abstract version that is defined on the iterates of Algorithm
2.2
.

Definition 3  –
(Suboptimality Gap Process)

The
suboptimality gap process
is defined as the stochastic process

\mathbf{G}\colon\mathbb{N}\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R},\;\mathbf{G}\hskip-0.50003pt(n,\omega)\,\mapsto\,\mathbf{1}_{\hskip-0.40974ptN}\hskip-0.35004pt(\omega)\max\hskip 1.00006pt\{\langle\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)-s\,|\,\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega))\hskip 0.50003pt\rangle\,\colon s\in D\hskip 0.24994pt\}\hskip 1.00006pt.

It follows from standard measurability results
[
36
,
13
]
that the suboptimality gap process forms a well-defined stochastic process.
Similar to above, we can reformulate

\mathbf{G}_{\hskip-0.12291ptn}\,=\,\mathbf{1}_{\hskip-0.40974ptN}\langle\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\,|\,\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt)\hskip 0.50003pt\rangle-\mathbf{1}_{\hskip-0.40974ptN}\hskip-0.50003pt\min\hskip 1.00006pt\{\langle\hskip 0.50003pts\,|\,\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt)\hskip 0.50003pt\rangle\,\colon s\in D\}\hskip 1.00006pt,

such that on
N
there is not much of a difference to the deterministic case.
That the suboptimality gap process can be used as an upper bound as well is due to the following result.

Lemma 2

Let
\varepsilon\in[\hskip 0.24994pt0,1]
be as in Assumption
2.1
.
Then, it holds

\mathbf{G}_{\hskip-0.12291ptn}\,\geq\,f(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt)-\Popt

(14)

on
N
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
In particular, (
14
) holds with probability at least
1-\varepsilon
.

Proof
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
be arbitrary and
\omega\in N
be a fixed sample point.
The convexity of the objective function
f
on
E
implies that

f(s)-f(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega))\,\geq\,\langle\hskip 0.50003pts-\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)\,|\,\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega))\hskip 0.50003pt\rangle

for all
s\in D\subseteq E
, such that taking the minimum over
D
on both sides yields

\displaystyle\Popt\hskip-1.00006pt-\hskip 1.00006ptf(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.19925ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega))

\displaystyle\geq\,\min\hskip 1.00006pt\{\langle\hskip 0.50003pts-\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)\,|\,\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega))\hskip 0.50003pt\rangle\,\colon s\in D\hskip 0.24994pt\}

\displaystyle=\,-\max\hskip 1.00006pt\{\langle\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)-s\,|\,\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega))\hskip 0.50003pt\rangle\,\colon s\in D\hskip 0.24994pt\}\,=\,-\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{G}_{\hskip-0.12291ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)\hskip 1.00006pt.

The second part now follows since
\mathbb{P}[\hskip-0.24994ptN\hskip 0.24994pt]\geq 1-\varepsilon
.
∎

Lipschitz Constant.

Above, we already commented on the Lipschitz continuity of the derivative
\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf
of the objective function
f
.
However, in this work we have to also consider the Lipschitz continuity of the objective function
f
itself.
In the usual analysis of the Frank -Wolfe algorithm this is rather uncommon, however, in stochastic settings, Lipschitz continuity of
f
is important to control value fluctuation between the random iterates.
Luckily, this comes without further restrictions.

Lemma 3

The objective function
f
is Lipschitz continuous on any compact subset of
\dom(f)
.
In particular,
f
is Lipschitz continuous on the uniform extension domain
E
with Lipschitz constant

L\,=\,\max\hskip 1.00006pt\{\hskip 0.50003pt\lVert\hskip 0.50003pt\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x)\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert\hskip 0.50003pt\colon x\in E\hskip 0.24994pt\}\hskip 1.00006pt.

Remark 4

If the objective function
f
is known, then Lemma
3.1
can be adapted to the setting of Remark
2.1
.
In this case, we have to define the
\Sigma
-measurable random Lipschitz constant

\mathbf{L}\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R},\;\omega\,\mapsto\,\max\hskip 1.00006pt\{\mathbf{1}_{\hskip-0.40974ptN_{0}}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)\hskip 0.50003pt\lVert\hskip 0.50003pt\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x)\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert\hskip 0.50003pt\colon x\in\mathbf{E}(\omega)\}\hskip 1.00006pt.

In this way, for all
\omega\in N_{0}
and
x,y\in\mathbf{E}(\omega)
it holds that

\lvert\hskip 0.50003ptf(x)-f(y)\hskip 0.50003pt\rvert\,\leq\,\mathbf{L}(\omega)\hskip 0.50003pt\lVert\hskip 0.50003ptx-y\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert\hskip 1.00006pt,

that is,
f
is Lipschitz continuous on
\mathbf{E}
with probability at least
1-\varepsilon_{0}
.
A detailed proof of this remark is provided in Appendix
B.2
.

3.2.  Preliminaries and Intermediate Results

In this section we will provide some intermediate results on upper and lower iteration and distance bounds.
At first, we consider a general result on deriving upper bounds for a sequence that obeys a recursive structure.
A slightly simpler version of this result can be found in the proof of
[
21
, Theorem 1]
, and will serve as the main tool to convert our descent inequalities into explicit finite -time convergence rates later on.

Proposition 1

Let
(T_{n}\hskip-0.35004pt)_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\subseteq\mathbb{R}
be a sequence.
Let
r\in[\hskip 0.24994pt0,1]
,
A_{1},A_{2}>0
be some fixed constants and let
(\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.35004pt)_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
be our usual step-size sequence.
If there exists some
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
with

T_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}\,\leq\,(1-\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn})\hskip 0.50003ptT_{n}+A_{1}\hskip-0.50003pt\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.81949pt1+r}+A_{2}\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\vskip 2.5pt

(15)

for all
n\geq m
, then

T_{n}\,\leq\,(m+3)\max\hskip 1.00006pt\{\hskip 0.50003pt\lvert\hskip 0.50003ptT_{m}\hskip 0.50003pt\rvert,A_{1}\}\hskip-0.50003pt\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974ptr}+A_{2}

(16)

for all
n\geq m+1
.
If
m=0
, then (
16
) can even be simplified to

T_{n}\,\leq\,2A_{1}\hskip-0.50003pt\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974ptr}+A_{2}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Bounding Iteration Error Differences.

As a first small result, we consider an adaptation of
[
21
, Lemma 5]
to our stochastic and approximate setting.
This result implicitly shows that we can bound the iteration error difference
f(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn+\hskip-0.40974pt1}\hskip-0.50003pt)-f(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt)
from above for all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
with high probability, where the additional error terms precisely quantify the effect of domain approximation and objective approximation.

Lemma 4

Let
\lambda\in(\hskip-0.24994pt0,1]
be a step -size and
\varepsilon\in[\hskip 0.24994pt0,1]
as in Assumption
2.1
.
Then, it holds

f(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt+\lambda\hskip 0.24994pt(\mathbf{S}_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}\hskip-0.50003pt-\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt))-f(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt)\,\leq\,-\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{G}_{\hskip-0.12291ptn}\lambda+(d_{H}(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn+\hskip-0.40974pt1},D)L+2\hskip 0.50003pt\Delta\hskip 0.50003pt\lVert\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{F}_{\hskip-0.61455ptn+\hskip-0.40974pt1}-f\hskip 0.24994pt\rVert_{\infty})\lambda+\frac{C}{2}\lambda^{\hskip-0.40974pt2}

(17)

on
N
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
In particular, (
17
) holds with probability at least
1-\varepsilon
.

Proof
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
be fixed and
\omega\in N
be a sample point.
For simplicity, we use the notation (
12
) for the rest of the proof.
By definition of the uniform curvature constant
C
and since we know that
x_{n},s_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}\in E
we have

f(x_{n}+\lambda(s_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}-x_{n}))\,\leq\,f(x_{n})+\lambda\hskip 0.50003pt\langle\hskip 0.50003pts_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}-x_{n}\,|\,\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x_{n})\hskip 0.50003pt\rangle+\frac{C}{2}\lambda^{\hskip-0.40974pt2}\hskip 1.00006pt.

(18)

Using the Cauchy -Schwarz inequality we can see that

\displaystyle\lvert\langle\hskip 0.50003pts-x_{n}\,|\,(\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}-\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf\hskip 0.50003pt)(x_{n})\hskip 0.50003pt\rangle\rvert\,\leq\,\lVert\hskip 0.50003pts-x_{n}\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert\,\lVert\hskip 0.50003pt(\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}-\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf\hskip 0.50003pt)(x_{n})\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert\,\leq\,\Delta\hskip 0.50003pt\lVert\hskip 0.50003ptf_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}-f\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert_{\infty}\hskip 1.00006pt,

(19)

for all
s\in E
such that we can bound the inner product in (
18
) from above as

\displaystyle\langle\hskip 0.50003pts_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}-x_{n}\,|\,\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x_{n})\hskip 0.50003pt\rangle\,\leq\,\langle\hskip 0.50003pts_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}-x_{n}\,|\,\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}(x_{n})\hskip 0.50003pt\rangle+\Delta\hskip 0.50003pt\lVert f_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}-f\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert_{\infty}\hskip 1.00006pt.

(20)

Furthermore, by definition of the subsolution process
\mathbf{S}
and using (
19
), we can see that

\displaystyle\langle\hskip 0.50003pts_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}-x_{n}\,|\,\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}(x_{n})\hskip 0.50003pt\rangle

\displaystyle=\,\min\hskip 1.00006pt\hskip-0.50003pt\{\langle\hskip 0.50003pts-x_{n}\,|\,\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}(x_{n})\hskip 0.50003pt\rangle\,\colon s\in D_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}\}

(21)

\displaystyle\leq\,\min\hskip 1.00006pt\{\langle\hskip 0.50003pts-x_{n}\,|\,\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x_{n})\hskip 0.50003pt\rangle\,\colon s\in D_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}\}+\Delta\hskip 0.50003pt\lVert\hskip 0.50003ptf_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}-f\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert_{\infty}\hskip 1.00006pt,

such that we can focus on reformulating the last minimization term.
Revisiting representation (
4
) of the Hausdorff distance, we know that
D\subseteq D_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}\hskip-1.00006pt+\mathbb{B}(0,d_{H}(D_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1},D))
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, such that

\displaystyle-\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{G}_{\hskip-0.12291ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)

\displaystyle=\,\min\hskip 1.00006pt\{\langle\hskip 0.50003pts-x_{n}\,|\,\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x_{n})\hskip 0.50003pt\rangle\,\colon s\in D\}

(22)

\displaystyle\geq\,\min\hskip 1.00006pt\{\langle\hskip 0.50003pts-x_{n}\,|\,\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x_{n})\hskip 0.50003pt\rangle\,\colon s\in D_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}\hskip-1.00006pt+\mathbb{B}(0,d_{H}(D_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1},D))\}

\displaystyle=\,\min\hskip 1.00006pt\{\langle\hskip 0.50003pts-x_{n}\,|\,\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x_{n})\hskip 0.50003pt\rangle\,\colon s\in D_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}\}-d_{H}(D_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1},D)\hskip 0.50003pt\lVert\hskip 0.50003pt\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x_{n})\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert\hskip 1.00006pt.

Combining (
18
) and (
20
) -(
22
) we then obtain

f(x_{n}+\lambda(s_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}-x_{n}))\hskip 0.50003pt-f(x_{n})\,\leq\,-\hskip 1.00006pt\mathbf{G}_{\hskip-0.12291ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)\lambda+\mathbf{A}_{\hskip 0.12291ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)\lambda+\frac{C}{2}\lambda^{\hskip-0.81949pt2}\hskip 1.00006pt,

where

\mathbf{A}_{\hskip 0.12291ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)\,=\,d_{H}(D_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1},D)L+2\hskip 0.50003pt\Delta\hskip 0.50003pt\lVert\hskip 0.50003ptf_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}-f\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert_{\infty}\,\geq\,d_{H}(D_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1},D)\hskip 0.50003pt\lVert\hskip 0.50003pt\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x_{n})\hskip 0.50003pt\|+2\hskip 0.50003pt\Delta\hskip 0.50003pt\lVert\hskip 0.50003ptf_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}-f\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert_{\infty}\hskip 1.00006pt.

The second claim follows since
\mathbb{P}[\hskip-0.24994ptN\hskip 0.24994pt]\geq 1-\varepsilon
.
∎

Remark 5

If the objective function
f
is known, then Lemma
3.2
can be adapted to the setting of the previous remarks.
By omitting the quantities that depend on the objective approximation process
\mathbf{F}
and replacing the quantities dealt with in the remarks, we obtain that

f(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt+\lambda(\mathbf{S}_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}\hskip-0.50003pt-\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt))-f(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt)\leq-\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{G}_{\hskip-0.12291ptn}\lambda+d_{H}(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{D}_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1},D)\hskip 0.24994pt\mathbf{L}\hskip 0.24994pt\lambda+\frac{\mathbf{C}}{2}\lambda^{\hskip-0.81949pt2}

(23)

on
N_{0}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
Here, (
23
) holds with probability at least
1-\varepsilon_{0}
.

Bounding Iteration Errors from Below.

In the deterministic analysis of the Frank -Wolfe algorithm, one can use that
f(x)\geq\Popt
for all
x\in D
to canonically bound the error
f(x)-\Popt
from below by zero.
However, in our domain adaptive setting it may occur that
f(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt)<\Popt
on a whole event
A\subseteq\Omega
with
\mathbb{P}(A)>0
as it can happen that
\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\in E\setminus D
on
A
and since
\Popt
is not necessarily a global minimum on the entire uniform extension domain.
To tackle this problem we have to also find a lower bound on the iteration error
f(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt)-\Popt
that holds with an at least high probability.
To obtain this lower bound we strongly rely on the convexity of the approximation domains. Recall that
h
is the metric induced by the inner product on
H
and that
h(x,D)
describes the distance of a point
x\in H
to the problem domain
D
.

Lemma 5

Let
K\subseteq H
be nonempty and convex.
Then, the map
K\to\,\mathbb{R},\,x\,\mapsto\,h(x,D)
is convex.

Lemma 6

Let
\varepsilon\in[\hskip 0.24994pt0,1]
be as in Assumption
2.1
.
Then, it holds that

f(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt)-\Popt\,\geq\,-h(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.19925ptn},D)L

(24)

on
N
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
In particular, (
24
) holds with probability at least
1-\varepsilon
.

Proof
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
\omega\in N
be a sample point.
Since
D
is compact, we can find
y\in D
satisfying

h(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega),D)\,=\,h(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega),y)\,=\,\lVert\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)-y\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert\hskip 1.00006pt.

Since both
\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega),y\in E
and
E
is nonempty and convex, we can use Lemma
3.1
to obtain

\lvert\hskip 0.24994ptf(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega))-f(y)\hskip 0.24994pt\rvert\,\leq\,L\hskip 0.50003pt\lVert\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)-y\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert\,=\,h(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega),D)L\hskip 1.00006pt,

such that we find

\displaystyle f(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega))-\Popt\,\geq\,f(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.19925ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega))-f(y)\,\geq\,-\hskip 0.24994pth(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.19925ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega),D)L\hskip 1.00006pt,

where we used that
f(y)\geq\Popt
since
y\in D
.
∎

3.3.  Results on Convergence of Algorithm
2.2

We can now state the first convergence result of Algorithm
2.2
, which is of pure asymptotic nature.
Later in this section, we will even derive results on the convergence rate of Algorithm
2.2
under some additional assumptions.

Theorem 1  –
(Asymptotic Convergence of Algorithm
2.2
)

Let
\mathbf{X}
be the iterate process generated by running Algorithm
2.2
with starting random variable
\mathbf{X}_{0}
, domain approximation process
\mathbf{D}
and objective approximation process
\mathbf{F}
.
Let
\beta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1},\beta_{\hskip 0.2048pt2},\varepsilon\in[\hskip 0.24994pt0,1]
and
\eta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1},\eta_{\hskip 0.40974pt2}\geq 0
as in Assumptions
2.1
,
2.1
, and
2.1
.
Then, it holds

\mathbb{P}\hskip-1.99997pt\left[-\eta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}\hskip-0.50003ptL\,\leq\,\liminf_{n\hskip 0.40974pt\to\hskip 0.40974pt\infty}\,f(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt)-\Popt\,\leq\,\limsup_{n\hskip 0.39864pt\to\hskip 0.39864pt\infty}\,f(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.19925ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt)-\Popt\,\leq\,\eta_{\hskip 0.19925pt1}\hskip-1.00006ptL+2\hskip 0.50003pt\eta_{\hskip 0.39864pt2}\Delta\right]\,\geq\,1-(\beta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}+\beta_{\hskip 0.2048pt2}+\varepsilon)\hskip 1.00006pt.

Proof
.

First, denote the event

M\,\coloneqq\,\left\{\limsup_{n\hskip 0.40974pt\to\hskip 0.40974pt\infty}\hskip 1.49994ptd_{H}(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip 0.50003pt,D)\,\leq\,\eta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}\right\}\,\cap\,\left\{\limsup_{n\hskip 0.40974pt\to\hskip 0.40974pt\infty}\hskip 1.99997pt\lVert\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{F}_{\hskip-0.61455ptn}-f\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert\,\leq\,\eta_{\hskip 0.40974pt2}\right\}

for the sample points corresponding to Assumption
2.1
and Assumption
2.1
and let
\omega\in M\cap N
be fixed.
For simplicity, we use the notation (
12
) for the rest of the proof.
We denote
e_{n}=f(x_{n})-\Popt
for the approximation error at iteration
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
By Lemma
3.2
, we know that

e_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}\,\leq\,e_{n}-\mathbf{G}_{\hskip-0.12291ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}+(d_{H}(D_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1},D)L+2\hskip 0.50003pt\Delta\lVert\hskip 0.50003pt\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}-\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert_{\infty})\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}+\frac{C}{2}\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.81949pt2}

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, such that using Lemma
3.1
this implies

e_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}\,\leq\,(1-\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt)\hskip 0.50003pte_{n}+(d_{H}(D_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1},D)L+2\hskip 0.50003pt\Delta\lVert\hskip 0.50003pt\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}-\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert_{\infty})\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}+\frac{C}{2}\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.81949pt2}\hskip 1.00006pt.

(25)

By choice of
\omega
, we obtain that for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
there exists some
m\in\mathbb{N}
satisfying

d_{H}(D_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1},D)\,\leq\,\eta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}+\frac{1}{k}\quad\text{ and }\quad\lVert\hskip 0.50003pt\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}-\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert_{\infty}\,\leq\,\eta_{\hskip 0.40974pt2}+\frac{1}{k}

(26)

for all
n\geq m
.
Hence, combining (
25
) and (
26
) we have

e_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}\,\leq\,(1-\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt)\hskip 0.50003pte_{n}+\left(\hskip-1.00006pt\left(\hskip-1.00006pt\eta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}+\frac{1}{k}\right)\hskip-1.49994pt\hskip-0.50003ptL+2\hskip 0.50003pt\Delta\hskip-1.49994pt\left(\hskip-1.00006pt\eta_{\hskip 0.40974pt2}+\frac{1}{k}\right)\hskip-1.00006pt\right)\hskip-1.99997pt\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}+\frac{C}{2}\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.81949pt2}

(27)

for all
n\geq m
, such that we can apply Proposition
3.2
to (
27
) to obtain that

e_{n}\,\leq\,(m+3)\max\left\{\hskip-1.00006pt\lvert\hskip 0.50003pte_{m}\hskip 0.50003pt\rvert,\frac{C}{2}\right\}\hskip-0.50003pt\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}+\left(\hskip-1.00006pt\eta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}+\frac{1}{k}\right)\hskip-1.99997ptL+2\hskip 0.50003pt\Delta\hskip-1.99997pt\left(\eta_{\hskip 0.40974pt2}+\frac{1}{k}\right)

for all
n\geq m+1
.
Thus, overall we have

\displaystyle\limsup_{n\hskip 0.40974pt\to\hskip 0.40974pt\infty}\,e_{n}\,\leq\,\left(\hskip-1.00006pt\eta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}+\frac{1}{k}\right)\hskip-1.99997ptL+2\hskip 0.50003pt\Delta\hskip-1.99997pt\left(\hskip-1.00006pt\eta_{\hskip 0.40974pt2}+\frac{1}{k}\right)\,=\,\eta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}\hskip-0.50003ptL+2\hskip 0.50003pt\Delta\hskip 0.50003pt\eta_{\hskip 0.40974pt2}+\frac{1}{k}(L+2\Delta)

(28)

for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Since (
28
) holds for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we eventually obtain that the limes superior of
e_{n}
as
n\to\infty
is bounded above by
\eta_{\hskip 0.40974pt1}\hskip-0.50003ptL+2\hskip 0.50003pt\Delta\hskip 0.50003pt\eta_{\hskip 0.40974pt2}
.
Analogously to (
25
), since
E
is nonempty and convex, by Lemma
3.2
we know that

\displaystyle h(x_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1},D)\,\leq\,(1-\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt)\hskip 0.50003pth(x_{n},D)+\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip 0.50003pth(s_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1},D)\,\leq\,(1-\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt)\hskip 0.50003pth(x_{n},D)+\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip 0.50003ptd_{H}(D_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1},D)\hskip 1.00006pt,

where in the last inequality we used that
s_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}\in D_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
Therefore, similar to (
27
), we have

h(x_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1},D)\,\leq\,(1-\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip 0.50003pt)\hskip 0.50003pth(x_{n},D)+\left(\hskip-1.00006pt\eta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}+\frac{1}{k}\right)\hskip-1.99997pt\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}

for all
n\geq m
, such that by applying Proposition
3.2
and considering the limes superior this yields

\limsup_{n\hskip 0.40974pt\to\hskip 0.40974pt\infty}\,h(x_{n},D)\,\leq\,\eta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}+\frac{1}{k}

(29)

for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
As before, since (
29
) holds for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we obtain that the limes superior of
h(x_{n},D)
as
n\to\infty
is bounded above by
\eta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}
.
Hence, combining (
29
) and Lemma
3.2
, we find

\liminf_{n\hskip 0.40974pt\to\hskip 0.40974pt\infty}\,e_{n}\,\geq\,\liminf_{n\hskip 0.40974pt\to\hskip 0.40974pt\infty}\,-h(x_{n},D)L\,=\,-\limsup_{n\hskip 0.40974pt\to\hskip 0.40974pt\infty}\,h(x_{n},D)L\,\geq\,-\hskip 0.50003pt\eta_{\hskip 0.40974pt1}L\hskip 1.00006pt,

such that the claim follows since
\mathbb{P}[M\cap N\hskip 0.50003pt]\geq 1-(\beta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}+\beta_{\hskip 0.2048pt2}+\varepsilon)
.
∎

Remark 6

If the objective function
f
is known, then Theorem
3.3
can be adapted to the setting of the previous remarks.
By omitting the quantities that depend on the domain approximation process
\mathbf{F}
and replacing the quantities dealt with in the remarks, we obtain that

\mathbb{P}\hskip-1.99997pt\left[\hskip 1.00006pt\limsup_{n\hskip 0.40974pt\to\hskip 0.40974pt\infty}\,\,\lvert\hskip 0.50003ptf(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt)-\Popt\rvert\,\leq\,\eta_{\hskip 0.39864pt1}\hskip-0.50003pt\mathbf{L}\hskip 0.50003pt\right]\,\geq\,1-(\beta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}+\varepsilon_{0})\hskip 1.00006pt.

Convergence Rates.

While Theorem
3.3
provides an asymptotic convergence result for the approximation error of
f(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt)-\Popt
as
n\to\infty
, there remains a lack of control on the actual convergence rate.
To obtain results involving the convergence rate or to give explicit approximation error bounds depending on the number of iterations in Algorithm
2.2
(or its deterministic counterpart Algorithm
1
), we have to impose stronger assumptions on the convergence rate of the domain approximation process
\mathbf{D}
and the objective approximation process
\mathbf{F}
.
More precisely, we consider a more restrictive version of Assumption
2.1
and Assumption
2.1
to obtain this control.

Assumption 4

The domain approximation process
\mathbf{D}
and objective approximation process
\mathbf{F}
are such that there exist constants
\beta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1},\beta_{\hskip 0.2048pt2}\in[\hskip 0.24994pt0,1],r_{1},r_{2}\in(0,1],\eta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1},\eta_{\hskip 0.40974pt2}\geq 0
and
c_{1},c_{\hskip 0.2048pt2}>0
satisfying

\mathbb{P}[\hskip 0.50003ptd_{H}(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{D}_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1},D)\,\leq\,c_{1}\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974ptr_{1}}+\eta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}\text{ for all }n\in\mathbb{N}\hskip 0.50003pt]\,\geq\,1-\beta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}

and

\mathbb{P}[\hskip 0.50003pt\lVert\hskip 0.50003pt\nabla\mathbf{F}_{\hskip-0.61455ptn+\hskip-0.40974pt1}-\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf\hskip 0.50003pt\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert_{\infty}\,\leq\,c_{\hskip 0.2048pt2}\hskip 0.24994pt\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974ptr_{2}}+\eta_{\hskip 0.40974pt2}\text{ for all }\in\mathbb{N}\hskip 0.50003pt]\,\geq\,1-\beta_{\hskip 0.2048pt2}\hskip 1.00006pt.

Note that Assumption
3.3
indeed implies both Assumption
2.1
and Assumption
2.1
since we have
