\limsup\limits_{n\hskip 0.40974pt\to\hskip 0.40974pt\infty}\,d_{H}(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{D}_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1},D)\,\leq\,\limsup\limits_{n\hskip 0.40974pt\to\hskip 0.40974pt\infty}\,c_{1}\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974ptr_{1}}+\eta_{\hskip 0.40974pt1}\,=\,\eta_{\hskip 0.40974pt1}

with probability at least
1-\beta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}
and

\limsup\limits_{n\hskip 0.40974pt\to\hskip 0.40974pt\infty}\,\lVert\hskip 0.50003pt\nabla\mathbf{F}_{\hskip-0.61455ptn+\hskip-0.40974pt1}-\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert_{\infty}\,\leq\,\limsup\limits_{n\hskip 0.40974pt\to\hskip 0.40974pt\infty}\hskip 1.99997ptc_{\hskip 0.2048pt2}\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974ptr_{2}}+\eta_{\hskip 0.40974pt2}\,=\,\eta_{\hskip 0.40974pt2}

with probability at least
1-\beta_{\hskip 0.2048pt2}
.
Therefore, as mentioned before, the constants
\beta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}
and
\beta_{\hskip 0.2048pt2}
represent approximation failure tolerances and
\eta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}
and
\eta_{\hskip 0.40974pt2}
represent the approximation qualities which can be obtained in the limit of the approximation processes
\mathbf{D}
and
\mathbf{F}
with a probability of at least
1-\beta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}
and
1-\beta_{\hskip 0.2048pt2}
, respectively.
The additional constants
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
are the corresponding convergence rates of the approximation processes (for example,
r_{1}=r_{2}=0.5
).
The scaling parameters
c_{1}
and
c_{\hskip 0.2048pt2}
can be used to influence the probabilities
1-\beta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}
and
1-\beta_{\hskip 0.2048pt2}
, for instance via concentration inequalities
[
11
]
.
Using this new assumption, we can now improve Theorem
3.3
.
However, we first introduce an event
M\subseteq\Omega
of sample points satisfying Assumption
3.3
.
To this end, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
we define the event

M_{n}\,\coloneqq\,\left\{\hskip-0.35004ptd_{H}(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn+\hskip-0.40974pt1},D)\,\leq\,c_{1}\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974ptr_{1}}+\eta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}\right\}\,\cap\,\left\{\lVert\hskip 0.50003pt\nabla\mathbf{F}_{\hskip-0.61455ptn+\hskip-0.40974pt1}-\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf\hskip 0.50003pt\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert_{\infty}\,\leq\,c_{\hskip 0.2048pt2}\hskip 0.24994pt\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974ptr_{2}}+\eta_{\hskip 0.40974pt2}\right\}

and set

M\,=\,\bigcap\hskip 1.99997pt\{M_{n}\,\colon n\in\mathbb{N}\}\hskip 1.00006pt.

In this way we have
\mathbb{P}[\hskip-0.24994ptM\hskip 0.24994pt]\geq 1-(\beta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}+\beta_{\hskip 0.2048pt2})
.

Theorem 2  –
(Convergence Rate for Algorithm 2)

Let
\mathbf{X}
be the iterate process generated by running Algorithm
2.2
with starting random variable
\mathbf{X}_{0}
, domain approximation process
\mathbf{D}
and objective approximation process
\mathbf{F}
.
Let
\varepsilon,\beta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1},\beta_{\hskip 0.2048pt2}\in[\hskip 0.24994pt0,1]
,
r_{1},r_{2}\in(0,1]
,
\eta_{\hskip 0.40974pt1},\eta_{\hskip 0.40974pt2}\geq 0
and
c_{1},c_{\hskip 0.2048pt2}>0
as in Assumption
2.1
and Assumption
3.3
.
Then, there exist constants
A,B\geq 0
, such that with
r=\min\{r_{1},r_{2}\}
it holds

\mathbb{P}\hskip-1.49994pt\left[\hskip 0.50003pt-A\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974ptr_{1}}-\eta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}\hskip-0.50003ptL\,\leq\,f(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt)-\Popt\leq B\lambda_{\hskip 0.19925ptn}^{\hskip-0.39864ptr}+\eta_{\hskip 0.19925pt1}\hskip-0.50003ptL+2\hskip 1.00006pt\eta_{\hskip 0.39864pt2}\hskip 0.24994pt\Delta\text{ for all }n\in\mathbb{N}\hskip 0.50003pt\right]\,\geq\,1-\left(\beta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}+\beta_{\hskip 0.2048pt2}+\varepsilon\right)\hskip 0.74997pt.

Proof
.

This proof is a natural extension of the proof of Theorem
3.3
based on
[
21
, Theorem 1]
.
Let
\omega\in M\cap N
be a fixed sample point corresponding to Assumption
2.1
and Assumption
3.3
.
For simplicity, we use the notation (
12
) for the rest of the proof.
We denote
e_{n}=f(x_{n})-\Popt
for the approximation error at iteration
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
Analogously to the proof of Theorem
3.3
, we obtain that

e_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}\,\leq\,(1-\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt)\hskip 0.50003pte_{n}+(d_{H}(D_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1},D)L+2\hskip 0.50003pt\Delta\lVert\hskip 0.50003pt\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf_{n+\hskip 0.40974pt1}-\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert_{\infty})\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}+\frac{C}{2}\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.81949pt2}

(30)

and by choice of the sample point
\omega
we have

d_{H}(\hskip-0.50003ptD_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1},D)\,\leq\,c_{1}\hskip-0.50003pt\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974ptr_{1}}+\eta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}\quad\text{ and }\quad\lVert\hskip 0.50003pt\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}-\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert_{\infty}\,\leq\,c_{\hskip 0.2048pt2}\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974ptr_{2}}+\eta_{\hskip 0.40974pt2}

(31)

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
Hence, combining (
30
) and (
31
) we have that

e_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}\,\leq\,(1-\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt)\hskip 0.50003pte_{n}+c_{1}\hskip-0.74997ptL\hskip 0.50003pt\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.81949pt1+\hskip 0.40974ptr_{1}}+2\hskip 0.50003ptc_{\hskip 0.2048pt2}\hskip 0.24994pt\Delta\hskip 0.50003pt\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.81949pt1+r_{2}}+(\eta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}\hskip-0.50003ptL+2\hskip 0.50003pt\eta_{\hskip 0.40974pt2}\Delta)\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}+\frac{C}{2}\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.81949pt2}

(32)

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, such that we can apply Proposition
3.2
to (
32
) to obtain that

e_{n}\,\leq\,B\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974pt\min\{r_{1},r_{2}\hskip-0.40974pt\}}+\eta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}\hskip-0.50003ptL+2\hskip 0.50003pt\eta_{\hskip 0.40974pt2}\Delta

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, where
B=2\hskip 0.50003ptc_{1}\hskip-0.50003ptL+4\hskip 0.50003ptc_{\hskip 0.2048pt2}\Delta+C
.
To bound
e_{n}
from below for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we can proceed as in the proof of Theorem
3.3
to obtain that

h(x_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1},D)\,\leq\,(1-\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt)\hskip 0.50003pth(x_{n},D)+c_{1}\hskip-0.50003pt\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.81949pt1+\hskip 0.40974ptr_{1}}+\eta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}\hskip-0.50003pt\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, such that by applying Proposition
3.2
this yields

h(x_{n},D)\,\leq\,2\hskip 0.50003ptc_{1}\hskip-0.50003pt\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974ptr_{1}}+\eta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}

(33)

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Using Lemma
3.2
and (
33
) we then obtain

e_{n}\,\geq\,-\hskip 0.50003pth(x_{n},D)L\,\geq\,-A\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974ptr_{1}}-\eta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}\hskip-0.50003ptL

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, where
A=2\hskip 0.50003ptc_{1}\hskip-0.50003ptL
.
The claim now follows since
\mathbb{P}[M\cap N]\geq 1-(\beta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}+\beta_{\hskip 0.2048pt2}+\varepsilon)
.
∎

Remark 7

If the objective function
f
is known, then Theorem
3.3
can be adapted to the setting of the previous remarks.
By omitting the quantities that depend on the objective approximation process
\mathbf{F}
and replacing the quantities dealt with in the remarks, we obtain that

\mathbb{P}\hskip-1.49994pt\left[\hskip 1.00006pt\lvert\hskip 0.50003ptf(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt)-\Popt\rvert\,\leq\,\mathbf{A}\lambda_{\hskip 0.19925ptn}^{\hskip-0.39864ptr_{1}}+\eta_{\hskip 0.19925pt1}\hskip-0.50003pt\mathbf{L}\text{ for all }n\in\mathbb{N}\hskip 1.00006pt\right]\,\geq\,1-(\beta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}+\varepsilon_{0})\hskip 1.00006pt,

where
\mathbf{A}\,=\,2\hskip 0.50003ptc_{1}\hskip-0.50003pt\mathbf{L}+\mathbf{C}\geq 0
now is a random variable.
Here, the resulting convergence rate is governed solely by the speed at which the domain approximation process
\mathbf{D}
approaches the problem domain
D
.

3.4.  Comments on the Assumptions

We now briefly comment on the assumptions on the domain approximation process
\mathbf{D}
of this section.
It is immediately clear that if the problem domain
D
is given as a closed ball in
H
, that is,
D=\mathbb{B}(y,\rho)
for some center point
y\in H
and radius
\rho\geq 0
, then a natural way to obtain domain approximations for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
is to consider the sets
D_{n}=\mathbb{B}(y_{n},\rho_{n}\hskip-0.50003pt)
, where the sequences
(y_{n}\hskip-0.50003pt)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq H
and
(\rho_{n}\hskip-0.50003pt)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{+}
approximate
y
and
\rho
, respectively.
However, this example is yet quite restrictive and can easily be extended to a larger collection of problem domains.

Domain Approximations via Common Sublevel Sets.

To generate a sequence of nonempty, compact, and convex domain approximations, our goal is to mimic the structure of the closed balls in
H
, centered around a fixed point, as common sublevel set of functions with suitable properties.

Definition 4  –
(Carathéodory Function)

Let
(S,\Sigma_{S}\hskip-0.50003pt)
be a measurable space and
X
be a topological space.
A function
g\colon S\times X\to\mathbb{R}
is called
Carathéodory function
if both

•

the function
s\mapsto g(s,x)
is
\Sigma_{S}
-measurable for all
x\in X
,

•

the function
x\mapsto g(s,x)
is continuous for all
s\in S
.

More specifically, fixing some
A\subseteq H
closed, we want to consider Carathéodory functions
g\colon A\times A\to\mathbb{R}
additionally satisfying

g(y,y)\,\leq\,0\qquad\text{and}\qquad t\,\mapsto\,g(y,t)\text{ is convex}

(34)

for all
y\in A
.
Taking
n\in\mathbb{N}
such Carathéodory functions
g_{\hskip 0.12291pt1},\ldots,g_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}
and assuming that further
x\mapsto g_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}\hskip-0.50003pt(y,x)
is coercive, that is,

g_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}\hskip-0.50003pt(y,x)\,\to\,\infty\quad\text{ as }\quad\lVert\hskip 0.50003ptx\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert\,\to\,\infty\hskip 1.00006pt,

(35)

fixing a center point
y\in A
and a radius
\rho\geq 0
we can define the common sublevel set

\mathbb{S}(y,\rho)\,\coloneqq\,\{x\in A\,\colon g_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}\hskip-0.50003pt(y,x)\leq\rho,\ldots,g_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(y,x)\leq\rho\}\,\subseteq\,H\hskip 1.00006pt.

By construction, we now have that the common sublevel set
\mathbb{S}(y,\rho)
is nonempty, compact, and convex and can be used as a baseline for domain approximation sequences as described above.
A detailed proof of the next result can be found in Appendix
B.2
.

Lemma 7

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and fix Carathéodory functions
g_{\hskip 0.2048pt1},\ldots,g_{n}
satisfying (
34
) and (
35
).
Let
y\in H
and
\rho\geq 0
.
Then, the corresponding common sublevel set
\mathbb{S}(y,\rho)
is nonempty, compact, and convex.
Furthermore, for
\Sigma
-measurable functions
\mathbf{Y}\colon\Omega\to H
and
\mathbf{P}\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
the map

\Omega\to\mathscr{D},\;\omega\,\mapsto\,\mathbb{S}(\mathbf{Y}(\omega),\mathbf{P}(\omega))

(36)

is well-defined and
\Sigma
-measurable.

Similar to above, Lemma
3.4
now yields an easy way to construct a domain approximation process if the problem domain
D
is given as the common sublevel set of certain Carathéodory functions, that is,
D=\mathbb{S}(y,\rho)
for some center point
y\in H
and radius
\rho\geq 0
.
Indeed, if we have access to a stochastic processes
\mathbf{Y}
and
\mathbf{P}
approximating
y
and
\rho
, respectively, in some probabilistic sense, then the process
\mathbf{D}
defined by
\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}=\mathbb{S}(\mathbf{Y}_{\hskip-0.81949ptn},\mathbf{P}_{\hskip-0.81949ptn})
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
forms a valid domain approximation process.
Note that the convergence behavior of this process
\mathbf{D}
is now directly influenced by the convergence behavior of the processes
\mathbf{Y}
and
\mathbf{P}
, but also depended on the explicit Carathéodory functions used.
Therefore, in the general case, it is not possible to derive good upper bounds on the Hausdorff distance without further knowledge of the Carathéodory functions.
Hence, Lemma
3.4
only yields a way to determine general feasible domain approximations but it remains to check for the approximation quality in every case separately, typically by exploiting additional structure such as Lipschitz bounds in the first argument or explicit control of the level sets.

Remark 8

The easiest example of a Carathéodory function satisfying (
34
) and (
35
) is the induced metric of
any
norm on
H
.
In contrast to the previous paragraph, taking
n\in\mathbb{N}
norms on
H
and considering their induced metrics
g_{i}
for
i\in[n]
, we can find explicit upper bounds on the Hausdorff distance of the common sublevel sets.
Taking two center points
y_{1},y_{2}\in H
and radii
\rho_{1},\rho_{\hskip 0.2048pt2}\geq 0
we find that

d_{H}(\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbb{S}(y_{1},\rho_{1}\hskip-0.50003pt),\mathbb{S}(y_{2},\rho_{2}))\,\leq\,\frac{M}{m}\hskip 0.50003pt\lVert\hskip 0.50003pty_{1}-y_{2}\rVert+\frac{1}{m}\hskip 0.50003pt\lvert\hskip 0.50003pt\rho_{1}-\rho_{\hskip 0.2048pt2}\rvert\hskip 1.00006pt,

where
0<m<M
are such that
m\hskip 0.50003pt\lVert s-x\rVert\leq g_{i}\hskip 0.24994pt(s,x)\leq M\lVert s-x\rVert
for all
s,x\in H
and
i\in[n]
.
A proof of this remark can be found in Appendix
B.2
.

4.  Convergence Acceleration

A natural question arising in the Frank -Wolfe algorithm framework is whether additional assumptions on the objective function
f
or on the problem domain
D
can provide accelerated convergence results.
Similar to some deterministic settings with open-loop step-sizes
[
43
,
44
]
or line -search step-sizes
[
18
,
22
]
, this question can be answered positively and in a structurally similar manner.
One key assumption in the following will be that the objective function
f
is
known
, such that our setting is slightly less general than in the previous section.
In particular, we will find ourselves in the setting of the previous remarks, where we replace
E
by
\mathbf{E}
,
N
by
N_{0}
,
\varepsilon
by
\varepsilon_{0}
,
C
by
\mathbf{C}
, and
L
by
\mathbf{L}
.
First, in the case of outer domain approximations, we will derive only improved upper convergence rates, before turning to settings with inner domain approximations and strong convexity, where genuinely accelerated rates can be obtained.
Lastly, we comment on how such outer and inner domain approximations may be constructed in practice.

4.1.  Outer Domain Approximation

A relatively simple way to obtain an acceleration of Algorithm
2.2
is to assume that the domain approximation process
\mathbf{D}
converges towards the domain
D
from the outside.

Assumption 5

The domain approximation process
\mathbf{D}
is such that there exists a constant
\delta\in[\hskip 0.24994pt0,1]
satisfying

\mathbb{P}\hskip-1.99997pt\left[D\,\subseteq\,\bigcap\hskip 1.99997pt\{\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\,\colon n\in\mathbb{N}\hskip 0.24994pt\}\right]\,\geq\,1-\delta\hskip 1.00006pt.

First, similar to the event
N_{0}^{+}
in Remark
2.1
, we can see that the set

\left\{\hskip-1.00006ptD\subseteq\bigcap\hskip 1.99997pt\{\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\,\colon n\in\mathbb{N}\hskip 0.24994pt\}\hskip-1.00006pt\right\}\,=\,\bigcap\hskip 1.99997pt\{\{\hskip-0.50003ptD\subseteq\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt\}\,\colon n\in\mathbb{N}\hskip 0.50003pt\}

(37)

is
\Sigma
-measurable, such that Assumption
4.1
is well-posed.
In other words, Assumption
4.1
tells us that for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
we can guarantee that
D\subseteq\mathbf{D}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}
with high probability (for example,
1-\delta=0.95
).
The reason why this can help with accelerated convergence to the optimal value
\Popt
from above is due to the fact that we can omit the Hausdorff distance term
d_{H}(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn+\hskip-0.40974pt1},D)\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{L}\hskip 0.50003pt\lambda
in (
17
), such that it does not matter whether we approximate the problem domain
D
fast or at all.
However, convergence to the optimal value
\Popt
from below is in general not improved by Assumption
4.1
.
In fact, for many cases it will even be of negative effect since in practice outer domain approximations potentially slow down Hausdorff convergence and inflate the underlying curvature bounds.

Theorem 3  –
(Accelerated Upper Convergence for Algorithm 2)

Let
\mathbf{X}
be the iterate process generated by running Algorithm
2.2
with starting random variable
\mathbf{X}_{0}
and domain approximation process
\mathbf{D}
.
Let
\beta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1},\varepsilon_{0},\delta\in[\hskip 0.24994pt0,1],r_{1}\in(\hskip-0.24994pt0,1]
,
\eta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}\geq 0
and
c_{1}>0
be as in Remark
2.2
, Assumption
3.3
and Assumption
4.1
.
Then, there exists a random variable
\mathbf{A}\geq 0
, such that

\mathbb{P}\hskip-1.49994pt\left[\hskip 0.50003pt-\mathbf{A}\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974ptr_{1}}-\eta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}\hskip-0.50003pt\mathbf{L}\,\leq\,f(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt)-\Popt\,\leq\,\mathbf{C}\lambda_{\hskip 0.19925ptn}\,\text{ for all }\,n\in\mathbb{N}\hskip 1.00006pt\right]\,\geq\,1-(\beta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}+\varepsilon_{0}+\delta)\hskip 1.00006pt.

Proof.
.

This proof is a simple adaptation of the proofs of Lemma
3.2
and Theorem
3.3
, restricted to the setting of a known objective function
f
.
Let

\omega\in\bigcap\left\{\hskip-0.35004ptD\subseteq\hskip 0.50003pt\bigcap\,\{\mathbf{D}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\,\colon n\in\mathbb{N}\}\right\}\,\cap\,M\,\cap\,N_{0}

(38)

be a fixed sample point corresponding to Remark
2.2
, Assumption
3.3
and Assumption
4.1
.
First, we follow the proof of Lemma
3.2
but additionally use that
D\subseteq\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn+\hskip-0.40974pt1}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)
to obtain that

-\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{G}_{\hskip-0.12291ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)\,=\,\min\hskip 1.00006pt\{\langle\hskip 0.50003pts-\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)\,|\,\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega))\hskip 0.50003pt\rangle\,\colon s\in D\}\,\geq\,\min\hskip 1.00006pt\{\langle\hskip 0.50003pts-\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)\,|\,\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega))\hskip 0.50003pt\rangle\,\colon s\in D_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}\}

instead of (
22
).
Hence, we can derive that

f(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)\hskip-0.50003pt+\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}(\mathbf{S}_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)-\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)))-f(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega))\,\leq\,-\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{G}_{\hskip-0.12291ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}+\frac{\mathbf{C}(\omega)}{2}\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.61455pt2}\hskip 0.09995pt

(39)

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
Then, inserting (
39
) instead of Lemma
3.2
in the proof of Theorem
3.3
, we have

f(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega))-\Popt\,\leq\,(1-\lambda_{\hskip 0.19925ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt)\hskip 0.50003pt(f(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.19925ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega))-\Popt)+\frac{\mathbf{C}}{2}\lambda_{\hskip 0.19925ptn}^{\hskip-0.39864pt2}

instead of (
32
), such that applying Proposition
3.2
yields

f(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega))-\Popt\,\leq\,\mathbf{C}(\omega)\lambda_{\hskip 0.19925ptn}

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
The random variable
\mathbf{A}
now can be chosen as in Theorem
3.3
but replacing
L
with
\mathbf{L}
, that is,
\mathbf{A}=2\hskip 0.50003ptc_{1}\hskip-0.50003pt\mathbf{L}
.
The claim follows since the event in (
38
) has probability at least
1-(\beta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}+\varepsilon_{0}+\delta)
.
∎

4.2.  Inner Domain Approximation

As a counterpart to Assumption
4.1
, we can also consider the case where the problem domain
D
is not approximated from the outside, but rather from the inside.

Assumption 6

The domain approximation process
\mathbf{D}
is such that there exists a constant
\delta\in[\hskip 0.24994pt0,1]
satisfying

\mathbb{P}\hskip-1.99997pt\left[\hskip 1.00006pt\bigcup\hskip 1.99997pt\{\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\,\colon n\in\mathbb{N}\}\,\subseteq\,D\hskip 0.50003pt\right]\,\geq\,1-\delta\hskip 1.00006pt.

In this case, similar to (
37
), we can see that the set

I\,\coloneqq\,\left\{\hskip 0.50003pt\bigcup\hskip 1.99997pt\{\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\,\colon n\in\mathbb{N}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\subseteq D\right\}\,=\,\bigcap\hskip 1.99997pt\{\{\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\subseteq D\}\,\colon n\in\mathbb{N}\hskip 0.50003pt\}

is
\Sigma
-measurable, such that Assumption
4.2
is well-posed.
Assumption
4.2
guarantees that with high probability (for example,
1-\delta=0.95
) we have
\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\subseteq D
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
This property is of particular interest since it canonically bounds the approximation error
f(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt)-\Popt
from below by zero for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, as in the deterministic Frank -Wolfe setting.
Therefore, we can completely focus on finding accelerated upper bounds.
However, we already note that making sure that a domain approximation process
\mathbf{D}
satisfies Assumption
4.2
is significantly harder than for Assumption
4.1
, such that every stronger bound comes with a certain cost.
Note that since

\bigcup\hskip 1.99997pt\{\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)\,\colon n\in\mathbb{N}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\cup D\,\subseteq\,D

for all
\omega\in I
, we do not need an artificial random domain extension
\mathbf{E}
on the event
I
.
In particular, choosing
\mathbf{E}\equiv D
we can replace
\varepsilon_{0}
with
\delta
from Assumption
4.2
.
As a consequence, we can replace the random curvature constant
\mathbf{C}
with the classical Frank -Wolfe curvature constant

C_{\hskip-0.40974ptf}\,=\,\sup\hskip-1.00006pt\left\{\hskip-0.50003pt\frac{2\,}{\,\lambda^{\hskip-0.40974pt2}}(f(x+\lambda(s-x))-\hskip-0.50003ptf(x)-\lambda\langle\hskip 0.50003pts-x\,|\,\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x)\hskip 0.50003pt\rangle)\hskip 1.00006pt\colon x,s\in D,\lambda\in[\hskip 0.24994pt0,1]\right\}

as in
[
21
]
and the random Lipschitz constant
\mathbf{L}
with the Lipschitz constant

L_{\hskip-0.40974ptf}\,\coloneqq\,\max\hskip 1.00006pt\{\lVert\hskip 0.50003pt\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x)\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert\,\colon x\in D\}\hskip 1.00006pt.

Additionally, as done in many other convergence acceleration settings for the deterministic Frank -Wolfe algorithm
[
43
,
18
,
22
]
, we assume the objective function
f
and the problem domain
D
to be strongly convex.

Definition 5  –
(Strongly Convex Function)

Let
\mu>0
.
A continuously differentiable function
g\colon\hskip-1.99997pt\dom(g)\to\mathbb{R}
is called
\mu
-strongly convex on a convex set
K\subseteq\dom(g)
if it holds that

g(x)\,\geq\ g(y)+\langle\hskip 0.50003ptx-y\,|\,\nabla\hskip-0.50003ptg(y)\hskip 0.50003pt\rangle+\frac{\mu}{2}\lVert\hskip 0.50003ptx-y\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert^{2}

for all
x,y\in K
.

Definition 6  –
(Strongly Convex Set)

Let
\alpha>0
.
A set
K\subseteq H
is called
\alpha
-strongly convex if it holds

\lambda\hskip 0.50003ptx+(1-\lambda)\hskip 0.50003pty+\lambda(1-\lambda)\frac{\alpha}{2}\lVert\hskip 0.50003ptx-y\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert^{2}z\in K

for all
x,y\in K
,
\lambda\in[\hskip 0.24994pt0,1]
and
z\in H
with
\lVert\hskip 0.50003ptz\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert=1
.

Since optimal points of strongly convex functions on convex sets are unique, in the following we denote by
x^{\star}\in D
the optimal solution of problem (
P
), that is, it holds
f(x^{\star}\hskip-0.50003pt)=\Popt
.
The reason why strong convexity of both the objective function
f
and the problem domain
D
are beneficial for convergence acceleration is that instead of bounding

\langle\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{S}_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}-\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\,|\,\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt)\hskip 0.50003pt\rangle\,\leq\,-\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{G}_{\hskip-0.12291ptn}+d_{H}(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn+\hskip-0.40974pt1},D)L_{\hskip-0.40974ptf}

as done in the proof of Lemma
3.2
(adapted to this restricted setting), we can derive sharper bounds.
To this end, we consider the next two intermediate results.
The first one is composed of parts from
[
43
]
and the second one is strongly inspired by
[
18
, Lemma 1]
, but was adapted to our setting.

Lemma 8

Let
f
be
\mu
-strongly convex on
D
and
\delta\in[\hskip 0.24994pt0,1]
as in Assumption
4.2
.
Then, it holds that

\lVert\hskip 0.50003pt\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt)\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert^{2}\,\geq\,\frac{\mu}{2}(f(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt)-\Popt)

(40)

on
I
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
In particular, (
40
) holds with probability at least
1-\delta
.

Proof.
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
\omega\in I
be a sample point.
For simplicity, we use the notation (
12
) for the rest of the proof.
First, without loss of generality, we assume that
x_{n}\neq x^{\star}
since otherwise the claim trivially holds.
Since by Assumption
4.2
we know that
x_{n}\in D
using that
f
is
\mu
-strongly convex on
D
we obtain that

f(x_{n}\hskip-0.24994pt)-\Popt\,\geq\,\langle\hskip 0.50003ptx_{n}\hskip-0.50003pt-x^{\hskip-0.39864pt\star}\hskip-0.50003pt\,|\,\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x^{\hskip-0.39864pt\star}\hskip-0.50003pt)\hskip 0.50003pt\rangle+\frac{\mu}{2}\lVert\hskip 0.50003ptx_{n}\hskip-0.50003pt-x^{\hskip-0.39864pt\star}\rVert^{2}\,\geq\,\frac{\mu}{2}\lVert\hskip 0.50003ptx_{n}\hskip-0.50003pt-x^{\hskip-0.39864pt\star}\rVert^{2},

(41)

where we used that
\langle\hskip 0.50003ptx_{n}\hskip-0.50003pt-x^{\hskip-0.40974pt\star}\hskip-0.50003pt\,|\,\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x^{\star}\hskip-0.50003pt)\hskip 0.50003pt\rangle\geq 0
due to the optimality of
x^{\hskip-0.40974pt\star}
on
D
.
Furthermore, we can see that

\lVert\hskip 0.50003pt\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x_{n}\hskip-0.24994pt)\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert^{2}\hskip 0.50003pt\lVert\hskip 0.50003ptx_{n}\hskip-0.50003pt-x^{\star}\rVert^{2}\,\geq\,\langle\hskip 0.50003ptx_{n}\hskip-0.50003pt-x^{\hskip-0.40974pt\star}\hskip-0.50003pt\,|\,\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x_{n}\hskip-0.24994pt)\hskip 0.50003pt\rangle^{2}\,\geq\,(f(x_{n}\hskip-0.24994pt)-\Popt)^{2},

(42)

where we used Cauchy-Schwarz in the first and the convexity of
f
in the second inequality.
Dividing (
42
) by
\lVert\hskip 0.50003ptx_{n}\hskip-0.50003pt-x^{\star}\rVert^{2}>0
and using (
41
) then yields

\lVert\hskip 0.50003pt\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x_{n})\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert^{2}\,\geq\,\frac{(f(x_{n})-\Popt)^{2}}{\lVert\hskip 0.50003ptx_{n}\hskip-0.50003pt-x^{\star}\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert^{2}}\,\geq\,\frac{\mu}{2}(f(x_{n})-\Popt)\hskip 1.00006pt.

The second claim now follows since
\mathbb{P}[I\hskip 0.74997pt]\geq 1-\delta
.
∎

Lemma 9

Let
D
be
\alpha
-strongly convex and
\delta\in[\hskip 0.24994pt0,1]
as in Assumption
4.2
.
Then, it holds that

\langle\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{S}_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}-\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\,|\,\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt)\hskip 0.50003pt\rangle\,\leq\,-\frac{1}{2}(f(\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.24994pt)-\Popt)-\frac{\alpha}{8}\lVert\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{S}_{n+\hskip-0.39864pt1}-\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.19925ptn}\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert^{2}\hskip 0.50003pt\lVert\hskip 0.50003pt\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.19925ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt)\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert+\frac{3}{2}\hskip 0.50003ptd_{H}(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.19925ptn+\hskip-0.39864pt1},D)L_{\hskip-0.39864ptf}

(43)

on
I
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
In particular, (
43
) holds with probability at least
1-\delta
.

Proof.
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
\omega\in I
be a sample point.
For simplicity, we use the notation (
12
) for the rest of the proof.
First, since
x^{\star}\in D
, by definition of the subsolution process
\mathbf{S}
and analogously to (
22
) we find

\langle\hskip 0.50003pts_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}-\hskip 0.50003ptx_{n}\,|\,\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x_{n}\hskip-0.24994pt)\hskip 0.50003pt\rangle\,\leq\,\langle\hskip 0.50003ptx^{\star}-\hskip 0.50003ptx_{n}\,|\,\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x_{n}\hskip-0.24994pt)\hskip 0.50003pt\rangle+d_{H}(D_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1},D)\hskip 0.50003ptL_{\hskip-0.40974ptf}\,\leq\,\Popt-\hskip 0.50003ptf(x_{n}\hskip-0.24994pt)+d_{H}(D_{n+\hskip-0.39864pt1},D)\hskip 0.50003ptL_{\hskip-0.39864ptf}\hskip 1.00006pt,

(44)

where the last inequality follows from the convexity of
f
.
Note now that, without loss of generality, we can assume that
\lVert\hskip 0.50003pt\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x_{n}\hskip-0.24994pt)\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert>0
since otherwise the claim trivially holds.
Since by Assumption
4.2
we know that
x_{n}\in D
and
s_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}\in D_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}\subseteq D
, by the
\alpha
-strong convexity of
D
we have that

y\,\coloneqq\,\frac{1}{2}s_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}+\frac{1}{2}x_{n}-\frac{\alpha}{8}\frac{\lVert\hskip 0.50003pts_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}-x_{n}\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert^{2}}{\lVert\hskip 0.50003pt\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x_{n})\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert}\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x_{n})\in D\hskip 1.00006pt.

Furthermore, by definition of the subsolution process
\mathbf{S}
and analogously to (
22
), we find

\displaystyle\hskip 0.50003pt\langle\hskip 0.50003pts_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}-\hskip 0.50003ptx_{n}\,|\,\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x_{n}\hskip-0.24994pt)\hskip 0.50003pt\rangle

\displaystyle\,\leq\,\hskip 0.50003pt\langle\hskip 0.50003pty-x_{n}\,|\,\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x_{n}\hskip-0.24994pt)\hskip 0.50003pt\rangle+\hskip 0.50003ptd_{H}(D_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1},D)\hskip 0.50003ptL_{\hskip-0.40974ptf}

\displaystyle\,=\,\frac{1}{2}\langle\hskip 0.50003pts_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}-\hskip 0.50003ptx_{n}\,|\,\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x_{n}\hskip-0.24994pt)\hskip 0.50003pt\rangle-\frac{\alpha}{8}\lVert\hskip 0.50003pts_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}-x_{n}\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert^{2}\lVert\hskip 0.50003pt\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x_{n}\hskip-0.24994pt)\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert+\hskip 0.50003ptd_{H}(D_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1},D)\hskip 0.50003ptL_{\hskip-0.40974ptf}\hskip 1.00006pt,

such that plugging in (
44
) yields

\langle\hskip 0.50003pts_{n+\hskip 0.40974pt1}-\hskip 0.50003ptx_{n}\,|\,\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x_{n}\hskip-0.24994pt)\hskip 0.50003pt\rangle\,\leq\,-\frac{1}{2}(f(x_{n}\hskip-0.24994pt)-\Popt)-\frac{\alpha}{8}\lVert\hskip 0.50003pts_{n+\hskip-0.39864pt1}-x_{n}\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert^{2}\hskip 0.50003pt\lVert\hskip 0.50003pt\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x_{n})\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert+\frac{3}{2}\hskip 0.50003ptd_{H}(D_{n+\hskip-0.39864pt1},D)\hskip 0.50003ptL_{\hskip-0.39864ptf}\hskip 1.00006pt.

The second claim now follows since
\mathbb{P}[I\hskip 0.74997pt]\geq 1-\delta
.
∎

Similar to Section
3
, we also have to provide a result on the accelerated convergence of a general nonnegative sequence that is bounded from above before we can turn to Algorithm
2.2
itself.
This result is inspired by
[
43
, Lemma 3.5]
but is adapted to fit our setting and is slightly more restrictive.
A detailed proof of this next result is provided in Appendix
B.3
.

Proposition 2

Let
(T_{n}\hskip-0.50003pt)_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{+}
be a sequence.
Let
A,B_{1},B_{2},B_{3}>0
be some fixed constants,
(\hskip-0.50003ptA_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathbb{R}
be a sequence satisfying
A_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\geq A
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
r\in[\hskip 0.24994pt0,1]
and let
(\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt)_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
be our usual step-size sequence.
If there exists some
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
such that

T_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}\,\leq\,\left(\hskip-1.49994pt1-\frac{\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}}{2}\hskip-1.49994pt\right)\hskip-1.00006ptT_{n}-A_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\sqrt{T_{n}}\hskip 0.24994ptB_{1}\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}+B_{2}\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.81949pt1+\hskip 0.40974ptr}+B_{3}\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}

(45)

for all
n\geq m
, then there exists some
N\in\mathbb{N}
with
N\geq m
such that

T_{n}\,\leq\,\left(\hskip-1.49994ptT_{m}+4\hskip-1.49994pt\left(\hskip-1.00006pt\hskip-0.50003pt\frac{B_{2}}{AB_{1}}\hskip-1.00006pt\right)^{\hskip-2.04861pt2}\hskip-1.99997pt+B_{2}\hskip-1.99997pt\right)\hskip-1.99997pt\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn-\hskip-0.40974pt1}^{\hskip-0.40974pt2\hskip 0.2048ptr}+B_{3}

(46)

for all
n\geq N+1
.
In particular, if
2\hskip 0.24994ptr\leq 1
, then
N=m
.

Comparing Proposition
4.2
to
[
43
, Lemma 3.5]
, we can see that a main difference is that the sequence
(\hskip-0.50003ptA_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in our case has to be bounded from below and not from above.
Since in the case where we want to apply Proposition
4.2
we have that
A_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}
is given as a sample of
\lVert\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{S}_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}-\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\rVert
a canonical upper bound is given by
\diam(D)
.
However, bounding
\lVert\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{S}_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}-\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\rVert
or more specifically
\inf\hskip 1.00006pt\{\hskip 0.50003pt\lVert\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{S}_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}\hskip-0.50003pt-\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{X}_{n}\rVert^{2}\,\colon n\in\mathbb{N}\hskip 0.50003pt\}
from below may be hard or even impossible without further restrictions since the infimum in general may tend towards zero.
To make sure that we can guarantee that
\lVert\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{S}_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}-\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\rVert
can be bounded by a positive constant at least eventually, we assume that the optimal solution
x^{\star}
is contained in the relative interior of
D
, such that the distance to its relative boundary is positive.

Theorem 4  –
(Asymptotic Accelerated Convergence of Algorithm
2.2
)

Let
f
be
\mu
-strongly convex on
D
,
D
be
\alpha
-strongly convex and
x^{\star}\in\relinterior(D)
.
Let
\mathbf{X}
be the iterate process generated by running the Algorithm
2.2
with starting random variable
\mathbf{X}_{0}
and domain approximation process
\mathbf{D}
.
Let
\beta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1},\delta\in[\hskip 0.24994pt0,1]
,
r_{1}\in(\hskip-0.24994pt0,1]
,
\eta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}\geq 0
with
3\hskip 0.50003pt\eta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}<h(x^{\star},\relbd(D))
, and
c_{1}>0
be as in Assumption
3.3
and Assumption
4.2
.
Then, denoting

\pi\,=\,\mathbb{P}[\hskip 0.24994pt\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(\mathbf{X}_{n}\hskip-0.50003pt)=0\text{ for some }n\in\mathbb{N}\hskip 0.50003pt]\hskip 1.00006pt,

(47)

there exists some
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
B\geq 0
such that

\mathbb{P}\hskip-1.99997pt\left[\hskip 0.50003pt0\,\leq\,f(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt)-\Popt\,\leq\,B\lambda_{\hskip 0.19925ptn-\hskip-0.39864pt1}^{\hskip-0.79727pt2\hskip 0.39864ptr_{1}}+\frac{3}{2}\eta_{\hskip 0.19925pt1}\hskip-0.50003ptL_{\hskip-0.39864ptf}\,\text{ for all }\,n\geq N\right]\,\geq\,1-(\beta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}+\delta+\pi)\hskip 1.00006pt.

Proof.
.

Let
\omega\in M\cap I
be a sample point corresponding to Assumption
3.3
and Assumption
4.2
.
For simplicity, we use the notation (
12
) for the rest of the proof.
We denote
e_{n}=f(x_{n})-\Popt
for the approximation error at iteration
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
First, by definition of the curvature constant, we have

f(x_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}\hskip-0.24994pt)\,\leq\,f(x_{n}\hskip-0.24994pt)+\langle\hskip 0.50003pts_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}-\hskip 0.50003ptx_{n}\,|\,\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x_{n}\hskip-0.24994pt)\hskip 0.50003pt\rangle\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}+\frac{C_{\hskip-0.40974ptf}}{2}\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974pt2}

(48)

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
Combining Lemma
4.2
and Lemma
4.2
, we further obtain that

\displaystyle\langle\hskip 0.50003pts_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}-\hskip 0.50003ptx_{n}\,|\,\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x_{n})\hskip 0.50003pt\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\,-\frac{1}{2}e_{n}-\frac{\alpha}{8}\lVert\hskip 0.50003pts_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}-\hskip 0.50003ptx_{n}\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert^{2}\hskip 0.50003pt\lVert\hskip 0.50003pt\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x_{n}\hskip-0.24994pt)\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert+\frac{3}{2}\hskip 0.50003ptd_{H}(D_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1},D)L_{\hskip-0.40974ptf}

\displaystyle\leq\,-\frac{1}{2}e_{n}-\frac{\alpha}{8}\lVert\hskip 0.50003pts_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}-\hskip 0.50003ptx_{n}\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert^{2}\sqrt{\frac{\mu}{2}e_{n}}+\frac{3}{2}\hskip 0.50003ptd_{H}(D_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1},D)L_{\hskip-0.40974ptf}

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, such that together with (
48
) and
d_{H}(D_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1},D)\leq c_{1}\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974ptr_{1}}+\eta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
we obtain the recursion bound

e_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}\leq\left(\hskip-1.49994pt1-\frac{\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}}{2}\hskip-1.49994pt\right)\hskip-1.49994pte_{n}-\lVert\hskip 0.50003pts_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}\hskip-0.50003pt-\hskip 0.50003ptx_{n}\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert^{2}\sqrt{e_{n}}\left(\hskip-1.00006pt\frac{\alpha^{2}\hskip-0.50003pt\mu}{128}\hskip-1.00006pt\right)^{\hskip-2.45836pt\frac{1}{2}}\hskip-3.00003pt\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}+\left(\hskip 0.0pt\frac{3}{2}\hskip 0.50003ptc_{1}\hskip-0.50003ptL_{\hskip-0.40974ptf}+\frac{C_{\hskip-0.40974ptf}}{2}\hskip-1.00006pt\right)\hskip-1.00006pt\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.81949pt1+r_{1}}+\frac{3}{2}\hskip 0.50003pt\eta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}\hskip-0.50003ptL_{\hskip-0.40974ptf}\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}

(49)

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
Now we turn to finding a lower bound for
\lVert\hskip 0.50003pts_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}-\hskip 0.50003ptx_{n}\rVert
for all
n\geq N
for some
N\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.
To this end, we set
\rho=h(x^{\star},\relbd(D))
and recall that by construction of the subsolution process
\mathbf{S}
it holds that

\langle\hskip 0.50003pts_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}-\hskip 0.50003ptx_{n}\,|\,\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x_{n}\hskip-0.24994pt)\hskip 0.50003pt\rangle\,\leq\,\langle\hskip 0.50003ptt-x_{n}\,|\,\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x_{n}\hskip-0.24994pt)\hskip 0.50003pt\rangle

(50)

for all
t\in D_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}
.
Since by assumption
3\hskip 0.50003pt\eta_{1}<\rho
and using again that
d_{H}(D_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1},D)\leq c_{1}\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974ptr_{1}}+\eta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we have that for

N_{0}\,\coloneqq\,\left\lceil 2\hskip-1.99997pt\left(\frac{6\hskip 0.50003ptc_{1}}{\rho}\right)^{\hskip-2.45836pt\frac{1}{\hskip 0.36807ptr_{1}}}\hskip-3.00003pt-2\right\rceil\in\mathbb{N}_{0}

it holds
2\hskip 0.50003ptd_{H}(D_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1},D)<\rho
, such that
x^{\star}\in D_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}
for all
n\geq N_{0}
.
Restricting ourselves to the complement of the event in (
47
), without loss of generality, we can assume that
\lVert\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x_{n})\rVert>0
for all
n\geq N_{0}
, such that together with (
50
) and

t\,=\,x^{\star}-\frac{\rho}{2\hskip 0.50003pt\lVert\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x_{n}\hskip 0.50003pt)\rVert}\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x_{n}\hskip 0.50003pt)\in D_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}

we find that

\displaystyle\langle\hskip 0.50003pts_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}-\hskip 0.50003ptx_{n}\,|\,\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x_{n}\hskip-0.24994pt)\hskip 0.50003pt\rangle\,\leq\,\langle\hskip 0.50003ptx^{\star}-\hskip 0.50003ptx_{n}\,|\,\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x_{n}\hskip-0.24994pt)\hskip 0.50003pt\rangle-\frac{\rho}{2}\lVert\hskip 0.50003pt\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x_{n}\hskip-0.24994pt)\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert\,\leq\,-\frac{\rho}{2}\lVert\hskip 0.50003pt\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x_{n}\hskip-0.24994pt)\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert

(51)

for all
n\geq N_{0}
, where in the second inequality we used that
\langle\hskip 0.50003ptx^{\star}-x_{n}\,|\,\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x_{n}\hskip-0.24994pt)\hskip 0.50003pt\rangle\leq 0
by optimality of
x^{\star}
.
Using (
51
) we obtain that for all
n\geq N_{0}
it holds that

0\,<\,\frac{\rho}{2}\lVert\hskip 0.50003pt\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x_{n})\rVert\,\leq\,\langle\hskip 0.50003ptx_{n}-\hskip 0.50003pts_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}\,|\,\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x_{n}\hskip-0.24994pt)\hskip 0.50003pt\rangle\,\leq\,\lVert\hskip 0.50003ptx_{n}-\hskip 0.50003pts_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}\rVert\hskip 0.50003pt\lVert\hskip 0.50003pt\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x_{n}\hskip-0.24994pt)\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert\hskip 1.00006pt,

such that dividing by
\lVert\hskip 0.50003pt\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x_{n})\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert
yields

\lVert s_{n+1}-\hskip 0.50003ptx_{n}\rVert\,\geq\,\frac{\rho}{2}\,>\,0

(52)

for all
n\geq N_{0}
.
Thus, we can apply Proposition
4.2
to (
49
), using (
52
), to obtain the hitting time
\mathbf{N}\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{N}
defined by

\mathbf{N}\,\coloneqq\,\max\left\{\hskip-1.00006ptN_{0},\mathbf{1}_{\{r\,\geq\,1/2\}}\hskip-3.00003pt\left\lceil 2\hskip-1.49994pt\left(\frac{2\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{B}}{3\hskip 0.50003ptc_{1}\hskip-0.50003ptL_{\hskip-0.40974ptf}+C_{\hskip-0.61455ptf}}\right)^{\hskip-2.45836ptr_{1}}\hskip-1.99997pt-2\right\rceil\right\},

(53)

where

\mathbf{B}\,\coloneqq\,\hskip-1.00006ptf(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{N_{0}}\hskip-0.50003pt)-\Popt+\hskip 1.99997pt\frac{512}{\alpha^{2}\rho^{2}\mu}\hskip-1.00006pt\left(3\hskip 0.50003ptc_{1}\hskip-0.50003ptL_{\hskip-0.39864ptf}+C_{\hskip-0.59789ptf}\right)^{2}\hskip-1.99997pt+\frac{3}{2}\hskip 0.50003ptc_{1}\hskip-0.50003ptL_{\hskip-0.39864ptf}+\frac{1}{2}C_{\hskip-0.59789ptf}\hskip 1.00006pt,

such that

e_{n}\,\leq\,\mathbf{B}(\omega)\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn-\hskip-0.40974pt1}^{\hskip-0.81949pt2\hskip 0.40974ptr_{1}}+\frac{3}{2}\hskip 0.50003pt\eta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}\hskip-0.50003ptL_{\hskip-0.40974ptf}\hskip 1.00006pt

(54)

for all
n\geq\mathbf{N}(\omega)+1
.
Furthermore, we can use Remark
3.3
to bound

f(\mathbf{X}_{N_{0}})-\Popt\,\leq\,(2\hskip 0.50003ptc_{1}\hskip-0.50003ptL_{\hskip-0.59789ptf}+C_{\hskip-0.59789ptf})\lambda_{\hskip-0.39864ptN_{0}}^{\hskip-0.39864ptr_{1}}+\eta_{\hskip 0.19925pt1}\hskip-0.50003ptL_{\hskip-0.59789ptf}\,\leq\,(2\hskip 0.50003ptc_{1}\hskip-0.50003pt+\eta_{\hskip 0.19925pt1})L_{\hskip-0.59789ptf}+C_{\hskip-0.59789ptf}\hskip 1.00006pt,

such that we can replace the random variable
\mathbf{B}
with the constant

B\,=\,\frac{512}{\alpha^{2}\rho^{2}\mu}\hskip-1.00006pt\left(3\hskip 0.50003ptc_{1}\hskip-0.50003ptL_{\hskip-0.40974ptf}+C_{\hskip-0.61455ptf}\right)^{2}\hskip-1.99997pt+\left(\frac{7}{2}\hskip 0.50003ptc_{1}+\hskip-0.50003pt\eta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}\hskip-1.49994pt\right)\hskip-1.49994ptL_{\hskip-0.40974ptf}+\frac{3}{2}C_{\hskip-0.61455ptf}

in (
53
) and (
54
).
The claim now follows since
\mathbb{P}[M\cap I\hskip 0.74997pt]\geq 1-(\beta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}+\delta)
and the probability of the complement of the event in (
47
) is given by
1-\pi
, such that their intersections have probability at least
1-(\beta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}+\delta+\pi)
.
∎

At first, using the probability
\pi
in Theorem
4.2
may seem restrictive for the overall result.
However, this is more of a technical subtlety.
In general it is even positive if
\pi
is large since this means that we can reach optimality in
N_{\text{optimal}}\in\mathbb{N}
many steps in Algorithm
1
.
Moreover, taking a closer look at the proof of Theorem
4.2
, we can see that if
N_{\text{optimal}}
is large, accelerated convergence up to this point still holds.

4.3.  Comments on the Assumptions

We briefly comment on the assumptions made in this section.
More specifically, given a domain approximation process
\mathbf{D}
, we will introduce a convenient way of constructing natural extended domain approximation processes satisfying the more restrictive Assumption
4.1
and Assumption
4.2
for convergence acceleration.

Extended Domain Approximations via Morphology.

Given a valid domain approximation process
\mathbf{D}
we can naturally extend it using tools from morphology called dilation and erosion.

Definition 7  –
(Dilation and Erosion)

Let
X,Y\subseteq H
be two sets.
The
dilation
of
X
by
Y
is defined as

X\oplus Y\,\coloneqq\,\bigcup\hskip 1.99997pt\{x+y\,\colon x\in X,y\in Y\}

and the
erosion
of
X
by
Y
is defined as

X\ominus Y\,\coloneqq\,\{z\in H\,\colon z+Y\subseteq X\}\hskip 1.00006pt,

where for
z\in H
we have
z+Y=\{z+y\,\colon y\in Y\}
.

Note that in the literature, for two sets
X,Y\subseteq H
, there is no difference between the dilation
X\oplus Y
and the Minkowski sum
X+Y
.
However, in some contexts the Minkowski difference
X-Y
may be defined as
X+(-Y)
, such that it does not necessarily coincide with the erosion
X\ominus Y
.
To avoid confusion, we therefore stick with the morphology terminology.
The main motivation for the use of the concepts of dilation and erosion is that these constructions yield an interpretable extension and contraction of the set
X
, respectively, which additionally can preserve the properties that are important in our analysis.

Lemma 10

Let
X,Y\subseteq H
be nonempty, compact, and convex sets.
Then, the dilation
X\oplus Y
is nonempty, compact, and convex and the erosion
X\ominus Y
is compact and convex.
If
X
is
\alpha
-strongly convex for some
\alpha>0
and it holds

\diam(X)^{2}\,\geq\,\frac{8}{\alpha}\diam(\hskip 0.50003ptY)\hskip 1.00006pt,

(55)

then
X\ominus Y
is also nonempty.

Having Lemma
4.3
at hand, we define the domain extension map

\mathbf{D}^{\hskip-0.40974pt+}\colon\mathbb{N}\times\Omega\to\mathscr{D},\;(n,\omega)\,\mapsto\,\mathbf{D}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)\,\oplus\,\mathbb{B}(0,d_{H}(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega),D))\hskip 1.00006pt.

(56)

For the corresponding domain contraction map, as can be seen in Lemma
4.3
, we have to assume that there exists a sequence of nonnegative random variables
(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and a constant
\gamma\in[\hskip 0.24994pt0,1]
such that

\mathbb{P}\hskip-1.99997pt\left[\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\text{ \hskip-0.50003ptis }\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{n}\text{-\hskip 0.50003ptstrongly convex and }\diam(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt)^{2}\,\geq\,\frac{16}{\hskip 1.99997pt\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{n}}d_{H}(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn},D)\,\text{ for all }\,n\in\mathbb{N}\hskip 0.50003pt\right]\,\geq\,1-\gamma\hskip 1.00006pt.

(57)

Denoting with
A\in\Sigma

the event corresponding to (
57
), whose measurability is shown in detail in Appendix
B.3
, the domain contraction map is then given by

\mathbf{D}^{\hskip-0.40974pt-}\colon\mathbb{N}\times\Omega\to\mathscr{D},\;(n,\omega)\,\mapsto\,\mathbf{1}_{\hskip-0.40974ptA}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)\cdot\hskip 0.50003pt(\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.40974ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)\,\ominus\,\mathbb{B}(0,d_{H}(\hskip 0.24994pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.40974ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega),D)))\hskip 1.00006pt,

(58)

where the product is to be understood pointwise.
By Lemma
4.3
, both maps
\mathbf{D}^{\hskip-0.40974pt+}
and
\mathbf{D}^{\hskip-0.40974pt-}
are well-defined.
However, it remains to show that these maps form stochastic processes satisfying Assumption
4.1
and Assumption
4.2
, respectively.
This follows from the next result which additionally uses the Hausdorff distance between
\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}
and
D
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
to derive upper bounds on the Hausdorff distance between the domain extension
\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.40974ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974pt+}
and the domain contraction
\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.40974ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974pt-}
and
D
, respectively.
A proof od this result can be found in Appendix
B.3
.

Lemma 11

Let
\mathbf{D}
be a domain approximation process.
Then, the extension map
\mathbf{D}^{\hskip-0.40974pt+}
defined as in (
56
) is a valid domain approximation process satisfying Assumption
4.1
for
\delta=0
and

\mathbb{P}\hskip-1.99997pt\left[\hskip 0.50003ptd_{H}(\hskip 0.24994pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.40974ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974pt+},D)\,\leq\,2\hskip 0.50003ptd_{H}(\hskip 0.24994pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.40974ptn},D)\,\text{ for all }\,n\in\mathbb{N}\hskip 1.00006pt\right]\,=\,1\hskip 1.00006pt.

Furthermore, the contraction map
\mathbf{D}^{\hskip-0.40974pt-}
defined as in (
58
) is a valid domain approximation process satisfying Assumption
4.2
for
\delta=\gamma
and

\mathbb{P}\hskip-1.99997pt\left[\hskip 0.50003ptd_{H}(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{-},D)\,\leq\,d_{H}(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn},D)+\sqrt{\frac{8}{\hskip 1.99997pt\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{n}}d_{H}(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn},D)}\,\text{ for all }\,n\in\mathbb{N}\hskip 0.50003pt\right]\,\geq\,1-\gamma\hskip 1.00006pt.

\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.41225ptn}^{\hskip-0.41225pt+}\hskip-1.27496pt(\omega)
\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.41225ptn}^{\hskip-0.41225pt-}\hskip-1.27496pt(\omega)
\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.41225ptn}\hskip-0.85005pt(\omega)
D

Figure 2:
Conceptual illustration of the introduced concepts of domain extension (dilation) and domain contraction (erosion) for a fixed sample point
\omega\in\Omega
.
The Hausdorff distance between
\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)
and
D
is exactly the distance between the two gray points (dotted line).
The domain extension
\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974pt+}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)
is given in blue and is, in particular, no longer polygonal.
The domain contraction
\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974pt-}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)
is given in red.

Remark 9

In a practical setting the definitions (
56
) and (
58
) are not directly applicable since the actual quantity
d_{H}(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn},D)
at each step
n\in\mathbb{N}
is most likely unknown.
However, if we assume that Assumption
3.3
holds, then we can simply replace the Hausdorff distance
d_{H}(\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn},D)
with
c_{1}\hskip-0.50003pt\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974ptr_{1}}+\eta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
in the constructions above.
In this case, the upper bounds on the Hausdorff distance from Lemma
4.3
can be adapted accordingly, taking into consideration the probabilistic tolerance
\beta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}
.
Hence, this yields implementable surrogate extensions and contractions controlled by the known convergence rates.

5.  Numerical Examples

We present two numerical examples.
The first is a simple academic toy problem, which serves to illustrate all theoretical results and assumptions required in our paper.
The second is a more complex distributionally robust Linear Quadratic Gaussian (LQG) problem, where we demonstrate the runtime efficiency of our proposed method compared to existing approaches.

The

code:
https://github.com/Nuwanda314/Recursive_Adaptive_Frank-Wolfe_Algorithm_Experiments

5.1.  Simple Example: Quadratic Objective on Rectangular Domain

For the purpose of illustrating the details of the assumptions and results we derived in the previous sections, we consider a simple problem with a quadratic objective function on a rectangular domain.
However, for the sake of simplicity, we will restrict ourselves to the one -dimensional case and note that an extension to higher dimensions would be possible with slight adjustments.
More explicitly, we consider the objective function

f\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}\hskip 0.24994pt,\;x\,\mapsto\,(x-x^{\star})^{2}

for some fixed point
x^{\star}\in\mathbb{R}
and the problem domain
D=[\hskip 0.24994pta,b\hskip 0.24994pt]
, where
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
with
a<b
.
To approximate the problem domain, we assume to have access to a sequence
(\mathbf{Y}_{\hskip-0.40974ptn}\hskip-0.24994pt)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of independent samples of a random variable
\mathbf{Y}\sim\Unif(a,b)
, that is, we assume to be able to iteratively draw points from the problem domain.
To highlight the effect of the domain approximation on the convergence of Algorithm
1
, we present two different approaches.
The first approach uses (centered) moments of the random variable
\mathbf{Y}
, while the second one is based on the convex hull of the samples.

Moment-Based Domain Approximations.

Recall that for
\mathbf{Y}\sim\Unif(a,b)
the first moment and second centered moment, that is, the mean and the variance, are given by

\mu\,\coloneqq\,\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{Y}]\,=\,\frac{a+b}{2}\quad\text{and}\quad\sigma^{2}\,\coloneqq\,\mathbb{V}[\mathbf{Y}]\,=\,\mathbb{E}[(\mathbf{Y}-\mu)^{2}]\,=\,\frac{(b-a)^{2}}{12\hskip 3.99994pt}\hskip 0.50003pt.

Hence, we can reformulate
a=\mu-\sqrt{3}\hskip 0.50003pt\sigma
and
b=\mu+\sqrt{3}\hskip 0.50003pt\sigma
, such that considering the empirical estimators

\mathbf{E}_{\hskip 0.12291ptn}\hskip-0.24994pt(\mathbf{Y})\,=\,\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\hskip 0.2048pt=1}^{n}\mathbf{Y}_{\hskip-0.81949pti}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbf{V}_{\hskip-0.81949ptn}\hskip-0.24994pt(\mathbf{Y})\,=\,\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\hskip 0.2048pt=1}^{n}\hskip 0.50003pt(\mathbf{Y}_{\hskip-0.81949pti}-\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{E}_{\hskip 0.12291ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\mathbf{Y})\hskip-0.50003pt)^{2}

(59)

of
\mu
and
\sigma^{2}
, respectively, we obtain the (centered) moment based empirical estimators

\mathbf{A}_{\hskip 0.12291ptn}^{\hskip-1.43405pt\text{MB}}\,=\,\mathbf{E}_{\hskip 0.12291ptn}\hskip-0.24994pt(\mathbf{Y})-\sqrt{3\mathbf{V}_{\hskip-0.81949ptn}\hskip-0.24994pt(\mathbf{Y})}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbf{B}_{n}^{\text{MB}}\,=\,\mathbf{E}_{\hskip 0.12291ptn}\hskip-0.24994pt(\mathbf{Y})+\sqrt{3\mathbf{V}_{\hskip-0.81949ptn}\hskip-0.24994pt(\mathbf{Y})}

of
a
and
b
, respectively.
Thus, an obvious moment-based (MB) choice for a candidate of a domain approximation process for the problem domain
D=[\hskip 0.24994pta,b\hskip 0.24994pt]
is

\mathbf{D}^{\hskip-0.2048pt\text{MB}}\colon\Omega\times\mathbb{N}\to\mathscr{D},\;(\omega,n)\,\mapsto\,[\hskip 0.24994pt\mathbf{A}_{\hskip 0.12291ptn}^{\hskip-1.43405pt\text{MB}}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega),\mathbf{B}_{n}^{\text{MB}}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)\hskip 0.24994pt]\hskip 1.00006pt.

(60)

Clearly
\mathbf{D}_{n}^{\hskip-0.2048pt\text{MB}}
attains nonempty, compact, and convex values for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, such that
\mathbf{D}^{\hskip-0.2048pt\text{MB}}
is well-defined.
Furthermore, by definition of
\mathbf{A}_{\hskip 0.12291ptn}^{\hskip-1.43405pt\text{MB}}
and
\mathbf{B}_{n}^{\text{MB}}
we can simply rewrite

\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.2048pt\text{MB}}\,=\,[\hskip 0.24994pt\mathbf{A}_{\hskip 0.12291ptn}^{\hskip-1.43405pt\text{MB}},\mathbf{B}_{n}^{\text{MB}}\hskip 0.24994pt]\,=\,\mathbb{B}\hskip-1.99997pt\left(\hskip-0.50003pt\mathbf{E}_{\hskip 0.12291ptn}\hskip-0.24994pt(\mathbf{Y}),\sqrt{3\mathbf{V}_{\hskip-0.81949ptn}\hskip-0.24994pt(\mathbf{Y})}\hskip-0.50003pt\right)\hskip-0.50003pt,

such that by Lemma
3.4
we know that
\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.2048pt\text{MB}}
is
\Sigma
-measurable for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and, therefore,
\mathbf{D}^{\hskip-0.2048pt\text{MB}}
is a stochastic process that can be used as domain approximation process.
Hence, it remains to check whether
\mathbf{D}^{\hskip-0.2048pt\text{MB}}
satisfies the necessary assumptions of Section
3
.
First, it is easy to see that
\mathbf{E}_{\hskip 0.12291ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\mathbf{Y})\in[\hskip 0.24994pta,b\hskip 0.50003pt]
and
\mathbf{V}_{\hskip-1.22911ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\mathbf{Y})\in[\hskip 0.24994pt0,(b-a)^{2}\hskip 0.50003pt]
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, such that

\mathbf{A}_{\hskip 0.12291ptn}^{\hskip-1.43405pt\text{MB}}\,=\,\mathbf{E}_{\hskip 0.12291ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\mathbf{Y})-\sqrt{3\mathbf{V}_{\hskip-1.22911ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\mathbf{Y})}\,\geq\,a-\sqrt{3\hskip 0.50003pt}(b-a)\,=\,(1+\sqrt{3\hskip 0.50003pt})\hskip 0.50003pta-\sqrt{3\hskip 0.50003pt}b\,\geq\,-3(\lvert a\rvert+\lvert b\rvert)

and, analogously,
\mathbf{B}_{n}^{\text{MB}}\leq 3\hskip 0.50003pt(\lvert a\rvert+\lvert b\rvert)
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Thus, we have

\bigcup\hskip 1.99997pt\{\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.2048pt\text{MB}}\,\colon n\in\mathbb{N}\}\cup D\,\subseteq\,3\hskip 0.50003pt(\lvert a\rvert+\lvert b\rvert)\hskip 0.50003pt[-1,1]\hskip 1.00006pt,

such that defining the random domain extension map
\mathbf{E}^{\text{MB}}\equiv 3\hskip 0.24994pt(\lvert a\rvert+\lvert b\rvert)[-1,1]
this directly implies that

\mathbb{P}\hskip-1.99997pt\left[\hskip 1.49994pt\bigcup\,\{\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.2048pt\text{MB}}\,\colon n\in\mathbb{N}\}\cup D\,\subseteq\,\mathbf{E}^{\text{MB}}\,\subseteq\,\dom(f)\hskip 1.00006pt\right]\,=\,1\hskip 1.00006pt,

such that
\mathbf{D}^{\hskip-0.2048pt\text{MB}}
satisfies Remark
2.1
for
\varepsilon_{0}=0
.
To show that
\mathbf{D}^{\hskip-0.2048pt\text{MB}}
also satisfies Assumption
3.3
we have to consider the next result, for which a proof can be found in Appendix
B.4
.

Proposition 3

Let
\mathbf{D}^{\hskip-0.2048pt\text{MB}}
be the domain approximation process defined as in (
60
).
Then, for all
r\in[\hskip 0.24994pt0,1/2)
and all
\beta\in(0,1]
it holds that

\mathbb{P}\hskip-1.99997pt\left[\hskip 0.24994ptd_{H}(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.2048pt\text{MB}},D)\,\leq\,c^{\text{MB}}n^{\hskip-0.40974pt-r}\text{ for all }n\in\mathbb{N}\hskip 0.50003pt\right]\,\geq\,1-\beta\hskip 0.50003pt,

where for
s=1-2\hskip 0.50003ptr
we have

c^{\text{MB}}\,=\,6\hskip 0.50003pt(b-a)\hskip-1.99997pt\left(\hskip-1.99997pt2+\hskip-1.00006pt\left(\hskip-0.50003pt\frac{8\hskip 0.50003pt\Gamma(1/s)}{s\hskip 0.24994pt\beta}\hskip-0.50003pt\right)^{\hskip-2.04861pts/2}\right)^{\hskip-2.25342pt2}\hskip-1.99997pt

and
\Gamma
denotes the complete Gamma function.

By Proposition
5.1
and since

c^{\text{MB}}\frac{1}{(n+1)^{r}}\,\leq\,c^{\text{MB}}\frac{2^{\hskip 0.2048ptr}}{(n+2)^{r}}\,=\,c^{\text{MB}}\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974ptr}

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we directly obtain that

\mathbb{P}\hskip-1.99997pt\left[\hskip 0.24994ptd_{H}(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn+\hskip-0.40974pt1}^{\hskip-0.2048pt\text{MB}},D)\,\leq\,c^{\text{MB}}\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974ptr}\text{ for all }n\in\mathbb{N}\hskip 0.50003pt\right]\,\geq\,1-\beta\hskip 0.50003pt,

such that
\mathbf{D}^{\hskip-0.2048pt\text{MB}}
satisfies Assumption
3.3
with
r_{1}=r,\beta_{1}=\beta,c_{1}=c^{\text{MB}}
and
\eta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}=0
for all
r\in[\hskip 0.50003pt0,1/2)
and
\beta\in(\hskip-0.24994pt0,1]
.
Since the complete Gamma function
\Gamma
in general cannot be computed explicitly, we use the fact that for
m\in\mathbb{N}
it holds
\Gamma(m)=(m-1)!
to derive a computable version of the constant
c^{\text{MB}}
in Proposition
5.1
.
To this end, we choose
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
and set

r^{\text{MB}}\,=\,\frac{m-1}{2\hskip 0.50003ptm}\,<\,\frac{1}{2}\quad\text{ and }\quad\beta\,=\,10^{-\tau}\in(0,1]\hskip 1.00006pt,

such that we can simplify

c^{\text{MB}}\,=\,6\hskip 0.24994pt(b-a)\hskip-0.74997pt\left(\hskip-1.00006pt(\hskip-0.50003pt2\cdot 10^{\hskip 0.2048pt\tau}\hskip 0.24994ptm\hskip 0.24994pt!\hskip 0.50003pt)^{1/(2\hskip 0.40974ptm)}\hskip-0.50003pt+2\hskip-0.50003pt\right)^{\hskip-0.81949pt2}\hskip-1.00006pt.

Overall, we have shown that the MB domain approximation process
\mathbf{D}^{\hskip-0.2048pt\text{MB}}
satisfies the necessary assumption to apply Theorem
3.3
, or in our case Remark
3.3
.
Computing the other constants appearing in Remark
3.3
, that is,

•

\diam(\mathbf{E}^{\text{MB}})\,=\,6\hskip 0.50003pt(\lvert\hskip 0.50003ptb\hskip 0.50003pt\rvert+\lvert\hskip 0.50003pta\hskip 0.50003pt\rvert)

•

\mathbf{C}^{\text{MB}}\,\leq\,\diam(\mathbf{E}^{\text{MB}})^{2}L_{\nabla}\,=\,72\hskip 0.50003pt(\lvert\hskip 0.50003ptb\hskip 0.50003pt\rvert+\lvert\hskip 0.50003pta\hskip 0.50003pt\rvert)^{2}

•

\mathbf{L}^{\hskip-1.22911pt\text{MB}}\,=\,\max\hskip 1.00006pt\{2\hskip 1.00006pt\lvert\hskip 0.50003ptx-x^{\star}\rvert\,\colon x\in\mathbf{E}^{\text{MB}}\}\,=\,6\hskip 0.50003pt(\lvert\hskip 0.50003ptb\hskip 0.50003pt\rvert+\lvert\hskip 0.50003pta\hskip 0.50003pt\rvert)+2\hskip 0.50003pt\lvert\hskip 0.50003ptx^{\star}\rvert

we obtain that

\mathbb{P}\hskip-1.49994pt\left[\hskip 1.00006pt\lvert\hskip 0.50003ptf(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\text{MB}}\hskip-0.50003pt)-\Popt\rvert\,\leq\,A^{\hskip-0.39864pt\text{MB}}\lambda_{\hskip 0.19925ptn}^{\hskip-0.39864ptr^{\text{MB}}}\text{ for all }n\in\mathbb{N}\hskip 1.00006pt\right]\,\geq\,1-\beta\hskip 1.00006pt,

where

\displaystyle A^{\hskip-0.40974pt\text{MB}}

\displaystyle=\,2\hskip 0.50003ptc^{\text{MB}}\mathbf{L}^{\hskip-1.22911pt\text{MB}}+\mathbf{C}^{\text{MB}}.

Convex Hull Domain Approximation.

For the second approach we first note that for
n\in\mathbb{N}
points
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\in\mathbb{R}
we have

\convex(\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\})\,=\,[\hskip 0.24994pt\min\hskip 1.00006pt\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\},\max\hskip 1.00006pt\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}\hskip 0.24994pt]\hskip 1.00006pt,

such that with the convex hull (CH) empirical estimators

\mathbf{A}_{\hskip 0.12291ptn}^{\hskip-1.63885pt\text{CH}}\,=\,\min\hskip 1.00006pt\{\mathbf{Y}_{\hskip-0.81949pti}\,\colon i\in[n]\}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbf{B}_{n}^{\text{CH}}\,=\,\max\hskip 1.00006pt\{\mathbf{Y}_{\hskip-0.81949pti}\,\colon i\in[n]\}\hskip 1.00006pt,

for
a
and
b
, respectively, we can define the candidate of a domain approximation process

\mathbf{D}^{\hskip-0.2048pt\text{CH}}\colon\Omega\times\mathbb{N}\to\mathscr{D},\;(\omega,n)\,\mapsto\,[\hskip 0.24994pt\mathbf{A}_{\hskip 0.12291ptn}^{\hskip-1.63885pt\text{CH}}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega),\mathbf{B}_{n}^{\text{CH}}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)\hskip 0.24994pt]\,=\,\convex(\{\mathbf{Y}_{\hskip-0.81949pti}\hskip-0.24994pt(\omega)\,\colon i\in[n]\})\hskip 1.00006pt.

(61)

As before, we trivially have that
\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048pt}^{\hskip-0.2048pt\text{CH}}
has nonempty, compact, and convex values for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, such that
\mathbf{D}^{\hskip-0.2048pt\text{CH}}
is well-defined.
Furthermore, we can rewrite

\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.2048pt\text{CH}}\,=\,[\hskip 0.24994pt\mathbf{A}_{\hskip 0.12291ptn}^{\hskip-1.63885pt\text{CH}},\mathbf{B}_{n}^{\text{CH}}\hskip 0.24994pt]\,=\,\mathbb{B}\hskip-1.99997pt\left(\hskip-0.50003pt\frac{\mathbf{A}_{\hskip 0.12291ptn}^{\hskip-1.63885pt\text{CH}}+\mathbf{B}_{n}^{\text{CH}}}{2},\frac{\mathbf{B}_{n}^{\text{CH}}-\mathbf{A}_{\hskip 0.12291ptn}^{\hskip-1.43405pt\text{CH}}}{2}\hskip-0.50003pt\right)\hskip-0.50003pt,

such that by Lemma
3.4
we know that
\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.2048pt\text{CH}}
is
\Sigma
-measurable for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and, therefore, a stochastic process that can be used as domain approximation process.
It remains to check whether
\mathbf{D}^{\hskip-0.2048pt\text{CH}}
satisfies the necessary assumptions of Section
3
.
Since by construction
\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.2048pt\text{CH}}
is a subset of
D
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, setting
\mathbf{E}^{\text{CH}}\equiv D
, we obtain that
\mathbf{D}^{\hskip-0.2048pt\text{CH}}
satisfies Remark
2.1
for
\varepsilon_{0}=0
.
To show that
\mathbf{D}^{\hskip-0.2048pt\text{CH}}
also satisfies Assumption
3.3
we have to consider the next result, whose proof is provided in Appendix
B.4
.

Proposition 4

Let
\mathbf{D}^{\hskip-0.2048pt\text{CH}}
be the domain approximation process defined as in (
61
).
Then, for all
r\in[\hskip 0.24994pt0,1)
and all
\beta\in(0,1]
it holds that

\mathbb{P}\hskip-1.99997pt\left[\hskip 0.24994ptd_{H}(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.2048pt\text{CH}},D)\,\leq\,c^{\text{CH}}\hskip 0.50003ptn^{\hskip-0.40974pt-r}\text{ for all }n\in\mathbb{N}\hskip 0.50003pt\right]\,\geq\,1-\beta\hskip 0.50003pt,

where for
s=1-r
we have

c^{\text{CH}}\,=\,(b-a)\hskip-1.00006pt\left(\hskip-0.50003pt\frac{2\hskip 0.50003pt\Gamma(1/s)}{s\hskip 0.24994pt\beta}\hskip-0.50003pt\right)^{\hskip-2.04861pts}

and
\Gamma
denotes the complete Gamma function.

Similar to before, from Proposition
5.1
we find that

\mathbb{P}\hskip-1.99997pt\left[\hskip 0.24994ptd_{H}(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn+\hskip-0.40974pt1}^{\hskip-0.2048pt\text{CH}},D)\,\leq\,c^{\text{CH}}\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974ptr}\text{ for all }n\in\mathbb{N}\hskip 0.50003pt\right]\,\geq\,1-\beta\hskip 0.50003pt,

such that
\mathbf{D}^{\hskip-0.2048pt\text{CH}}
satisfies Assumption
3.3
with
r_{1}=r,\beta_{1}=\beta,c_{1}=c^{\text{CH}}
and
\eta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}=0
for all
r\in[\hskip 0.50003pt0,1)
and
\beta\in(\hskip-0.24994pt0,1]
.
Again, we aim to derive a computable version of the constant
c^{\text{CH}}
in Proposition
5.1
.
To this end, we choose
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
and set

r^{\text{CH}}\,=\,\frac{m-1}{m}\,<\,1\quad\text{ and }\quad\beta\,=\,10^{-\tau}\in(0,1]\hskip 1.00006pt,

such that we can simplify

c^{\text{CH}}\,=\,(b-a)\hskip 0.50003pt(\hskip-0.50003pt2\cdot 10^{\hskip 0.2048pt\tau}\hskip 0.24994ptm\hskip 0.24994pt!\hskip 0.50003pt)^{1/m}\hskip 1.00006pt.

Overall, we have shown that the CH domain approximation process
\mathbf{D}^{\hskip-0.2048pt\text{CH}}
satisfies the necessary assumption to apply Theorem
3.3
, or in this case Remark
3.3
.
Computing the other constants appearing in Remark
3.3
, that is,

•

\diam(\mathbf{E}^{\text{CH}})\,=\,b-a

•

\mathbf{C}^{\text{CH}}\,\leq\,\diam(\mathbf{E}^{\text{CH}})^{2}L_{\nabla}\,=\,2\hskip 0.50003pt(b-a)^{2}

•

\mathbf{L}^{\hskip-1.22911pt\text{CH}}\,=\,\max\hskip 1.00006pt\{2\hskip 1.00006pt\lvert\hskip 0.50003ptx-x^{\star}\rvert\,\colon x\in\mathbf{E}^{\text{CH}}\}\,=\,2\hskip 0.50003pt\max\hskip 1.00006pt\{\lvert\hskip 0.50003pta-x^{\star}\rvert\hskip 0.24994pt,\lvert\hskip 0.50003ptb-x^{\star}\rvert\}

we obtain that

\mathbb{P}\hskip-1.49994pt\left[\hskip 1.00006pt\lvert\hskip 0.50003ptf(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\text{CH}}\hskip-0.50003pt)-\Popt\rvert\,\leq\,A^{\hskip-0.39864pt\text{CH}}\lambda_{\hskip 0.19925ptn}^{\hskip-0.39864ptr^{\text{CH}}}\text{ for all }n\in\mathbb{N}\hskip 1.00006pt\right]\,\geq\,1-\beta\hskip 1.00006pt,

where

\displaystyle A^{\hskip-0.40974pt\text{CH}}

\displaystyle=\,2\hskip 0.50003ptc^{\text{CH}}\mathbf{L}^{\hskip-1.22911pt\text{CH}}+\mathbf{C}^{\text{CH}}.

In contrast to the moment-based domain approximation process
\mathbf{D}^{\text{MB}}
, the convex hull domain approximation process
\mathbf{D}^{\text{CH}}
also satisfies Assumption
4.2
for
\delta=0
as
\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}^{\text{CH}}\subseteq D
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Hence, we can also consider the accelerated convergence results from Section
4
, more explicitly Theorem
4.2
.
Computing the remaining constants appearing in Theorem
4.2
, that is,

•

\diam(D)\,=\,b-a

•

C_{\hskip-0.40974ptf}\,\leq\,\diam(D)^{2}L_{\nabla}\,=\,2\hskip 0.50003pt(b-a)^{2}

•

L_{\hskip-0.40974ptf}\,=\,\max\hskip 1.00006pt\{\hskip 0.50003pt2\hskip 1.00006pt\lvert\hskip 0.50003ptx-x^{\star}\rvert\,\colon x\in D\}\,=\,2\max\{\lvert\hskip 0.50003pta-x^{\star}\rvert,\lvert\hskip 0.50003ptb-x^{\star}\rvert\}

•

\alpha=4\hskip 0.50003pt/(b-a)

•

\mu=2

•

\rho=\min\{\lvert\hskip 0.50003pta-x^{\star}\rvert,\lvert\hskip 0.50003ptb-x^{\star}\rvert\}

we can see that

\mathbb{P}\hskip-1.99997pt\left[\hskip 0.50003pt0\,\leq\,f(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt)-\Popt\,\leq\,B^{\text{CH}}\lambda_{\hskip 0.19925ptn-\hskip-0.39864pt1}^{\hskip-0.79727pt2\hskip 0.19925ptr^{\text{CH}}}\,\text{ for all }\,n\geq N\right]\,\geq\,1-(\beta+\pi)\hskip 1.00006pt,

where

\displaystyle B^{\text{CH}}

\displaystyle=\,\frac{512}{\alpha^{2}\rho^{2}\mu}\hskip-1.00006pt\left(3\hskip 0.50003ptc^{\text{CH}}L_{\hskip-0.40974ptf}+C_{\hskip-0.61455ptf}\right)^{\hskip-1.22911pt2}\hskip-1.99997pt+\frac{7}{2}\hskip 0.50003ptc^{\text{CH}}L_{\hskip-0.40974ptf}+\frac{3}{2}C_{\hskip-0.61455ptf}

and

\displaystyle N

\displaystyle=\,\left\lceil 2\hskip-1.49994pt\left(\frac{2\hskip 0.50003ptB^{\text{CH}}}{3\hskip 0.50003ptc^{\text{CH}}\hskip-0.50003ptL_{\hskip-0.40974ptf}+C_{\hskip-0.61455ptf}}\right)^{\hskip-2.45836ptr^{\text{CH}}}\hskip-1.99997pt-2\right\rceil\hskip-1.00006pt.

Numerical Results.

First, note again that running Algorithm
1
can be interpreted as considering a single sample path of Algorithm
2.2
.
Hence, for a given sample point
\omega\in\Omega
, we compare the best-case (BC) errors given at iteration
n\in\mathbb{N}
, that is,

\lvert\hskip 0.50003pt\min\hskip 1.00006pt\{f(x)\,\colon x\in\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}^{\text{MB}}\hskip-0.74997pt(\omega)\}-\Popt\hskip 0.50003pt\rvert\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\lvert\hskip 0.50003pt\min\hskip 1.00006pt\{f(x)\,\colon x\in\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.19925ptn}^{\text{CH}}\hskip-0.74997pt(\omega)\}-\Popt\hskip 0.50003pt\rvert

(62)

with the corresponding recursive adaptive (RA) errors of Algorithm
1
at iteration
n\in\mathbb{N}
, that is,

\lvert\hskip 0.50003ptf(\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\text{MB}}\hskip-0.74997pt(\omega))-\Popt\rvert\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\lvert\hskip 0.50003ptf(\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.19925ptn}^{\text{CH}}\hskip-0.74997pt(\omega))-\Popt\rvert\hskip 1.00006pt,

(63)

respectively.
We consider the setting with the problem parameters
a=0,b=1
and
x^{\star}=2
, and choose
m=20
and
\tau=2
.
Thus, we can compute the convergence rates
r^{\text{MB}}=0.475<0.5
and
r^{\text{CH}}=0.95<1
as well as the constants

A^{\hskip-0.40974pt\text{MB}}\,\approx\,3578.83504\qquad\text{ and }\qquad A^{\text{CH}}\,\approx\,88.58601\hskip 1.00006pt.

Figure
3
shows the error comparisons for both (a) the MB domain approximation and (b) the CH domain approximation on the same scale for better comparison between both approximation methods.
It can be seen that not only the theoretical bounds are influenced by the different approximation methods but also the convergence speed of the BC and the RA error.
Additionally, we want to highlight the possible improvement of the convergence rate of Algorithm
1
.
Thus, we consider the case with
x^{\star}=0.5\in\relinterior([\hskip 0.24994pt0,1])
and compute

B^{\text{CH}}\,\approx\,304210.83314\qquad\text{ and }\qquad N\,=\,28472\hskip 1.00006pt.

In Figure
3
(c) we can see that with the optimal solution in the interior of the problem domain, we can obtain a faster convergence rate, theoretically and practically.
Figure
3
(d) shows that as soon as

A^{\text{CH}}\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974ptr^{\text{CH}}}\,>\,B^{\text{CH}}\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn-\hskip-0.40974pt1}^{\hskip-0.40974pt2\hskip 0.2048ptr^{\text{CH}}}

for some
n>N
, the improved theoretical convergence bound takes over and guarantees a convergence rate of
2\hskip 0.50003ptr^{\text{CH}}=1.9<2
.

Figure 3:
Comparison of the BC error (
62
), the RA error (
63
), and the theoretical upper bound trajectories for the (a) MB domain approximation and the (b) CH domain approximation.
Depiction of the (c) practical and theoretical accelerated convergence of Theorem
4.2
and (d) comparison of the default and improved theoretical upper convergence bound.
The error trajectories are computed as mean errors over 10 independent test runs and the shaded surrounding area shows two standard errors around this mean error.

5.2.  Data -Driven Distributionally Robust Linear Quadratic Control Problem

As a second numerical example, we consider the classical Linear Quadratic Gaussian (LQG) problem, a cornerstone in optimal control and reinforcement learning, see, for example,
[
2
,
8
]
for a detailed treatment.
We study a discrete-time, linear, time-varying system

\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptt+\hskip-0.40974pt1}\,=\,A_{\hskip 0.2048ptt}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptt}+B_{\hskip 0.2048ptt}\mathbf{U}_{\hskip-0.2048ptt}+\mathbf{W}_{\hskip-0.81949ptt}

for all
t\in[T-1]_{0}
, where
\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptt}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
are the states,
\mathbf{U}_{\hskip-0.2048ptt}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
are the controls (or actions),
\mathbf{W}_{\hskip-0.81949ptt}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
is the process noise, and
A_{\hskip 0.2048ptt}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
and
B_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times m}
are the evolution matrices.
Additionally, the state
\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptt}
is not directly observable, but instead we have access to measurements

\mathbf{Y}_{\hskip-0.81949ptt}\,=\,C_{t}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptt}+\mathbf{V}_{\hskip-0.81949ptt}

for all
t\in[T-1]_{0}
, where
\mathbf{V}_{\hskip-0.81949ptt}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
is the measurement noise and
C_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times n}
is the measurement matrix.
The initial condition
\mathbf{X}_{0}
and the noise vectors
\mathbf{W}_{\hskip-0.81949ptt}
and
\mathbf{V}_{\hskip-0.81949ptt}
for all
t\in[T-1]_{0}
are assumed to be normally distributed with zero mean.
For simplicity, we collect all noise vectors in a single noise variable
\mathbf{Z}
, that is,

\mathbf{Z}=(\mathbf{X}_{0},\mathbf{W}_{\hskip-0.61455pt0},\ldots,\mathbf{W}_{\hskip-0.40974ptT-1},\mathbf{V}_{\hskip-0.61455pt0},\ldots,\mathbf{V}_{\hskip-0.40974ptT-1})\in\Omega\,\coloneqq\,\mathbb{R}^{n}\times(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{T}\times(\mathbb{R}^{p})^{T}

and store the corresponding dimension of the components of
\mathbf{Z}
in a vector
d\in\mathbb{R}^{2\hskip 0.0819ptT+1}
, that is, it holds
\mathbf{Z}_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{k}}
for all
k\in[2\hskip 0.50003ptT+1]
.
Furthermore, we assume the components of
\mathbf{Z}
to be mutually independent, such that with

\mathbb{P}\,\coloneqq\,\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{Z}}\,=\,\bigotimes_{k=1}^{2\hskip 0.0819ptT+1}\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{Z}_{k}}\,=\,\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{X}_{0}}\otimes\left(\hskip 1.00006pt\bigotimes_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{W}_{\hskip-0.73616ptt}}\hskip-1.00006pt\right)\otimes\left(\hskip 1.00006pt\bigotimes_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{V}_{\hskip-0.73616ptt}}\hskip-1.00006pt\right)

as the probability distribution of
\mathbf{Z}
, we obtain the underlying probability space
(\Omega,\mathscr{B}(\Omega),\mathbb{P}\hskip 0.24994pt)
.

Robust Formulations.

Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}
denote the set of all feasible control inputs arising from causal policies, that is, policies where the control
\mathbf{U}_{\hskip-0.2048ptt}
depends only on past observations
\mathbf{Y}_{\hskip-0.61455pt0},\dots,\mathbf{Y}_{\hskip-0.81949ptt}
for all
t\in[T-1]_{0}
.
For cost matrices
Q_{t}\in\mathbb{S}^{\hskip 0.40974ptn}_{+}
and
R_{t}\in\mathbb{S}^{\hskip 0.40974ptm}_{++}
for
t\in[T-1]_{0}
, the standard LQG problem is then

J^{\star}\,=\,\inf\hskip 1.00006pt\left\{\hskip 1.00006pt\sum_{t\hskip 0.40974pt=\hskip 0.40974pt0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}_{\hskip 0.40974pt\mathbb{P}}\hskip-1.99997pt\left[\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptt}^{\top}\hskip-1.00006ptQ_{t}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptt}+\mathbf{U}_{\hskip-0.2048ptt}^{\top}\hskip-1.00006ptR_{\hskip 0.2048ptt}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{U}_{\hskip-0.2048ptt}\right]+\mathbb{E}_{\hskip 0.40974pt\mathbb{P}}\hskip-1.99997pt\left[\mathbf{X}_{T}^{\top}Q_{T}\mathbf{X}_{T}\right]\,\colon\mathbf{U}_{\hskip-0.2048ptt}\in U\right\},

(64)

which can be solved efficiently via the Kalman filter and dynamic programming
[
8
]
.
Indeed, given the system matrices
A,B
and
C
and the cost matrices
Q
and
R
we can first solve the backward Ricatti equation

P_{t}\,=\,A_{t}^{\top}P_{t+1}A_{t}+Q_{+}-A_{t}^{\top}P_{t+1}B_{t}(R_{t}+B_{t}^{\top}P_{t+1}B_{t})^{-1}B_{t}^{\top}P_{t+1}A_{t}

initialized at
P_{T}=Q_{T}
.
Then, considering the covariance matrices
Z_{k}=\text{Var}(\mathbf{Z}_{k})\in\mathbb{S}_{+}^{\hskip 0.2048ptd_{k}}
for all
k\in[2T+1]
and writing

Z=(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{2\hskip 0.0819ptT+1})\in\mathbb{S}_{\hskip 0.61455pt\Omega}\,\coloneqq\,\mathbb{S}_{+}^{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\times(\mathbb{S}_{+}^{\hskip 0.2048ptn})^{T}\times(\mathbb{S}_{+}^{\hskip 0.2048ptp})^{T},

where due to the product structure of
\mathbb{P}
we can identify
Z
with
\text{Var}(\mathbf{Z})
, we can recursively compute the Kalman filter covariance estimations

\Sigma_{\hskip 0.2048ptt}(Z)\,=\,\Sigma_{\hskip 0.2048ptt\hskip 0.0819pt|\hskip 0.0819ptt-1}(Z)-\Sigma_{\hskip 0.2048ptt\hskip 0.0819pt|\hskip 0.0819ptt-1}(Z)C_{t}^{\top}(C_{t}\Sigma_{\hskip 0.2048ptt\hskip 0.0819pt|\hskip 0.0819ptt-1}(Z)C_{t}^{\top}+Z_{T+t+2})^{-1}C_{t}\Sigma_{\hskip 0.2048ptt\hskip 0.0819pt|\hskip 0.0819ptt-1}(Z),

and

\Sigma_{t+1|t}(Z)\,=\,A_{t}\Sigma_{\hskip 0.2048ptt}(Z)A_{t}^{\top}+Z_{t+2}

for all
t\in[T-1]_{0}
initialized at
\Sigma_{0|-1}(Z)=Z_{1}
.
Then, defining

f\colon\mathbb{S}_{\hskip 0.61455pt\Omega}\to\mathbb{R},\,Z\,\mapsto\,\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\trace((Q_{t}-P_{t})\Sigma_{\hskip 0.2048ptt}(Z))+\sum_{t=0}^{T}\trace(P_{t}\Sigma_{\hskip 0.2048ptt\hskip 0.0819pt|\hskip 0.0819ptt-1}(Z))\hskip 1.00006pt,

(65)

we have
J^{\star}=f(Z)

[
39
, Appendix A]
.
In practice, however, a control designer may wish to ensure robustness against potential misspecification of
\mathbb{P}
.
This motivates the
distributionally robust
LQG problem

J^{\star}\hskip-0.74997pt(\mathbb{W})\,\coloneqq\,\inf\hskip 1.00006pt\left\{\sup\hskip 1.00006pt\left\{\hskip 1.00006pt\sum_{t\hskip 0.40974pt=\hskip 0.40974pt0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}_{\hskip 0.40974pt\mathbb{Q}}\hskip-1.99997pt\left[\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptt}^{\top}\hskip-1.00006ptQ_{t}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptt}+\mathbf{U}_{\hskip-0.2048ptt}^{\top}\hskip-1.00006ptR_{\hskip 0.2048ptt}\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{U}_{\hskip-0.2048ptt}\right]+\mathbb{E}_{\hskip 0.40974pt\mathbb{Q}}\hskip-1.99997pt\left[\mathbf{X}_{T}^{\top}Q_{T}\mathbf{X}_{T}\right]\,\colon\mathbb{Q}\in\mathbb{W}\right\}\,\colon\mathbf{U}_{\hskip-0.2048ptt}\in U\right\},

(66)

where
\mathbb{W}
is an
ambiguity set
containing all plausible distributions
[
39
]
.
Assuming that at time
t\in\mathbb{N}
we are given an approximation
\hat{\mathbb{P}}^{(t)}
of the true distribution
\mathbb{P}
with the product structure

\hat{\mathbb{P}}^{(t)}\,\coloneqq\,\bigotimes_{k=1}^{2T+1}\hat{\mathbb{P}}_{k}^{(t)}\hskip 1.00006pt,

where each distribution
\hat{\mathbb{P}}_{k}^{(t)}
for
k\in[2\hskip 0.50003ptT+1]
is a centered Gaussian distribution, a possible ambiguity set
\mathbb{W}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\rho}^{(t)}
may then be constructed by first defining the Wasserstein ball

\mathbb{W}\hskip-1.99997pt\left(\hskip-0.50003pt\hat{\mathbb{P}}_{k}^{(t)}\hskip-1.00006pt,\rho_{k}\hskip-1.00006pt\right)\,\coloneqq\,\left\{\mathbb{Q}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{k}})\,\colon d_{\hskip 0.2048ptW}\hskip-3.00003pt\left(\hat{\mathbb{P}}_{k}^{(t)},\mathbb{Q}\right)\leq\rho_{k}\text{ and }\mathbb{E}_{\hskip 0.2048pt\mathbb{Q}}[\hat{\mathbf{Z}}_{k}]=0\text{ for all }\hat{\mathbf{Z}}_{k}\sim\hat{\mathbb{P}}_{k}^{(t)}\right\}

for all
k\in[2\hskip 0.50003ptT+1]
and then defining the product Wasserstein ball

\mathbb{W}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\rho}^{(t)}\,\coloneqq\,\mathbb{W}\hskip-1.99997pt\left(\hat{\mathbb{P}}^{(t)},\rho\right)\,\coloneqq\,\prod_{k=1}^{2T+1}\mathbb{W}\hskip-1.99997pt\left(\hskip-0.50003pt\hat{\mathbb{P}}_{k}^{(t)}\hskip-1.00006pt,\rho_{k}\hskip-1.00006pt\right)\hskip-1.00006pt.

Here, for
d\in\{n,p\}
we denote
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for the set of all probability measures on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with finite second moment
and
d_{\hskip 0.2048ptW}
for the 2 -Wasserstein distance and
\rho\in\mathbb{R}^{2\hskip 0.0819ptT+1}
is a radius vector with component-wise nonnegative entries.
It has been shown in
[
39
]
that, in the setting described, the problem (
66
) is solved by a Gaussian product measure
\tilde{\mathbb{Q}}^{(t)}
on
\Omega
determined by the ambiguity set
\mathbb{W}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\rho}^{(t)}
which is of the product form

\tilde{\mathbb{Q}}^{(t)}\,=\,\bigotimes_{k=1}^{2\hskip 0.0819ptT+1}\tilde{\mathbb{Q}}_{k}^{(t)}\,=\,\bigotimes_{k=1}^{2\hskip 0.0819ptT+1}\mathcal{N}\hskip-1.99997pt\left(\hskip-1.00006pt0,\tilde{Z}_{k}^{(t)}\hskip-0.50003pt\right)\hskip-1.00006pt,

for some covariance matrices
\tilde{Z}_{k}^{(t)}\in\mathbb{S}_{+}^{d_{k}}
for
k\in[2T+1]
.
In particular, these covariance matrices can be computed by solving a maximization problem over the function
f
defined in (
65
), where we have to consider a matrix analogue
\mathbb{G}_{\rho}^{(t)}
of the product Wasserstein ball
\mathbb{W}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\rho}^{(t)}
as problem domain.
Specifically, instead of assuming that, for
t\in\mathbb{N}
, we have access to an approximation
\hat{\mathbb{P}}^{(t)}
of the true distribution
\mathbb{P}
, we assume to have access to an approximation
\hat{Z}^{(t)}
of the covariance matrix
Z
of
\mathbb{P}
.
