That is, we can reconstruct the distribution
\hat{\mathbb{P}}^{(t)}
via

\hat{\mathbb{P}}_{k}^{(t)}=\mathcal{N}(0,\hat{Z}_{k}^{(t)})

for all
k\in[2T+1]
.
To formulate a matrix analogue
\mathbb{G}_{\rho}^{(t)}
of the product Wasserstein ball
\mathbb{W}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\rho}^{(t)}
we need to define an analogue of the 2 -Wasserstein distance, which is given by the Gelbrich distance (sometimes also called Bures distance or Bures -Wasserstein distance).

Definition 8  –
(Gelbrich Distance)

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}
.
The
Gelbrich distance

d_{\hskip 0.2048ptG}
on the set
\mathbb{S}_{+}^{d}
is defined by

d_{\hskip 0.2048ptG}(A,B)\,=\,\left(\trace(A)+\trace(B)-2\hskip-1.00006pt\trace\hskip-1.99997pt\left(\hskip-1.99997pt\left(A^{1/2}BA^{1/2}\right)^{\hskip-2.04861pt1/2}\right)\hskip-1.99997pt\right)^{\hskip-1.63885pt1/2}

for
A,B\in\mathbb{S}_{+}^{\hskip 0.2048ptd}
.

As for the product Wasserstein ball setting, we first define the restricted Gelbrich ball

\mathbb{G}\hskip-1.99997pt\left(\hskip-1.00006pt\hat{Z}_{k}^{(t)}\hskip-1.00006pt,\rho_{k}\hskip-1.00006pt\right)\,\coloneqq\,\left\{Y\in\mathbb{S}_{+}^{\hskip 0.2048ptd_{k}}\,\colon d_{\hskip 0.2048ptG}\hskip-2.5pt\left(\hskip-1.00006pt\hat{Z}_{k}^{(t)},Y\hskip-0.50003pt\right)^{\hskip-1.22911pt2}\hskip-1.00006pt\leq\rho_{k}^{2}\text{ and }Y\succeq\lambda_{\text{min}}\hskip-1.99997pt\left(\hat{\mathbf{Z}}_{k}^{(t)}\right)\hskip-1.99997ptI\right\}

(67)

for
k\in[2T+1]
and then define the restricted product Gelbrich ball

\mathbb{G}_{\rho}^{(t)}\,\coloneqq\,\mathbb{G}\hskip-1.99997pt\left(\hskip-1.00006pt\hat{Z}^{(t)}\hskip-1.00006pt,\rho\right):=\prod_{k\hskip 0.40974pt=1}^{2\hskip 0.40974ptT+1}\mathbb{G}\hskip-1.99997pt\left(\hskip-0.50003pt\hat{\mathbf{Z}}_{k}^{(t)}\hskip-1.00006pt,\rho_{k}\hskip-1.00006pt\right)\hskip-1.00006pt.

Given the product Gelbrich ball
\mathbb{G}_{\rho}^{(t)}
we now can state one of the main results in
[
39
]
, namely that

J^{\star}\hskip-2.5pt\left(\hskip-0.50003pt\mathbb{W}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\rho}^{(t)}\hskip-1.00006pt\right)\,=\,\max\hskip 0.0pt\left\{\hskip-1.00006ptf(Z)\,\colon Z\in\mathbb{G}_{\rho}^{(t)}\hskip-1.00006pt\right\}

if we additionally assume that
Z_{k}
is
positive definite
for all
k\in\{T+2,\ldots,2T+1\}
.
Hence, we have derived a way to compute a solution to the distributionally robust LQG problem with ambiguity set
\mathbb{W}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\rho}^{(t)}
as a maximization problem of
f
over the problem domain
\mathbb{G}_{\rho}^{(t)}
for all
t\in\mathbb{N}
.

Connection to our Setting.

In the above, we have derived a way to compute the optimal value of the distributionally robust LQG problem (
66
) over
\mathbb{W}_{\hskip-0.81949pt\rho}^{(t)}
as a maximization problem of the function
f
from (
65
) over the set
\mathbb{G}_{\rho}^{(t)}
for all
t\in\mathbb{N}
.
Since the function
f
is concave and smooth
[
39
, Proposition 4.2]
we now want to apply Algorithm
1
to use an input stream of covariance matrix collection approximations to solve the limit problem

\text{maximize }\;f(X)\;\text{ subject to }\;X\in D\hskip 1.00006pt,

where
D\coloneqq\mathbb{G}(Z,\rho)
and the radius vector
\rho\in\mathbb{R}^{2T+1}
is fixed.
Hence, theoretically, instead of assuming access to a single covariance matrix collection
\hat{Z}^{(t)}
as before, we assume to have access to a measurable covariance matrix collection map
\hat{\mathbf{Z}}^{(t)}\in\mathbb{S}_{\hskip 0.61455pt\Omega}
at each time step
t\in\mathbb{N}
.
That is, for any sample point
\omega\in\Omega
we obtain an approximation
\hat{\mathbf{Z}}^{(t)}\hskip-1.00006pt(\omega)
of the true covariance matrix collection
Z
.
Here, the domain approximation process
\mathbf{D}^{\mathbb{G}}
is canonically given by our previous analysis and constructions, that is, we define

\mathbf{D}^{\mathbb{G}}\colon\mathbb{N}\times\Omega\to\mathscr{D},\;(t,\omega)\,\mapsto\,\mathbb{G}\hskip-1.99997pt\left(\hskip-0.50003pt\hat{\mathbf{Z}}^{(t)}\hskip-1.00006pt,\rho\hskip-0.50003pt\right)\hskip-1.00006pt.

(68)

That
\mathbf{D}^{\mathbb{G}}
in fact is a stochastic process follows from Lemma
B.4
in Appendix
B.4
.
Furthermore, the next theorem shows that if our covariance matrix collection approximations are of good quality, then this directly transfers to the domain approximations themselves, which is another motivation for using Algorithm
1
.
A detailed proof of the next theorem can be found in Appendix
B.4
.

Theorem 5

Let
\mathbf{D}^{\mathbb{G}}
be the domain approximation process defined as in (
68
).
If there exist
\beta\in[\hskip 0.24994pt0,1)
and
\eta\geq 0
such that

\mathbb{P}\hskip-1.99997pt\left[\hskip 0.50003pt\limsup_{t\hskip 0.40974pt\to\hskip 0.40974pt\hskip 0.40974pt\infty}\hskip 2.5pt\lVert\hskip 0.50003pt\hat{\mathbf{Z}}^{(t)}-Z\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert_{F}\,\leq\,\eta\right]\,\geq\,1-\beta\hskip 1.00006pt,

(69)

then there exists some
M\geq 0
such that

\mathbb{P}\hskip-1.99997pt\left[\hskip 0.50003pt\limsup_{t\hskip 0.40974pt\to\hskip 0.40974pt\infty}\hskip 2.5ptd_{H}(\hskip 0.24994pt\mathbf{D}_{t}^{\mathbb{G}},D)\,\leq\,M\hskip-0.24994pt\sqrt{\hskip 0.50003pt\eta\hskip 1.00006pt}\hskip 1.00006pt\right]\geq 1-\beta\hskip 1.00006pt.

Numerical Results.

Note again that running Algorithm
1
can be interpreted as considering a single sample path of Algorithm
2.2
.
Hence, for any sample point
\omega\in\Omega
we compare the BC procedure, where at iteration
t\in\mathbb{N}
we are given the optimal value of the problem

\max\hskip-1.00006pt\left\{f(X)\,\colon X\in\mathbf{D}_{t}^{\mathbb{G}}\hskip-0.74997pt(\omega)\right\}

with the RA procedure, where we are given the evaluation of the iterate of Algorithm
1
, that is,

f\hskip-1.99997pt\left(\hskip-0.50003pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048ptt}^{\mathbb{G}}\hskip-0.74997pt(\omega)\hskip-0.50003pt\right)\hskip-0.50003pt.

We consider the setting where for
d,T\in\mathbb{N}
we construct an LQG system with
n=m=d
and
p=\lceil d/2\hskip 0.50003pt\rceil
.
First, we define the matrices
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
by setting
A_{ij}=1
if
i=j
or
i=j-1
for
i,j\in[n]
and
0
otherwise and
C\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times n}
by setting
C_{ij}=1
if
i=j
for
i,j\in[p]
and
0
otherwise.
We then define the system and cost matrices

A_{t}\,=\,A,\;B_{t}\,=\,I_{m},\;C_{t}\,=\,C,\;R_{t}\,=\,\frac{1}{m^{2}}\cdot I_{m}\quad\text{for}\quad t\in[T-1]_{0}\quad\text{and}\quad Q_{t}\,=\,\frac{1}{n^{2}}\cdot I_{n}\quad\text{for}\quad t\in[T]_{0}

and the true covariance matrices as

X_{0}\,=\,I_{n},\quad\text{and}\quad W_{t}\,=\,0.05\cdot I_{n},\;V_{t}\,=\,0.1\cdot I_{p}\quad\text{for}\quad t\in[T-1]_{0}

and set
\rho\in\mathbb{R}^{2T+1}
to be
10^{-1}
in each component.
We can now compare both procedures in approximating the optimal value of the problem

\text{maximize }\;f(X)\;\text{ subject to }\;X\in D\hskip 1.00006pt,

(70)

where
D=\mathbb{G}(Z,\rho)
, which is equal to the optimal value of the distributionally robust LQG problem (
66
) over
\mathbb{W}(\mathbb{P},\rho)
, over the incoming data stream
\big(\hat{\mathbf{Z}}^{(t)}\big)_{t\in\mathbb{N}}
.
In Figure
4
we set
d=T=10
and compare the (a) trajectories of the BC procedure and the RA procedure as well as the (b) error of both procedures with respect to the optimal solution to the distributionally robust limit problem (
70
) for a number of sample points
N=1000
.
Furthermore, with Table
1
we provide a runtime table of the BC procedure and the RA procedure which tracks the mean computation time
\pm
one standard deviation (SD) and the minimal computation time of both procedures over all combinations
d,T\in\{5,10,15\}
.
Additionally, we provide a normalized root mean squared error (NRMSE) of the error of the RA procedure with respect to the error of the BC procedure over independent sampling rounds for some sampling set
A\subseteq\Omega
.
That is, we compute

\left(\hskip-1.99997pt\frac{1}{N}\sum_{t\hskip 0.2048pt=1}^{N}\left(\hskip-1.00006ptM_{t}^{\text{BC}}-M_{t}^{\text{RA}}\right)^{\hskip-0.81949pt2}\right)^{\hskip-2.45836pt1/2}\hskip-3.00003pt\left(\hskip-1.99997pt\frac{1}{N}\sum_{t\hskip 0.40974pt=1}^{N}M_{t}^{\text{BC}}\right)^{\hskip-1.63885pt-1}\hskip-5.0pt,

where

M_{t}^{\text{BC}}\,\coloneqq\,\frac{1}{\lvert A\rvert}\sum_{\omega\in A}\lvert\hskip 0.50003pt\max\hskip-1.00006pt\left\{f(X)\,\colon X\in\mathbf{D}_{t}^{\mathbb{G}}\hskip-0.74997pt(\omega)\right\}-\Popt\hskip 0.50003pt\rvert\quad\text{ and }\quad M_{t}^{\text{RA}}\,\coloneqq\,\frac{1}{\lvert A\rvert}\sum_{\omega\in A}\lvert\hskip 0.50003ptf\hskip-1.99997pt\left(\hskip-0.50003pt\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.19925ptt}^{\mathbb{G}}\hskip-0.74997pt(\omega)\hskip-0.50003pt\right)-\Popt\hskip 0.50003pt\rvert

are the mean values of the errors of the BC procedure and the RA procedure over
\lvert A\rvert=10
sampling rounds, to highlight that in spite of the benefit in computation time we have relatively similar behavior of the error trajectories, which coincides with what can be observed in Figure
4
(b).

).

Comparison of the mean
\pm

Appendix A
Measurable Correspondences

In this section we briefly introduce the notion of correspondences, their measurability and some basic results on measurability and measurable selectors.
Great sources for this topic are the textbooks
[
1
,
36
,
13
]
from which we will cite most of the following results.

Definition and Basic Results.

In the following, let
(\hskip-0.50003ptS,\Sigma_{S})
be a measurable space and
(\hskip-0.50003ptX,d_{X})
be a Polish metric space. With a correspondence (also known as multifunction or set-valued map)
\varphi
from
S
to
X
, we simply describe a map satisfying
\varphi(s)\subseteq X
for all
s\in S
, in symbols
\varphi\colon S\rightrightarrows X
.
That is,
\varphi
simply is a map from
S
to
\mathscr{P}(\hskip-0.50003ptX)
, where
\mathscr{P}(\hskip-0.50003ptX)
denotes the power set of
X
.
Since correspondences take values in subsets of
X
rather than in points of
X
, measurability is more subtle than for ordinary functions.
Hence, instead of defining the notion of measurability of a correspondence
\varphi
in terms of the preimage, we use the concept of a
lower inverse
of
\varphi
, which is defined as

\varphi^{\hskip 0.2048pt\ell}\hskip-0.50003pt(\hskip-0.50003ptA)\,=\,\{s\in S\,\colon\varphi(s)\cap A\neq\emptyset\hskip 0.50003pt\}

for every
A\subseteq X
.
Given the definition of a lower inverse, we can define the measurability of a correspondence in a similar way as done for usual maps.
The two notions most relevant for the present paper are weak measurability and measurability with respect to closed sets.
The first is often easier to verify directly from the definition, whereas the second is more convenient in later arguments involving closed-valued correspondences and measurable graphs.

Definition 9  –
(Measurability)

A correspondence
\varphi
is called

•

weakly measurable
, if
\varphi^{\hskip 0.2048pt\ell}\hskip-0.50003pt(G)\in\Sigma_{S}
for every
open
subset
G
of
X
.

•

measurable
, if
\varphi^{\hskip 0.2048pt\ell}\hskip-0.50003pt(F)\in\Sigma_{S}
for every
closed
subset
F
of
X
.

Although weak measurability and measurability are not identical in general, they coincide in the case that
\varphi
has compact values, which is most relevant for this paper.
Since our stochastic domain approximations take values in nonempty, compact, and convex sets, this equivalence allows us to move between the two notions without additional effort.

Lemma 12

Let
\varphi
be a correspondence.

•

If
\varphi
is measurable, then it is also weakly measurable.

•

If
\varphi
is compact valued and weakly measurable, then it is also measurable.

Since the values of correspondences are itself sets, it is natural to question whether combining correspondences with set theoretic operations as closure, convex hull, product, union or intersection preserves measurability.
This is given by the next result, which is a collection of multiple smaller results in
[
1
,
36
]
.

Lemma 13

Let
\varphi
be a correspondence and
(\varphi_{n}\hskip-0.50003pt)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of correspondences.

•

The closure correspondence
s\mapsto\cl(\varphi(s))
is weakly measurable if and only if
\varphi
is weakly measurable.

•

If
\varphi
is weakly measurable, then the convex hull correspondence
s\mapsto\convex(\varphi(s))
is weakly measurable.

•

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
.
If
\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{\hskip-0.2048ptN}
are weakly measurable, then the product correspondence

S\rightrightarrows H^{\hskip-0.2048ptN},\;s\,\mapsto\,\varphi_{1}(s)\times\ldots\times\varphi_{\hskip-0.2048ptN}(s)

is weakly measurable.

•

If
\varphi_{n}
is (weakly) measurable for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, then the union correspondence

s\,\mapsto\,\bigcup\hskip 1.99997pt\{\varphi_{n}(s)\,\colon n\in\mathbb{N}\hskip 0.50003pt\}

is (weakly) measurable.

•

If
\varphi_{n}
has closed values and is (weakly) measurable for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and if for each
s\in S
there exists some
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\varphi_{k}(s)
is compact, then the intersection correspondence

s\,\mapsto\,\bigcap\hskip 1.00006pt\{\varphi_{n}(s)\,\colon n\in\mathbb{N}\}

is measurable.

Beyond purely set-theoretic operations, measurable correspondences also arise through inverse images of jointly measurable and continuous maps.
The next result is therefore a basic device for deriving measurability of feasible set correspondences generated by Carathéodory functions.

Lemma 14

Let
Y
be a topological space and
f\colon S\times X\to Y
be a Carathéodory function.
Then, for each open subset
G
of
Y
the correspondence
s\mapsto\{x\in X\,\colon f(s,x)\in G\hskip 0.50003pt\}
is measurable.

Measurability via Distance Functions.

Considering a nonempty valued correspondence
\varphi
, we can consider the associated distance function

\delta\colon S\times X\to\mathbb{R},\;(s,x)\mapsto d_{X}(x,\varphi(s))\hskip 1.00006pt.

The next theorem connects the weak measurability of
\varphi
to properties of the induced distance function
\delta
, which is useful since
\delta
simply is a scalar function.
A proof of this result is provided in
[
1
, Theorem 18.5]

Theorem 6

Let
\varphi
be a correspondence with nonempty values. Then,
\varphi
is weakly measurable if and only if its corresponding distance function
\delta
is a Carathéodory function.

Correspondences with Measurable Graph.

For a given function
g\colon S\to X
, we know that
g
is measurable if and only if its graph

\graph(g)\,=\,\{(s,x)\in S\times X\,\colon g(s)=x\}\,\subseteq\,S\times X

is measurable, that is, an element of the product
\sigma
-algebra
\Sigma_{S}\otimes\mathscr{B}(X)
.
For a correspondence
\varphi
and its counterpart of a graph

\graph(\varphi)\,=\,\{(s,x)\in S\times X\,\colon x\in\varphi(s)\}\,\subseteq\,S\times X

such an equivalence does not hold.
Nevertheless, weak measurability is still strong enough to imply measurable graph properties after passing to the closure correspondence.
A proof of this next result can be found in
[
1
, Theorem 18.6]
.

Theorem 7

Let
\varphi
be a weakly measurable correspondence with nonempty values. Then, the closure correspondence
\cl(\varphi)
has a measurable graph, that is,
\graph(\varphi)\in\Sigma_{S}\otimes\mathscr{B}(X)
.

Measurable Correspondences as Measurable Functions.

As explained in more detail in Section
2
for the Hilbert space
H
instead of the Polish metric space
X
, denoting with
\mathscr{K}
the set of all nonempty and compact subsets of
X
, we obtain a measurable space
(\mathscr{K},\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{K}))
.
The next result now shows that given a (weakly) measurable correspondence
\varphi
, we can derive measurability of its induced function on
\mathscr{K}
.
This is of particular interest since we have to connect the two different modeling approaches from Section
2
and Appendix
B
in the case where
\varphi
has nonempty and compact values.
A proof of this theorem is provided in

[
1
, Theorem 18.10]
.

Theorem 8  –
(Equivalence of Measurabilities)

Let
\varphi
be a correspondence with nonempty and compact values.
Then, the following are equivalent:

•

The correspondence
\varphi
is weakly measurable.

•

The correspondence
\varphi
is measurable.

•

The function
S\to\mathscr{K},\,s\mapsto\varphi(s)
is
\mathscr{B}(\mathscr{K})
-measurable.

Measurable Selectors and the Measurable Maximum Theorem.

A question that arises naturally in the context of correspondences is whether there exists a measurable selector for a given (weakly) measurable correspondence
\varphi\colon S\rightrightarrows X
, that is, a
\Sigma_{S}
-measurable map
f\colon S\to X
satisfying
f(s)\in\varphi(s)
for all
s\in S
.
One such case, for example, is when the correspondence of interest is an optimizer set produced by a Carathéodory objective over a measurable compact-valued correspondence.
In this case, the following theorem yields three facts simultaneously: Measurability of the optimal value function, measurability of the optimizer correspondence, and existence of a measurable selector.
It can be regarded as a measurable counterpart of the famous Berge Maximum Theorem
[
7
, Chapter 6.3]
and a proof of it can be found in
[
1
, Theorem 18.19]

Theorem 9  –
(Measurable Maximum Theorem)

Let
\varphi
be a (weakly) measurable correspondence with nonempty and compact values and
g\colon S\times X\to\mathbb{R}
be a Carathéodory function.
Then, the optimal value function

m\colon S\to\mathbb{R},\;s\,\mapsto\max\hskip 1.00006pt\{\hskip 0.50003ptg(s,x)\,\colon x\in\varphi(s)\hskip 0.50003pt\}

is
\Sigma_{S}
-measurable and the corresponding optimal solution correspondence

\mu\colon S\rightrightarrows X,\;s\,\mapsto\,\argmax\hskip 1.00006pt\{\hskip 0.50003ptg(s,x)\,\colon x\in\varphi(s)\hskip 0.50003pt\}

attains nonempty and compact values, is measurable, and admits a measurable selector.

Appendix B
Additional Proofs

Here, we provide some of the more technical proofs that occurred throughout the paper.
Some of the following proofs strongly rely on the concepts of correspondences and results on their measurability.
Hence, it is suggested to take a look at Appendix
A
or the textbooks
[
1
,
36
,
13
]
before continuing.

To begin with, we point out a fundamental connection between our setting and Appendix
A
.
Recall that from Section
2
we are given the complete probability space
(\Omega,\Sigma,\mathbb{P})
, the Polish metric space
(\hskip-0.50003ptH,h)
, and the measurable space
(\hskip-0.50003pt\mathscr{D},\mathscr{B}(\hskip-0.50003pt\mathscr{D}))
.
Then, since
(\hskip-0.50003ptH,h)
is separable, due to Theorem
A
we have a bijection between the
\Sigma
-measurable maps from
\Omega
to
\mathscr{D}
and the measurable correspondences from
\Omega
to
H
with nonempty, compact and convex values.
Therefore, for any domain approximation process
\mathbf{D}
, the maps
\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, or the random extension domain
\mathbf{E}
may be seen as measurable correspondences, such that all notions and results from Appendix
A
are applicable.
In particular, we note that for any
\Sigma
-measurable map
\varphi\colon\Omega\to\mathscr{D}
and any open or closed subset
G\subseteq H
we have

\Omega\hskip-0.50003pt\setminus\hskip-1.00006pt\{\varphi\subseteq G\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,\{\hskip 0.50003pt\varphi\cap(H\hskip-0.50003pt\setminus\hskip-1.00006ptG)\neq\emptyset\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,\varphi^{\hskip 0.2048pt\ell}\hskip-0.50003pt(H\hskip-0.50003pt\setminus\hskip-1.00006ptG)\in\Sigma\hskip 1.00006pt,

(71)

such that implicitly
\{\varphi\subseteq G\}\in\Sigma
.
Furthermore, we include a useful technical result.

Lemma 15

Let
\varphi\colon\Omega\rightrightarrows H
be a measurable correspondence,
A\in\Sigma
and
G\subseteq H
.
Then, the correspondence

\varphi_{\hskip-0.40974ptA,G}\colon\Omega\rightrightarrows H,\;\omega\,\mapsto\,\mathbf{1}_{\hskip-0.40974ptA}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)\hskip 0.74997pt\varphi(\omega)+(1-\mathbf{1}_{\hskip-0.40974ptA}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega))\hskip 0.74997ptG

is measurable.

Proof.
.

Let
F\subseteq H
be closed.
Then,

\displaystyle\varphi_{\hskip-0.81949ptA,G}^{\hskip 0.2048pt\ell}(F)

\displaystyle=\,\{\omega\in\Omega\,\colon\varphi_{\hskip-0.40974ptA,G}(\omega)\cap F\neq\emptyset\hskip 0.50003pt\}\cap(A\cup(\Omega\hskip-0.50003pt\setminus\hskip-1.00006ptA))

\displaystyle=\,(\{\omega\in\Omega\,\colon\varphi(\omega)\cap F\neq\emptyset\hskip 0.50003pt\}\cap A)\cup(\{\omega\in\Omega\,\colon G\cap F\neq\emptyset\hskip 0.50003pt\}\cap(\Omega\hskip-0.50003pt\setminus\hskip-1.00006ptA))

\displaystyle=\,(\varphi^{\hskip 0.2048pt\ell}\hskip-0.50003pt(F)\cap A)\cup\left\{\hskip-3.50006pt\begin{array}[]{cl}\Omega\hskip-0.50003pt\setminus\hskip-1.00006ptA&\text{if }G\cap F\neq\emptyset\\
\hskip 1.00006pt\emptyset&\text{if }G\cap F=\emptyset\end{array}\hskip-3.50006pt\right\}\in\Sigma\hskip 1.00006pt,

since
\varphi^{\hskip 0.2048pt\ell}\hskip-0.50003pt(F)\in\Sigma
due to the measurability of
\varphi
and
A\in\Sigma
by assumption.
∎

B.1.  Proofs of Section 2

Stochastic Approximation Processes.

.

Proof of Remark
2.1
.

We first show that the set
N_{0}^{+}\subseteq\Omega
defined as in (
6
) is measurable by showing that the sets
\{\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt\subseteq\mathbf{E}\hskip 0.24994pt\}\subseteq\Omega
are measurable for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
To this end, we fix
n\in\mathbb{N}
and note that for any
\omega\in\Omega
it holds that

\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)\subseteq\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{E}(\omega)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)\cap(\Omega\hskip-0.50003pt\setminus\hskip-1.00006pt\mathbf{E}(\omega))=\emptyset\hskip 1.00006pt.

(72)

Define the set

A\,=\,\{(\omega,x)\in\Omega\times H\,\colon x\in\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)\text{ and }x\notin\mathbf{E}(\omega)\}\,=\,\graph(\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn})\cap(\Omega\times H\hskip-0.50003pt\setminus\hskip-1.00006pt\hskip-0.50003pt\graph(\mathbf{E}))\,\subseteq\,\Omega\times H

and note that
\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}
and
\mathbf{E}
can be interpreted as closed valued measurable correspondences, hence
A\in\Sigma\otimes\mathscr{B}(\hskip-0.50003ptH)
due to Theorem
A
.
Using (
72
) we can now find that

\Omega\hskip-0.50003pt\setminus\hskip-1.00006pt\{\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt\subseteq\mathbf{E}\hskip 0.24994pt\}\,=\,\{\omega\in\Omega\,\colon\exists\hskip 0.50003ptx\in H\hskip 1.00006pt\colon x\in\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)\text{ and }x\notin\mathbf{E}(\omega)\}\,=\,\pi(A)\hskip 1.00006pt,

(73)

where
\pi\colon\Omega\times H\to\Omega
denotes the projection onto the first component.
Since by assumption
(\Omega,\Sigma,\mathbb{P})
is complete probability space, we have that projections of
\Sigma\otimes\mathscr{B}(\hskip-0.50003ptH)
-measurable sets are
\Sigma
-measurable, such that overall with (
73
) we find
\{\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt\subseteq\mathbf{E}\hskip 0.24994pt\}\in\Sigma
.
Furthermore, by (
71
) we have that
\{\mathbf{E}\subseteq\dom(f)\}\in\Sigma
and

\{(H\hskip-0.50003pt\setminus\hskip-1.00006pt\mathbf{E})\cap F\neq\emptyset\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,\{F\hskip-0.50003pt\setminus\hskip-1.00006pt(\mathbf{E}\cap F)\neq\emptyset\hskip 0.50003pt\}=\{\mathbf{E}\not\subseteq F\}\in\Sigma

for any closed subset
F\subseteq H
.
Hence, the complement correspondence
H\hskip-0.50003pt\setminus\hskip-1.00006pt\mathbf{E}
is measurable, such that again by (
71
) we have

\{\hskip-0.35004ptD\subseteq\mathbf{E}\hskip 0.24994pt\}\,=\,\{(H\hskip-0.50003pt\setminus\hskip-1.00006pt\mathbf{E})\subseteq(H\hskip-0.50003pt\setminus\hskip-1.00006ptD)\}\in\Sigma\hskip 1.00006pt.

Overall we now obtain that

N_{0}^{+}\,=\,\bigcap\hskip 1.99997pt\{\{\hskip 0.24994pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt\subseteq\mathbf{E}\hskip 0.24994pt\}\,\colon n\in\mathbb{N}\hskip 0.24994pt\}\,\cap\,\{\hskip-0.35004ptD\subseteq\mathbf{E}\hskip 0.24994pt\}\,\cap\,\{\mathbf{E}\subseteq\dom(f)\}\in\Sigma\hskip 1.00006pt.

For the second part of the statement we again note that
\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}
can be interpreted as a measurable correspondence for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, such that using Lemma
A
, we obtain that the union correspondence

\varphi\colon\Omega\rightrightarrows H,\;\omega\,\mapsto\,\bigcup\hskip 1.99997pt\{\hskip 0.24994pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)\,\colon n\in\mathbb{N}\hskip 0.24994pt\}\cup D

is measurable.
Hence, by Lemma
A
we also obtain that the closure correspondence
\cl(\varphi)
and the closed convex hull correspondence
\convex(\cl(\varphi))
are measurable correspondences.
Furthermore, under Assumption
2.1
, we know that

\mathbb{P}\hskip-1.99997pt\left[\hskip 0.74997pt\bigcup\hskip 1.99997pt\{\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\hskip 0.50003pt\colon n\in\mathbb{N}\hskip 0.24994pt\}\text{ is bounded}\hskip 0.50003pt\right]\,\geq\,\mathbb{P}\hskip-1.99997pt\left[\hskip 0.50003pt\limsup_{n\hskip 0.40974pt\to\hskip 0.40974pt\infty}\hskip 1.00006ptd_{H}(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\hskip 0.50003pt,D)\,\leq\,\eta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}\hskip-0.50003pt\right]\,\geq\,1-\beta_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}\hskip 1.00006pt.

(74)

Thus, denoting with
B
the measured event in (
74
), we can consider the correspondence

\omega\,\mapsto\,\mathbf{1}_{\hskip-0.40974ptB}\hskip-0.24994pt(\omega)\hskip-0.24994pt\convex(\cl(\varphi))(\omega)+(1-\mathbf{1}_{\hskip-0.40974ptB}\hskip-0.24994pt(\omega))\hskip 0.74997pt\{0\}\hskip 1.00006pt,

(75)

which is measurable by Lemma
B
.
Since the correspondence (
75
) now attains nonempty, compact, and convex values it can be interpreted as a
\Sigma
-measurable map from
\Omega
to
\mathscr{D}
satisfying (
7
) and the claim follows.
∎

Stochastic Algorithm Definition and Interpretation.

.

Proof of Remark
2.2
.

Since
\mathbf{X}_{0}
is
\Sigma
-measurable we have
\graph(\mathbf{X}_{0}\hskip-0.50003pt)\in\Sigma\otimes\mathscr{B}(\hskip-0.50003ptH)
and since
\mathbf{E}
can be interpreted as a closed valued measurable correspondence, by Theorem
A
, we also have that
\graph(\mathbf{E}\hskip 0.24994pt)\in\Sigma\otimes\mathscr{B}(\hskip-0.50003ptH)
.
Writing

\displaystyle\{\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048pt0}\in\mathbf{E}\hskip 0.24994pt\}\,=\,\{\omega\in\Omega\,\colon\exists\hskip 0.50003ptx\in H\colon\mathbf{X}_{\hskip 0.2048pt0}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)=x\text{ and }x\in\mathbf{E}(\omega)\}\,=\,\pi(\graph(\mathbf{X}_{0}\hskip-0.50003pt)\cap\graph(\mathbf{E}\hskip 0.24994pt))\hskip 1.00006pt,

where
\pi\colon\Omega\times H\to\Omega
denotes the projection onto the first component, now yields the claim since by the completeness of
(\Omega,\Sigma,\mathbb{P})
we have that projections of
\Sigma\otimes\mathscr{B}(\hskip-0.50003ptH)
-measurable sets are
\Sigma
-measurable.
∎

Proof of Lemma
2.2
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
be fixed.
We consider the measurable space
(\Omega,\Sigma)
as well as the Polish space
H
and define the correspondence

\varphi\colon\Omega\,\rightrightarrows\,H,\;\omega\,\mapsto\,\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)

as well as the function

g\colon\Omega\times H\to\,\mathbb{R},\;(\omega,s)\,\mapsto\,-\mathbf{1}_{\hskip-0.40974ptA}(\omega)\langle\hskip 0.50003pts\,|\,\nabla\mathbf{F}_{\hskip-0.40974ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)(\mathbf{Y}(\omega))\hskip 0.50003pt\rangle\hskip 1.00006pt.

The correspondence
\varphi=\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}
is weakly measurable and admits nonempty and compact values.
On the other hand, we know that
A\in\Sigma
, such that
\mathbf{1}_{\hskip-0.40974ptA}
is
\Sigma
-measurable and since
\nabla\mathbf{F}_{\hskip-0.61455ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)(\mathbf{Y}(\omega))
is well-defined for all
\omega\in A
this yields that
\omega\mapsto g(\omega,s)
is
\Sigma
-measurable for all
s\in H
.
Furthermore, for fixed
\omega\in\Omega
, we have that
s\mapsto g(\omega,s)
is continuous, such that
g
is a Carathéodory function.
Hence, we can apply Theorem
A
to obtain that the map

\omega\,\mapsto\,-\max\hskip 1.00006pt\{g(\omega,s)\,\colon s\in\varphi(\omega)\}\,=\,\min\hskip 1.00006pt\{\mathbf{1}_{\hskip-0.40974ptA}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)\hskip 1.00006pt\langle\hskip 0.50003pts\,|\,\nabla\mathbf{F}_{\hskip-0.61455ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)(\mathbf{Y}(\omega))\hskip 0.50003pt\rangle\,\colon s\in\mathbf{D}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)\}

is
\Sigma
-measurable and its corresponding optimal solution correspondence

\Omega\,\rightrightarrows\,H,\,\,\omega\,\mapsto\,\argmin\hskip 1.00006pt\{\mathbf{1}_{\hskip-0.39864ptA}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)\hskip 1.00006pt\langle\hskip 0.50003pts\,|\,\nabla\mathbf{F}_{\hskip-0.79727ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)(\mathbf{Y}(\omega))\hskip 0.50003pt\rangle\,\colon s\in\mathbf{D}_{\hskip 0.19925ptn}\hskip 0.50003pt(\omega)\}

is measurable and admits a
\Sigma
-measurable selector.
∎

B.2.  Proofs of Section 3

Stochastic Convergence Analysis Tools.

.

Proof of Remark
3.1
.

That
\mathbf{C}
is well-defined follows from Remark
2.1
together with the indicator function
\mathbf{1}_{\hskip-0.40974ptN_{0}}
and the convexity of the objective function
f
.
To prove the
\Sigma
-measurability of
\mathbf{C}
we use Theorem
A
.
We consider the measurable space
(\Omega,\Sigma)
as well as the Polish space
X_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}=\dom(f)^{2}\times[1/n,1]
for some fixed
n\in\mathbb{N}
and define the correspondence

\varphi_{n}\colon\Omega\,\rightrightarrows\,X_{\hskip-0.2048ptn},\;\omega\,\mapsto\,\mathbf{1}_{\hskip-0.40974ptN_{0}}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)(\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{E}(\omega)^{2}\times[1/n,1]\hskip 0.50003pt)+(1-\mathbf{1}_{\hskip-0.40974ptN_{0}}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega))(\hskip 0.24994pt\{y\}^{2}\times[1/n,1]\hskip 0.50003pt)\hskip 1.00006pt,

where
y\in\dom(f)
is an arbitrary point, as well as the function
g\colon\Omega\times(\dom(f)^{2}\times(\hskip-0.24994pt0,1])\,\to\,\mathbb{R}
, defined by

g(\omega,(x,s,\lambda))\,=\,\mathbf{1}_{\hskip-0.40974ptN_{0}}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)\frac{2\,}{\,\lambda^{2}}(f(x+\lambda(s-x))-f(x)-\lambda\langle\hskip 0.50003pts-x\,|\,\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x)\rangle)

for all
\omega\in\Omega
and
(x,s,\lambda)\in\dom(f)^{2}\times(\hskip-0.24994pt0,1]
.
The correspondence
\varphi_{n}
is well-defined and attains nonempty and compact values since we have that
\mathbf{E}\subseteq\dom(f)
on
N_{0}
and
\mathbf{E}
attains nonempty and compact values by assumption.
Since,
\mathbf{E}
can be interpreted as a measurable correspondence, using Lemma
B
, it is straightforward to verify that
\varphi_{n}
is weakly measurable.
On the other hand, the function
g
restricted to the domain
\Omega\times X_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}
is a Carathéodory function.
Indeed, for any tuple
(x,s,\lambda)\in X_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}
we have that
\omega\mapsto g(\omega,(x,s,\lambda))
is
\Sigma
-measurable since
N_{0}\in\Sigma
, and for any
\omega\in\Omega
we have that
(x,s,\lambda)\mapsto g(\omega,(x,s,\lambda))
is continuous due to the continuity of
f
and
\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf
on
\dom(f)
.
Hence, we can apply Theorem
A
to obtain that the function

m_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\colon\Omega\,\to\,\mathbb{R},\;\omega\,\mapsto\,\max\hskip 1.00006pt\{\hskip 0.50003ptg(\omega,(x,s,\lambda))\,\colon(x,s,\lambda)\in\varphi_{n}(\omega)\}

is
\Sigma
-measurable.
Note that since for
\omega\notin N_{0}
it holds
g(\omega,(x,s,\lambda))=0
independent of
(x,s,\lambda)\in X_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}
we have that
m_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}(\omega)=0
, such that for any
\omega\in\Omega
we can write

\displaystyle m_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}(\omega)

\displaystyle\,=\,\max\hskip 1.00006pt\{\hskip 0.50003ptg(\omega,(x,s,\lambda))\,\colon x,s,\in\mathbf{E}(\omega),\lambda\in[1/n,1]\hskip 0.50003pt\}

\displaystyle\,=\,\max\hskip 1.00006pt\{\hskip 0.50003pt\max\hskip 1.00006pt\{\hskip 0.50003ptg(\omega,(x,s,\lambda))\,\colon x,s,\in\mathbf{E}(\omega)\}\,\colon\lambda\in[1/n,1]\hskip 0.50003pt\}\hskip 1.00006pt.

Finally, using the identity

(\hskip-0.24994pt0,1]\,=\,\bigcup\hskip 1.99997pt\{\hskip 0.50003pt[1/n,1]\,\colon n\in\mathbb{N}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\hskip 1.00006pt,

we obtain that
\mathbf{C}=\sup\hskip 1.00006pt\{m_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\,\colon n\in\mathbb{N}\hskip 0.50003pt\}
is
\Sigma
-measurable.
The second part of the statement can be shown analogously to the deterministic case.
We use that for an
L_{\nabla}
-smooth objective function
f
it holds that

f(x)\,\leq\,f(y)+\langle\hskip 0.50003ptx-y\,|\,\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(y)\rangle+\frac{L_{\nabla}}{2}\lVert\hskip 0.50003ptx-y\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert^{2}

for all
x,y\in\dom(f)
, such that we can bound

\frac{2\,}{\,\lambda^{2}}(f(x+\lambda(s-x))-f(x)-\lambda\langle\hskip 0.50003pts-x\,|\,\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x)\hskip 0.50003pt\rangle)\,\leq\,\frac{2\,}{\,\lambda^{2}}\frac{L_{\nabla}}{2}\lambda^{2}\lVert\hskip 0.50003pts-x\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert^{2}\,=\,L_{\nabla}\lVert\hskip 0.50003pts-x\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert^{2}

(76)

for all
x,s\in\dom(f)
and
\lambda\in(\hskip-0.24994pt0,1]
.
Hence, noting that
\mathbf{C}=0
on
\Omega\setminus\hskip-0.50003ptN_{0}
and using (
76
), we obtain that

\mathbf{C}\,\leq\,\sup\hskip 1.00006pt\{L_{\nabla}\lVert\hskip 0.50003pts-x\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert^{2}\,\colon s,x,\in\mathbf{E}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,\diam(\mathbf{E})^{2}L_{\nabla}

and the overall claim follows.
∎

Proof of Remark
3.1
.

That
\mathbf{L}
is well-defined follows from Remark
2.1
together with the indicator function
\mathbf{1}_{\hskip-0.40974ptN_{0}}
and the compactness of
\mathbf{E}(\omega)
for all
\omega\in\Omega
.
To prove the
\Sigma
-measurability of
\mathbf{L}
we use Theorem
A
.
To this end, we consider the measurable space
(\Omega,\Sigma)
as well as the Polish space
X=\dom(f)
and define the correspondence

\varphi\colon\Omega\,\rightrightarrows\,X,\;\omega\,\mapsto\,\mathbf{1}_{\hskip-0.40974ptN_{0}}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{E}(\omega)+(1-\mathbf{1}_{\hskip-0.40974ptN_{0}}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega))\hskip 0.50003pt\{y\}\hskip 1.00006pt,

where
y\in\dom(f)
is an arbitrary point, as well as the function

g\colon\Omega\times X\,\to\,\mathbb{R},\;(\omega,x)\,\mapsto\,\mathbf{1}_{\hskip-0.40974ptN_{0}}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)\hskip 0.50003pt\lVert\hskip 0.50003pt\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf(x)\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert\hskip 1.00006pt.

The correspondence
\varphi
is well-defined and attains nonempty and compact values since it holds
\mathbf{E}\subseteq\dom(f)
on
N_{0}
and
\mathbf{E}
attains nonempty and compact values by assumption.
Furthermore, since
\mathbf{E}
can be seen as a measurable correspondence, by Lemma
B
, we have that
\varphi
is also weakly measurable.
On the other hand, the function
g
is a Carathéodory function.
Indeed, for any
x\in\dom(f)
we have that
\omega\mapsto g(\omega,x)
is
\Sigma
-measurable since
N_{0}\in\Sigma
, and for any
\omega\in\Omega
we have that
x\mapsto g(\omega,x)
is continuous due to the continuity of
\nabla\hskip-1.00006ptf
on
\dom(f)
.
Hence, we can apply Theorem
A
to obtain that the function

m\colon\Omega\,\to\,\mathbb{R},\;\omega\,\mapsto\,\max\hskip 1.00006pt\{g(\omega,x)\,\colon x\in\varphi(\omega)\}

is
\Sigma
-measurable.
Note that since for
\omega\notin N_{0}
it holds
g(\omega,x)=0
independent of
x\in X
we have
m(\omega)=0
, such that for any
\omega\in\Omega
we can write

m(\omega)\,=\,\max\hskip 1.00006pt\{g(\omega,x)\,\colon x\in\mathbf{E}(\omega)\}\,=\,\mathbf{L}(\omega)\hskip 1.00006pt,

such that
\mathbf{L}
is
\Sigma
-measurable.
∎

Preliminaries and Intermediate Results.

.

Proof of Proposition
3.2
.

We prove the result by induction on
n\geq m+1
.
For the base case, we obtain

T_{m+\hskip-0.40974pt1}\,\leq\,(1-\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptm})\hskip 0.50003ptT_{m}+A_{1}\hskip-0.50003pt\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptm}^{\hskip-0.81949pt1+r}+A_{2}\lambda_{m}\,\leq\,2\hskip-0.50003pt\max\hskip 1.00006pt\{\lvert\hskip 0.50003ptT_{m}\hskip 0.50003pt\rvert,A_{1}\hskip-0.50003pt\}+A_{2}\,\leq\,(m+3)\hskip-0.50003pt\max\hskip 1.00006pt\{\lvert\hskip 0.50003ptT_{m}\hskip 0.50003pt\rvert,A_{1}\hskip-0.50003pt\}\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptm+\hskip-0.40974pt1}^{\hskip-0.40974ptr}+A_{2}\hskip 1.00006pt,

where we used (
15
) in the first,
\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptm}\in(\hskip-0.24994pt0,1]
in the second, and
\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptm+\hskip-0.40974pt1}=2\hskip 0.50003pt(m+3)^{-1}
and
r\leq 1
in the third inequality.
For the induction step
(n\to n+1)
we abbreviate
B=(m+3)\hskip-0.50003pt\max\hskip 1.00006pt\{\lvert\hskip 0.50003ptT_{m}\hskip 0.50003pt\rvert,A_{1}\hskip-0.50003pt\}
and note that
B\geq 2A_{1}
such that

\displaystyle T_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}

\displaystyle\leq\,(1-\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn})\hskip 0.50003ptT_{n}+A_{1}\hskip-0.50003pt\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.81949pt1+r}+A_{2}\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}

(77)

\displaystyle\leq\,(1-\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn})(B\hskip-0.50003pt\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974ptr}+A_{2})+A_{1}\hskip-0.50003pt\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.81949pt1+r}+A_{2}\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}

\displaystyle\leq\,B\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974ptr}(1-\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn})+2A_{1}\hskip-0.50003pt\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974ptr}\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}+(1-\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn})A_{2}+\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}A_{2}

\displaystyle\leq\,B\hskip-0.50003pt\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974ptr}\hskip-1.99997pt\left(\hskip-1.49994pt1-\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-1.99997pt\right)+A_{2}\hskip 1.00006pt,

where we used (
15
) in the first and the induction hypothesis in the second inequality.
Furthermore, by definition of the step-sizes, we know that

\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974ptr}\hskip-1.99997pt\left(\hskip-1.49994pt1-\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-1.99997pt\right)\,=\,\frac{2^{\hskip 0.2048ptr}}{(n+2)^{r}}\frac{n+1}{n+2}\,\leq\,\frac{2^{\hskip 0.2048ptr}}{(n+2)^{r}}\frac{(n+2)^{r}}{(n+3)^{r}}\,=\,\frac{2^{\hskip 0.2048ptr}}{(n+3)^{r}}\,=\,\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn+\hskip-0.40974pt1}^{\hskip-0.40974ptr}\hskip 1.00006pt,

(78)

where we used that
r\leq 1
in the inequality.
Combining (
77
) and (
78
) then yields the first claim.
The second part of the claim now follows by noticing that for
n=1
in the base case we have

T_{1}\,\leq\,(1-\lambda_{0})\hskip 0.50003ptT_{0}+A_{1}\hskip-0.50003pt\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048pt0}^{\hskip-0.81949pt1+r}+A_{2}\lambda_{0}\,=\,A_{1}+A_{2}\,\leq\,2A_{1}\hskip-0.50003pt\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}^{\hskip-0.40974ptr}+A_{2}\hskip 1.00006pt,

where we used (
15
) in the first inequality,
\lambda_{0}=1
in the equality, and
\lambda_{1}=2/3
and
r\leq 1
in the last inequality.
Then, for
B=2A_{1}
in (
77
) and with (
78
) the overall claim follows.
∎

Proof of Lemma
3.2
.

Let
x_{1},x_{2}\in K
and
\lambda\in[\hskip 0.24994pt0,1]
.
Since
D
is compact, there exist unique
y_{1},y_{\hskip 0.12291pt2}\in D
satisfying

h(x_{1},D)\,=\,\lVert\hskip 0.50003ptx_{1}-y_{1}\rVert\quad\text{and}\quad h(x_{2},D)\,=\,\lVert\hskip 0.50003ptx_{2}-y_{\hskip 0.12291pt2}\rVert\hskip 1.00006pt.

Thus, for
z=\lambda\hskip 0.50003pty_{1}+(1-\lambda)\hskip 0.50003pty_{\hskip 0.12291pt2}
we obtain that

\displaystyle h(\lambda\hskip 0.50003ptx_{1}+(1-\lambda)\hskip 0.50003ptx_{2},D)\,\leq\,\lVert\hskip 0.50003pt\lambda\hskip 0.50003ptx_{1}+(1-\lambda)\hskip 0.50003ptx_{2}-z\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert\,\leq\,\lambda\lVert\hskip 0.50003ptx_{1}-y_{1}\rVert+(1-\lambda)\lVert\hskip 0.50003ptx_{2}-y_{\hskip 0.12291pt2}\rVert\hskip 1.00006pt,

where we used that
z\in D
due to the convexity of
D
.
∎

Comments on the Assumptions.

.

Proof of Remark
3.4
.

First, we have that
\mathbb{S}(y,\rho)
is nonempty since
g_{i}\hskip-0.50003pt(y,y)\leq 0\leq\rho
for all
i\in[n]
by (
34
).
Furthermore, the convexity of
\mathbb{S}(y,\rho)
follows from the convexity of the Carathéodory functions in their second argument (
35
).
Since
H
is finite -dimensional, by the
Heine -Borel Theorem
, to show that
\mathbb{S}(y,\rho)
is compact it suffices to show that it is both bounded and closed.
To show the boundedness, take
(x_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathbb{S}(y,\rho)
and assume that
\lVert\hskip 0.50003ptx_{k}\rVert\to\infty
as
k\to\infty
.
Then, the coercivity of
g_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}
implies that
g_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}\hskip-1.00006pt(y,x_{k})\to\infty
as
k\to\infty
, contradicting
g_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}\hskip-1.00006pt(y,x_{k})\leq\rho
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Lastly, the closedness follows from the continuity of
g_{i}
in the second argument for all
i\in[n]
, such that overall the first part of the claim follows.
For the second part, let
\mathbf{Y}\colon\Omega\to H
and
\mathbf{P}\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
be
\Sigma
-measurable.
Considering the correspondences

\varphi_{\hskip 0.2048pti}\colon\Omega\,\rightrightarrows\,H,\;\omega\,\mapsto\,\{x\in H\,\colon g_{i}\hskip-0.50003pt(\mathbf{Y}(\omega),x)<\mathbf{P}(\omega)\}\hskip 1.00006pt,

we can apply Lemma
A
to the Carathéodory function
(\omega,x)\mapsto g_{i}\hskip-0.50003pt(\mathbf{Y}(\omega),x)-\mathbf{P}(\omega)
and the open subset
G=(-\infty,0)
of
\mathbb{R}
to obtain that
\varphi_{\hskip 0.2048pti}
is measurable for all
i\in[n]
.
Now, using Lemma
A
and Lemma
A
also implies that the closure correspondence
\cl(\varphi_{i})
is weakly measurable for all
i\in[n]
.
Considering the intersection correspondence
\varphi\colon\Omega\rightrightarrows H
defined by

\varphi(\omega)\,\coloneqq\,\bigcap\,\{\hskip 0.50003pt\cl(\varphi_{i})(\omega)\,\colon i\in[n]\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,\{x\in H\,\colon g_{\hskip 0.2048pt1}\hskip-1.00006pt(\mathbf{Y}(\omega),x)\leq\mathbf{P}(\omega),\ldots,g_{n}\hskip-0.50003pt(\mathbf{Y}(\omega),x)\leq\mathbf{P}(\omega)\}

for all
\omega\in\Omega
and noticing that
\cl(\varphi_{i})
is nonempty and compact valued for all
i\in[m]
by the first part, we can use Lemma
A
to obtain that
\varphi
is measurable.
Since
\varphi
is again nonempty and compact valued we can use Theorem
A
to derive that
\varphi
interpreted as a map from
\Omega
to
\mathscr{D}
, that is, (
36
), is measurable.
∎

Proof of Lemma
3.4
.

For the first part of the statement let
g
be the induced metric of some norm
\lVert\hskip 1.00006pt\cdot\hskip 1.00006pt\rVert_{g}
on
H
.
Since all norms are equivalent in finite -dimensional Hilbert spaces, we can find constants
0<m_{g}<M_{\hskip-0.2048ptg}
with

m_{g}\lVert\hskip 0.50003pts-x\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert\,\leq\,\lVert\hskip 0.50003pts-x\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert_{g}\,=\,g(s,x)\,=\,\lVert\hskip 0.50003pts-x\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert_{g}\,\leq\,M_{\hskip-0.2048ptg}\lVert\hskip 0.50003pts-x\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert

(79)

for all
x,s\in H
.
Hence, for all
s,x\in H
with
\lVert\hskip 0.50003ptx\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert\to\infty
we have
g(s,x)\geq m_{g}\lVert\hskip 0.50003pts-x\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert\to\infty
, such that
g
is coercive.
Furthermore,
g
is a Carathéodory function due to its joint continuity and the convexity of
g
in the second argument follows from the subadditivity and absolute homogeneity of norms.
For the second part of the statement on the upper bound for the Hausdorff distance, without loss of generality, we can assume that
\rho_{1}>0
.
We denote with
\lVert\hskip 1.00006pt\cdot\hskip 1.00006pt\rVert_{\hskip 0.2048pti}
the norm on
H
inducing the metric
g_{i}
for all
i\in[n]
.
By
[
42
, Theorem 20]
the Hausdorff distance between two nonempty compact and convex sets can be reduced to their boundaries.
Hence, we choose
x_{1}\in\bd(\mathbb{S}(y_{1},\rho_{1}\hskip-0.74997pt))
and since
\mathbb{S}(y_{1},\rho_{1}\hskip-0.74997pt)
is the intersection of the sets
\{x\in H\,\colon g_{i}\hskip 0.24994pt(y_{1},x)\leq\rho_{1}\hskip-0.50003pt\}
, there exists an index
k\in[n]
with
\lVert\hskip 0.50003pty_{1}-x_{1}\rVert_{k}=g(y_{1},x_{1}\hskip-0.74997pt)=\rho_{1}
, such that we can write
x_{1}=y_{1}+\rho_{1}u
for some
u\in H
with
\lVert\hskip 0.50003ptu\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert_{k}=1
.
Furthermore, we know that

\rho_{1}\lVert\hskip 0.50003ptu\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert_{i}\,=\,\lVert\hskip 0.50003pty_{1}-x_{1}\rVert_{i}\,=\,g_{i}\hskip 0.24994pt(y_{1},x_{1}\hskip-0.74997pt)\,\leq\,\rho_{1}\hskip 1.00006pt,

such that
\lVert\hskip 0.50003ptu\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert_{i}\leq 1
for all
i\in[n]
.
Defining
x_{2}=y_{\hskip 0.12291pt2}+\rho_{2}u
we then obtain that

g_{i}\hskip 0.24994pt(y_{\hskip 0.12291pt2},x_{2}\hskip-0.74997pt)\,=\,\lVert\hskip 0.50003pty_{\hskip 0.12291pt2}-x_{2}\rVert_{i}\,=\,\rho_{2}\lVert u\rVert_{i}\,\leq\,\rho_{2}

for all
i\in[n]
, such that

x_{2}\in\mathbb{S}(y_{\hskip 0.12291pt2},\rho_{2}\hskip-0.50003pt)
.
Now, taking constants
0<m<M
as in (
79
) but for all norms at once, we find that

\lVert\hskip 0.50003ptx_{1}-x_{2}\rVert\,\leq\,\frac{1}{m}\lVert\hskip 0.50003ptx_{1}-x_{2}\rVert_{k}\,\leq\,\frac{1}{m}(\lVert\hskip 0.50003pty_{1}-y_{\hskip 0.12291pt2}\rVert_{k}+\lvert\hskip 0.50003pt\rho_{1}-\rho_{2}\rvert)\,\leq\,\frac{M}{m}\lVert\hskip 0.50003pty_{1}-y_{\hskip 0.12291pt2}\rVert+\frac{1}{m}\lvert\hskip 0.50003pt\rho_{1}-\rho_{2}\rvert\hskip 1.00006pt,

(80)

where we used that
\lVert\hskip 0.50003ptu\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert_{k}=1
in the second inequality.
Therefore, using (
80
) and the compactness of
\mathbb{S}(y_{1},\rho_{1}\hskip-0.74997pt)
we find

\displaystyle\sup\hskip 1.00006pt\{\hskip 0.50003pth(x,\bd(\mathbb{S}(y_{\hskip 0.12291pt2},\rho_{2}\hskip-0.74997pt)))\,\colon x\in\bd(\mathbb{S}(y_{1},\rho_{1}\hskip-0.74997pt))\}

\displaystyle=\,\sup\hskip 1.00006pt\{\hskip 0.50003pth(x,\mathbb{S}(y_{\hskip 0.12291pt2},\rho_{2}\hskip-0.74997pt))\,\colon x\in\bd(\mathbb{S}(y_{1},\rho_{1}\hskip-0.74997pt))\}

\displaystyle\leq\,\frac{M}{m}\lVert\hskip 0.50003pty_{1}-y_{\hskip 0.12291pt2}\rVert+\frac{1}{m}\lvert\hskip 0.50003pt\rho_{1}-\rho_{2}\rvert\hskip 1.00006pt\hskip 1.00006pt,

such that the claim follows by symmetry in the proof.
∎

B.3.  Proofs of Section 4

Inner Domain Approximation.

.

Proof of Proposition
4.2
.

We prove the result by induction on
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
For the base case (
n=m+1
), we note that

T_{m+\hskip-0.40974pt1}\,\leq\,\left(\hskip-1.49994pt1-\frac{\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptm}}{2}\hskip-1.49994pt\right)\hskip-1.00006ptT_{m}-A_{m}\sqrt{T_{m}}\hskip 0.24994ptB_{1}\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptm}+B_{2}\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptm}^{\hskip-0.81949pt1+r}+B_{3}\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptm}\,\leq\,\left(\hskip-1.49994ptT_{m}+4\hskip-1.49994pt\left(\hskip-1.00006pt\hskip-0.50003pt\frac{B_{2}}{AB_{1}}\hskip-1.00006pt\right)^{\hskip-2.04861pt2}\hskip-1.99997pt+B_{2}\hskip-1.99997pt\right)\hskip-1.99997pt\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptm}^{\hskip-0.81949pt2\hskip 0.2048ptr}+B_{3}\hskip 1.00006pt,

where we used (
45
) in the first and
\lambda_{m}\in[\hskip 0.24994pt0,1]
and
2\hskip 0.50003ptr\leq 1+r
in the second inequality.
For the induction step (
n\to n+1
) we distinguish between two cases.
First, assume that

T_{n}\,\leq\,\left(\hskip-1.00006pt\frac{2B_{2}}{AB_{1}}\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974ptr}\hskip-1.00006pt\right)^{\hskip-2.04861pt2}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\sqrt{T_{n}}\,\leq\,\frac{2B_{2}}{AB_{1}}\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974ptr}\hskip 1.00006pt.

(81)

Then,

\displaystyle T_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}

\displaystyle\,\leq\,\left(\hskip-1.49994pt1-\frac{\lambda_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}}{2}\hskip-1.49994pt\right)\hskip-1.00006ptT_{n}-A_{n}\sqrt{T_{n}}\hskip 0.24994ptB_{1}\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}+B_{2}\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.81949pt1+r}+B_{3}\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}

\displaystyle\,\leq\,\left(\hskip-1.00006pt\frac{2B_{2}}{AB_{1}}\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974ptr}\hskip-1.49994pt\right)^{\hskip-2.04861pt2}+B_{2}\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974pt2\hskip 0.2048ptr}+B_{3}\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}

\displaystyle\,\leq\,\left(\hskip-1.49994ptT_{m}+4\hskip-1.49994pt\left(\hskip-1.00006pt\hskip-0.50003pt\frac{B_{2}}{AB_{1}}\hskip-1.00006pt\right)^{\hskip-2.04861pt2}\hskip-1.99997pt+B_{2}\hskip-1.99997pt\right)\hskip-1.99997pt\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974pt2\hskip 0.2048ptr}+B_{3}\hskip 1.00006pt,

where we used (
45
) in the first, (
81
) and
2\hskip 0.50003ptr\leq 1+r
in the second, and
T_{m}\geq 0
in the third inequality.
Assume now that (
81
) does not hold.
Then,

\displaystyle T_{n+\hskip-0.40974pt1}

\displaystyle\leq\,\left(\hskip-1.49994pt1-\frac{\lambda_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}}{2}\hskip-1.49994pt\right)\hskip-1.00006ptT_{n}-A_{n}\sqrt{T_{n}}\hskip 0.24994ptB_{1}\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}+B_{2}\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.81949pt1+\hskip-0.2048ptr}+B_{3}\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}

(82)

\displaystyle\leq\,\left(\hskip-1.49994pt1-\frac{\lambda_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}}{2}\hskip-1.49994pt\right)\hskip-1.00006ptT_{n}-2B_{2}\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.81949pt1+\hskip-0.2048ptr}+B_{2}\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.81949pt1+r}+B_{3}\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}

\displaystyle\leq\,\left(\hskip-1.49994pt1-\frac{\lambda_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}}{2}\hskip-1.49994pt\right)\hskip-2.5pt\left(\hskip-1.49994ptT_{m}+4\hskip-1.49994pt\left(\hskip-1.00006pt\hskip-0.50003pt\frac{B_{2}}{AB_{1}}\hskip-1.00006pt\right)^{\hskip-2.04861pt2}\hskip-1.99997pt+B_{2}\hskip-1.99997pt\right)\hskip-1.99997pt\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn-\hskip-0.81949pt1}^{\hskip-0.40974pt2\hskip 0.2048ptr}-B_{2}\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.81949pt1+r}+B_{3}\hskip 1.00006pt,

where we used (
45
) in the first, the negation of (
81
) and
A_{n}\geq A
in the second, and the induction hypothesis (
46
) in the third inequality.
Again, we distinguish between two cases.
First, assume that
r\in[\hskip 0.24994pt0,1/2\hskip 0.24994pt]
.
The claim then follows combining (
82
) and

\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn-\hskip-0.40974pt1}^{\hskip-0.40974pt2\hskip 0.2048ptr}\hskip-1.99997pt\left(\hskip-1.49994pt1-\frac{\lambda_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}}{2}\hskip-1.49994pt\right)\,=\,\frac{\hskip-3.99994pt4^{\hskip 0.2048ptr}}{(n+1)^{2\hskip 0.2048ptr}}\frac{n+1}{n+2}\,\leq\,\frac{\hskip-3.99994pt4^{\hskip 0.2048ptr}}{(n+1)^{2\hskip 0.2048ptr}}\frac{(n+1)^{2\hskip 0.2048ptr}}{(n+2)^{2\hskip 0.2048ptr}}\,=\,\frac{\hskip-3.99994pt4^{\hskip 0.2048ptr}}{(n+2)^{2\hskip 0.2048ptr}}\,=\,\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974pt2\hskip 0.2048ptr}\hskip 0.50003pt,

where we used that
2\hskip 0.50003ptr\leq 1
in the inequality.
On the other hand, if
r\in[1/2,1]
, then we have

\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn-\hskip-0.40974pt1}^{\hskip-0.40974pt2\hskip 0.2048ptr}\hskip-1.99997pt\left(\hskip-1.49994pt1-\frac{\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}}{2}\hskip-1.49994pt\right)\,=\,\left(\frac{2}{n+1}\right)^{\hskip-2.04861pt2\hskip 0.2048ptr}\frac{n+1}{n+2}\,=\,\left(\frac{2}{n+1}\right)^{\hskip-2.04861pt2\hskip 0.2048ptr}\hskip-3.00003pt\left(\frac{n+1}{n+2}\right)^{\hskip-2.04861pt2\hskip 0.2048ptr}\hskip-3.00003pt\left(\frac{n+1}{n+2}\right)^{\hskip-2.04861pt1-2\hskip 0.2048ptr}\hskip-5.0pt\,=\,\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974pt2\hskip 0.2048ptr}\left(1+\frac{1}{n+1}\right)^{\hskip-2.04861pt2\hskip 0.2048ptr-1}\hskip-15.00002pt.

(83)

Furthermore, by concavity of the function
x\mapsto(1+x)^{s}
for
x,s\in[0,1]
, we have
(1+x)^{s}\leq 1+sx
, such that with (
83
) we have

\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn-\hskip-0.40974pt1}^{\hskip-0.40974pt2\hskip 0.2048ptr}\hskip-1.99997pt\left(\hskip-1.49994pt1-\frac{\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}}{2}\hskip-1.49994pt\right)\,\leq\,\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974pt2\hskip 0.2048ptr}\left(1+\frac{2\hskip 0.50003ptr-1}{n+1}\right)\,\leq\,\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974pt2\hskip 0.2048ptr}+\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974pt2\hskip 0.2048ptr}\frac{2}{n+2}\,=\,\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974pt2\hskip 0.2048ptr}+\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974pt2\hskip 0.2048ptr+\hskip-0.40974pt1}\hskip 1.00006pt.

(84)

Writing

C\,=\,T_{m}+4\hskip-1.49994pt\left(\hskip-1.00006pt\hskip-0.50003pt\frac{B_{2}}{AB_{1}}\hskip-1.00006pt\right)^{\hskip-2.04861pt2}\hskip-1.99997pt+B_{2}

and choosing

N\,=\,\max\left\{\left\lceil 2\hskip-1.49994pt\left(\frac{C}{B_{2}}\right)^{\hskip-2.45836pt1/r}\hskip-3.99994pt-2\right\rceil\hskip-2.5pt,\hskip 0.50003ptm\right\}\in\mathbb{N}

together with (
84
) we the find

\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn-\hskip-0.40974pt1}^{\hskip-0.40974pt2\hskip 0.2048ptr}\hskip-1.99997pt\left(\hskip-1.49994pt1-\frac{\lambda_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}}{2}\hskip-1.49994pt\right)\hskip-1.49994ptC-B_{2}\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.81949pt1+r}\,\leq\,C\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974pt2\hskip 0.2048ptr}+\left(C\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974ptr}-B_{2}\right)\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.81949pt1+r}\,\leq\,C\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974pt2\hskip 0.2048ptr}+\left(C\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptN}^{\hskip-0.40974ptr}-B_{2}\right)\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.81949pt1+r}\,\leq\,C\lambda_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974pt2\hskip 0.2048ptr}

for all
n\geq N
, such that together with (
82
) the claim follows.
∎

Comments on the Assumptions.

.

Proof of Lemma
4.3
.
.

First, we consider the dilation
X\oplus Y
.
It is straightforward to verify that
X\oplus Y
is nonempty, bounded and convex.
To show the closedness, let
(z_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.24994pt)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq X\oplus Y
be a sequence with
z_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\to z\in H
as
n\to\infty
.
Then, there exist sequences
(x_{n}\hskip-0.24994pt)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq X
and
(y_{n}\hskip-0.24994pt)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq Y
with
z_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}=x_{n}+y_{n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Since
X
and
Y
are bounded, by Bolzano -Weierstraß there exist joint subsequences
(x_{n_{k}}\hskip-0.50003pt)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq X
and
(y_{n_{k}}\hskip-0.50003pt)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq Y
as well as
x\in X
and
y\in Y
with
x_{n_{k}}\to x
and
y_{n_{k}}\to y
as
k\to\infty
.
Overall this yields

z\,=\,\lim_{n\to\infty}z_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\,=\,\lim_{k\to\infty}z_{n_{k}}\,=\,\lim_{k\to\infty}x_{n_{k}}+y_{n_{k}}\,=\,x+y\in X\oplus Y\hskip 1.00006pt,

such that
X\oplus Y
is bounded and closed and therefore compact.
Now, we consider the erosion
X\ominus Y
.
Let
z\in X\ominus Y
and
\rho_{X},\rho_{Y}\geq 0
with
X\subseteq\mathbb{B}(0,\rho_{X}\hskip-0.24994pt)
and
Y\subseteq\mathbb{B}(0,\rho_{Y}\hskip-0.24994pt)
.
Then, for each
y\in Y
we have
z+y\in X\subseteq\mathbb{B}(0,\rho_{X}\hskip-0.24994pt)
such that

\rho_{X}\,\geq\,\lVert\hskip 0.50003ptz+y\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert\,\geq\,\lVert\hskip 0.50003ptz\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert-\lVert\hskip 0.50003pty\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert\,\geq\,\lVert\hskip 0.50003ptz\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert-\rho_{Y}\hskip 1.00006pt,

showing that
X\ominus Y\subseteq\mathbb{B}(0,\rho_{X}+\rho_{Y}\hskip-0.24994pt)
is bounded.
For the closedness let
(z_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.24994pt)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq X\ominus Y
be a sequence with
z_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\to z\in H
as
n\to\infty
.
Then, for each
y\in Y
we have that the sequence
(z_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}+y)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq X
is bounded, such that by Bolzano -Weierstraß there exists a subsequence
(z_{n_{k}}+y)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq X
and
x\in X
with
z_{n_{k}}+y\to x
as
k\to\infty
.
Overall, by the laws of limits, this yields

z+y\,=\,\lim_{n\to\infty}z_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}+y\,=\,\lim_{k\to\infty}z_{n_{k}}+y\,=\,x\hskip 1.00006pt,

such that
z\in X\ominus Y
since
y\in Y
was arbitrary.
Hence, together with the previously shown boundedness of
X\ominus Y
we obtain that
X\ominus Y
is compact.
To show the convexity of
X\ominus Y
let
z_{1},z_{2}\in X\ominus Y
and
\lambda\in[\hskip 0.24994pt0,1]
.
Then, for all
y\in Y
we have

\lambda\hskip 0.50003ptz_{1}+(1-\lambda)\hskip 0.50003ptz_{2}+y\,=\,\lambda(z_{1}+y)+(1-\lambda)\hskip 0.24994pt(z_{2}+y)\,=\,\lambda\hskip 0.50003ptx_{1}+(1-\lambda)\hskip 0.50003ptx_{2}\in X\hskip 1.00006pt,

(85)

where we denoted
z_{1}+y=x_{1}\in X
,
z_{2}+y=x_{2}\in X
and used the convexity of
X
.
Since
y\in Y
was arbitrary by (
85
) we have
z+Y\subseteq X
such that
z\in X\ominus Y
and
X\ominus Y
is convex.
Let us now additionally assume that
X
is
\alpha
-strongly convex for some
\alpha>0
and that (
55
) holds.
If
X
is a point set, then
Y
is a point set as well by (
55
).
In this case, writing
X=\{x\}
and
Y=\{y\}
for some
x,y\in H
we have that
x-y\in\{x-y\}=X\ominus Y
.
Assume now that
X
is no point set.
If
Y
is still a point set, then again
x-y\in X\ominus Y
for all
x\in X
, where
Y=\{y\}
for some
y\in H
.
Hence, we can now also assume that
Y
is no point set.
Due to the compactness of
X
and
Y
we can find
x_{1},x_{2}\in X
with
R_{X}\coloneqq\lVert\hskip 0.50003ptx_{1}-x_{2}\rVert=\diam(X)>0
and
y_{1},y_{2}\in Y
with
R_{Y}\coloneqq\lVert\hskip 0.50003pty_{1}-y_{2}\rVert=\diam(Y)>0
.
Defining
u=(x_{1}-x_{2}\hskip-0.24994pt)/R_{X}\in H
we have
\lVert u\rVert=1
and
x_{1}-R_{X}u=x_{2}\in X
.
Hence, by the
\alpha
-strong convexity of
X
we find

\displaystyle x_{1}-\frac{R_{X}}{2}u+\frac{\alpha R_{X}^{\hskip 0.40974pt2}}{8}z\,=\,\frac{1}{2}x_{1}+\frac{1}{2}(x_{1}-R_{X}u)+\frac{\alpha}{8}\lVert R_{X}u\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert^{2}z\,=\,\frac{1}{2}x_{1}+\frac{1}{2}x_{2}+\frac{\alpha}{8}\lVert\hskip 0.50003ptx_{1}-x_{2}\rVert^{2}z\in X

for all
z\in H
with
\lVert\hskip 0.50003ptz\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert=1
, such that

\left(\hskip-1.49994ptx_{1}-\frac{R_{X}}{2}u\hskip-1.99997pt\right)+\mathbb{B}\hskip-2.5pt\left(\hskip-1.49994pt0,\frac{\alpha R_{X}^{\hskip 0.40974pt2}}{8}\hskip-1.99997pt\right)\,\subseteq\,X\hskip 1.00006pt.

(86)

On the other hand, for all
y\in Y
we have that

\left\lVert\hskip 0.50003pty-\frac{y_{1}+y_{2}}{2}\hskip 0.50003pt\right\rVert\,\leq\,\frac{1}{2}\lVert y-y_{1}\rVert+\frac{1}{2}\lVert y-y_{2}\rVert\,\leq\,R_{Y}\hskip 1.00006pt,

such that

Y\,\subseteq\,\mathbb{B}\hskip-2.5pt\left(\hskip-1.00006pt\frac{y_{1}+y_{2}}{2},R_{Y}\hskip-1.99997pt\right)\hskip 1.00006pt.

(87)

Combining (
86
) and (
87
) then yields

\displaystyle\left(\hskip-1.49994ptx_{1}-\frac{R_{X}}{2}u+\frac{y_{1}+y_{2}}{2}\hskip-1.00006pt\right)+Y

\displaystyle\subseteq\,\left(\hskip-1.49994ptx_{1}-\frac{R_{X}}{2}u+\frac{y_{1}+y_{2}}{2}\hskip-1.00006pt\right)+\mathbb{B}\hskip-2.5pt\left(\hskip-1.00006pt\frac{y_{1}+y_{2}}{2},R_{Y}\hskip-1.99997pt\right)

\displaystyle=\,\left(\hskip-1.49994ptx_{1}-\frac{R_{X}}{2}u\hskip-1.99997pt\right)+\mathbb{B}(0,R_{Y})

\displaystyle\subseteq\,\left(\hskip-1.49994ptx_{1}-\frac{R_{X}}{2}u\hskip-1.99997pt\right)+\mathbb{B}\hskip-2.5pt\left(\hskip-1.49994pt0,\frac{\alpha R_{X}^{\hskip 0.40974pt2}}{8}\hskip-1.99997pt\right)

\displaystyle\subseteq\,X\hskip 1.00006pt,

where we used (
55
) in the second inclusion.
Therefore,
X\ominus Y
is nonempty and the overall claim follows.
∎

As already mentioned in Section
4
, we have to prove that the set

A\,=\,\left\{\mathbf{D}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\text{ \hskip-0.50003ptis }\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{n}\text{-\hskip 0.50003ptstrongly convex and }\diam(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt)^{2}\,\geq\,\frac{16}{\hskip 1.99997pt\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{n}}d_{H}(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn},D)\,\text{ for all }\,n\in\mathbb{N}\right\}

corresponding to (
57
) is measurable.
Rewriting

A\,=\,\bigcup\left\{\{\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\text{ \hskip-0.50003ptis }\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{n}\text{-\hskip 0.50003ptstrongly convex}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,\cap\,\left\{\diam(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt)^{2}\,\geq\,\frac{16}{\hskip 1.99997pt\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{n}}d_{H}(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn},D)\right\}\colon n\in\mathbb{N}\hskip 0.50003pt\right\}

we can see by standard results of measure theory this reduces to showing that
\{\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\text{ is }\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{n}\text{-\hskip 0.50003ptstrongly convex}\hskip 0.50003pt\}
is measurable for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
This follows from the next two results.

Lemma 16

Let
\varphi\colon\Omega\rightrightarrows H
be a measurable correspondence with nonempty and compact values.
Then, the set
\{\varphi\text{ is }\alpha\text{\hskip 1.00006pt-\hskip 0.50003ptstrongly convex}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\subseteq\Omega
is measurable for all
\alpha>0
.

Proof.
.

We fix
\alpha>0
and define the map

f_{\alpha}\colon H^{2}\times\mathbb{B}(0,1)\times[\hskip 0.24994pt0,1]\to H,\;(x,y,z,\lambda)\,\mapsto\,\lambda\hskip 0.50003ptx+(1-\lambda)\hskip 0.50003pty+\frac{\alpha}{2}\lambda(1-\lambda)\hskip 0.50003pt\lVert\hskip 0.50003ptx-y\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert^{2}z\hskip 1.00006pt,

which is jointly continuous.
By definition of strong convexity and since
\varphi
has closed values it now follows that

\displaystyle\varphi(\omega)\text{ is }\alpha\text{\hskip 1.00006pt-\hskip 0.50003ptstrongly convex}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\quad\forall\hskip 0.24994ptx,y\in\varphi(\omega)\,\colon\forall\hskip 0.24994ptz\in\mathbb{B}(0,1)\,\colon\forall\hskip 0.24994pt\lambda\in[\hskip 0.24994pt0,1]\,\colon f_{\alpha}(x,y,z,\lambda)\in\varphi(\omega)

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\quad\forall\hskip 0.24994ptx,y\in\varphi(\omega)\,\colon\forall\hskip 0.24994ptz\in\mathbb{B}(0,1)\,\colon\forall\hskip 0.24994pt\lambda\in[\hskip 0.24994pt0,1]\,\colon h(f_{\alpha}(x,y,z,\lambda),\varphi(\omega))=0

for all
\omega\in\Omega
, where as before
h(x,\varphi(\omega))=\inf\hskip 0.50003pt\{\lVert\hskip 0.50003ptx-y\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert\,\colon y\in\varphi(\omega)\}
describes the distance between a point
x\in H
and the set
\varphi(\omega)\subseteq H
.
Furthermore, since
x\mapsto h(x,\varphi(\omega))
is nonnegative on
H
for all
\omega\in\Omega
, we overall obtain that

\varphi(\omega)\text{ is }\alpha\hskip 1.00006pt\text{-\hskip 0.50003ptstrongly convex}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\sup\hskip 1.00006pt\{\hskip 0.50003pth(f_{\alpha}(x,y,z,\lambda),\varphi(\omega))\,\colon x,y\in\varphi(\omega),z\in\mathbb{B}(0,1),\lambda\in[\hskip 0.24994pt0,1]\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,0

for all
\omega\in\Omega
.
Hence, showing that the map

m\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R},\;\omega\,\mapsto\,\sup\hskip 1.00006pt\{\hskip 0.50003pth(f_{\alpha}(x,y,z,\lambda),\varphi(\omega))\,\colon x,y\in\varphi(\omega),z\in\mathbb{B}(0,1),\lambda\in[\hskip 0.24994pt0,1]\hskip 0.50003pt\}

is
\Sigma
-measurable yields the claim since then
\{\varphi(\omega)\text{ is }\alpha\hskip 1.00006pt\text{-\hskip 0.50003ptstrongly convex}\}\,=\,m^{-1}(\{0\})\in\Sigma
.
To this end, we consider the measurable space
(\Omega,\Sigma)
as well as the Polish space
H^{2}\times\mathbb{B}(0,1)\times[\hskip 0.24994pt0,1]
and define the correspondence

\tilde{\varphi}\colon\Omega\rightrightarrows H^{2}\times\mathbb{B}(0,1)\times[\hskip 0.24994pt0,1],\;\omega\,\mapsto\,\varphi(\omega)^{2}\times\mathbb{B}(0,1)\times[\hskip 0.24994pt0,1]

as well as the function

g\colon\Omega\times H^{2}\times\mathbb{B}(0,1)\times[\hskip 0.24994pt0,1]\to\mathbb{R},\;(\omega,x,y,z,\lambda)\,\mapsto\,h(f_{\alpha}(x,y,z,\lambda),\varphi(\omega))\hskip 1.00006pt.

Since
\varphi
is measurable, the measurability of the correspondence
\tilde{\varphi}
follows immediately.
Furthermore, we can see that
g
is a Carathéodory function.
Indeed, for fixed
(x,y,z,\lambda)\in H^{2}\times\mathbb{B}(0,1)\times[\hskip 0.24994pt0,1]
we have that the map
\omega\mapsto g(\omega,x,y,z,\lambda)
is measurable since
\varphi(\omega)\in\mathscr{K}
and
K\mapsto h(x,K)
is Lipschitz continuous on
\mathscr{K}
for all
x\in H
.
On the other hand, we have that
(x,y,z,\lambda)\mapsto g(\omega,x,y,z,\lambda)
is continuous for each
\omega\in\Omega
since
f_{\alpha}
is continuous and
x\mapsto h(x,K)
is Lipschitz continuous on
H
for all
K\in\mathscr{K}
.
Thus, we can apply Theorem
A
to obtain that the function

m\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R},\;\omega\,\mapsto\,\sup\hskip 1.00006pt\{\hskip 0.50003ptg\,\colon(x,y,z,\lambda)\in\tilde{\varphi}(\omega)\hskip 0.50003pt\}

is
\Sigma
-measurable such that the claim follows.
∎

Lemma 17

Let
\varphi\colon\Omega\rightrightarrows H
be a measurable correspondence with nonempty and compact values and
\boldsymbol{\alpha}
be a nonnegative random variable.
Then, the set
\{\varphi\text{ is }\boldsymbol{\alpha}\text{\hskip 1.00006pt-\hskip 0.50003ptstrongly convex}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\subseteq\Omega
is measurable.

Proof.
.

First, note that if a set is
\alpha_{\hskip 0.2048pt0}
-strongly convex for some
\alpha_{\hskip 0.2048pt0}>0
, then it is
\alpha
-strongly convex for all
\alpha\in(\hskip-0.50003pt0,\alpha_{\hskip 0.2048pt0}]
.
Therefore, we consider function

\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\hskip 0.2048pt0}\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R},\,\omega\,\mapsto\,\sup\hskip 1.00006pt\{\alpha>0\,\colon\varphi(\omega)\text{ is }\alpha\text{\hskip 1.00006pt-\hskip 0.50003ptstrongly convex}\}\hskip 1.00006pt,

which is
\Sigma
-measurable since by Lemma
B.3
it holds

\{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\hskip 0.2048pt0}>t\}\,=\,\bigcup\hskip 1.99997pt\{\{\varphi\text{ is }t\text{\hskip 1.00006pt-\hskip 0.50003ptstrongly convex}\}\,\colon q\in\mathbb{Q}\text{ and }q>t\}\in\Sigma

for all
t>0
.
Hence, we directly obtain
\{\varphi\text{ is }\boldsymbol{\alpha}\text{\hskip 1.00006pt-\hskip 0.50003ptstrongly convex}\hskip 0.50003pt\}\,=\,\{\boldsymbol{\alpha}\leq\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{\hskip 0.2048pt0}\hskip-0.24994pt\}\in\Sigma
.
∎

Lemma 18

Let
\varphi\colon\Omega\rightrightarrows H
be a measurable correspondence with nonempty and closed values and
\mathbf{P}\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
be a nonnegative random variable.
Then, both the dilation correspondence
\varphi\oplus\mathbb{B}(0,\mathbf{P})
and the erosion correspondence
\varphi\ominus\mathbb{B}(0,\mathbf{P})
are measurable.

Proof.
.

First, we consider the dilation correspondence
\varphi\oplus\mathbb{B}(0,\mathbf{P})
and note that for each
\omega\in\Omega
it holds

\varphi(\omega)\oplus\mathbb{B}(0,\mathbf{P}(\omega))\,=\,\{x\in H\,\colon h(x,\varphi(\omega))\leq\mathbf{P}(\omega)\}\hskip 1.00006pt.

(88)

Indeed, if
x\in\varphi(\omega)+\mathbb{B}(0,\mathbf{P}(\omega))
, then there exist
y\in\varphi(\omega)
and
u\in\mathbb{B}(0,\mathbf{P}(\omega))
with
x=y+u
, such that

h(x,\varphi(\omega))\,\leq\,h(y,\varphi(\omega))+\lVert u\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert\,=\,\lVert u\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert\,\leq\,\mathbf{P}(\omega)

for all
\omega\in\Omega
.
Vice versa, if
h(x,\varphi(\omega))\leq\mathbf{P}(\omega)
then there exists some
y\in\varphi(\omega)
with
\lVert\hskip 0.50003ptx-y\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert\leq\mathbf{P}(\omega)
, such that
x=y+(x-y)\in\varphi(\omega)\oplus\mathbb{B}(0,\mathbf{P}(\omega))
for all
\omega\in\Omega
.
Hence, (
88
) follows and as a direct consequence we obtain that

h(x,\varphi\oplus\mathbb{B}(0,\mathbf{P}))\,=\,\max\{h(x,\varphi)-\mathbf{P},0\}

(89)

for all
x\in H
.
Since
\varphi
is measurable with nonempty values, Theorem
A
yields that
(s,x)\mapsto h(x,\varphi(s))
is a Carathéodory function.
Using the representation (
89
) this already implies that

(s,x)\,\mapsto\,h(x,\varphi(s)\oplus\mathbb{B}(0,\mathbf{P}(s)))\,=\,\max\{h(x,\varphi(s))-\mathbf{P}(s),0\}

is a Carathéodory function.
Now, since
\varphi\oplus\mathbb{B}(0,\mathbf{P})
attains nonempty and compact values, we can again use Theorem
A
to obtain that
\varphi\oplus\mathbb{B}(0,\mathbf{P})
is weakly measurable and by Lemma
A
even measurable.
Let us now consider the erosion correspondence
\varphi\ominus\mathbb{B}(0,\mathbf{P})
.
We want to show that
(\varphi\ominus\mathbb{B}(0,\mathbf{P}))^{\hskip 0.40974pt\ell}(F)\in\Sigma
for all closed
F\subseteq H
.
To this end, let
F\subseteq X
be closed and note that since

F\,=\,\bigcup\hskip 1.99997pt\{F\cap\mathbb{B}(0,n)\,\colon n\in\mathbb{N}\hskip 0.50003pt\}

and since the lower inverse of a correspondence is compatible with unions, without loss of generality, we can assume that
F
is even compact.
We then obtain that

\displaystyle\varphi(\omega)\ominus\mathbb{B}(0,\mathbf{P}(\omega))\cap F\neq\emptyset

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\quad\exists\hskip 0.50003ptx\in F\,\colon\forall u\in\mathbb{B}(0,\mathbf{P}(\omega))\,\colon x+u\in\varphi(\omega)

(90)

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\quad\exists\hskip 0.50003ptx\in F\,\colon\forall u\in\mathbb{B}(0,\mathbf{P}(\omega))\,\colon h(x+u,\varphi(\omega))\,=\,0

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\quad\inf\hskip 1.00006pt\{\sup\hskip 1.00006pt\{\hskip 0.50003pth(x+u,\varphi(\omega))\,\colon u\in\mathbb{B}(0,\mathbf{P}(\omega))\}\,\colon x\in F\}\,=\,0

for all
\omega\in\Omega
, where we used that
\varphi(\omega)
is closed in the second and
x\mapsto h(x,\varphi(\omega))
is nonnegative on
H
as well as the compactness of
F
in the third equivalence.
Hence, defining the function

m\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R},\;\omega\,\mapsto\,\inf\hskip 1.00006pt\{\sup\hskip 1.00006pt\{\hskip 0.50003pth(x+u,\varphi(\omega))\,\colon u\in\mathbb{B}(0,\mathbf{P}(\omega))\}\,\colon x\in F\}\hskip 1.00006pt,

by (
90
) it holds
(\varphi\ominus\mathbb{B}(0,\mathbf{P}))^{\hskip 0.40974pt\ell}(F)=m^{-1}(\{0\})
, such that it suffices to show the
\Sigma
-measurability of
m
.
To this end, we define the function

g_{\hskip 0.2048pt0}\colon\Omega\times H^{2}\to\mathbb{R},\;(\omega,x,u)\,\mapsto\,h(x+u,\varphi(\omega))

and note that since
\varphi
is measurable, by Theorem
A
, we know that
(\omega,x)\mapsto h(x,\varphi(\omega))
is a Carathéodory function, such that
(x,u)\mapsto g(\omega,x,u)
is jointly continuous for each
\omega\in\Omega
.
Therefore, we can apply
Berges Maximum Theorem

[
1
, Theorem 17.31]
to obtain that

x\,\mapsto\,\sup\hskip 1.00006pt\{g_{\hskip 0.2048pt0}(\omega,x,u)\,\colon u\in\mathbb{B}(0,\mathbf{P}(\omega))\}

(91)

is continuous for each
\omega\in\Omega
.
By Lemma
3.4
the correspondence
\omega\mapsto\mathbb{B}(0,\mathbf{P}(\omega))
is measurable, such that we can also use Theorem
A
to obtain that

\omega\,\mapsto\,\sup\hskip 1.00006pt\{g_{\hskip 0.2048pt0}(\omega,x,u)\,\colon u\in\mathbb{B}(0,\mathbf{P}(\omega))\}

(92)

is
\Sigma
-measurable for each
x\in H
.
Hence, combining (
91
) and (
92
), the function

g_{\hskip 0.12291pt1}\colon\Omega\times H\mapsto\mathbb{R},\;(\omega,x)\,\mapsto\,\sup\hskip 1.00006pt\{g_{\hskip 0.2048pt0}(\omega,x,u)\,\colon u\in\mathbb{B}(0,\mathbf{P}(\omega))\}

is again a Carathéodory function.
Now, using Theorem
A
, we obtain that

m_{1}\colon\Omega\to\mathbb{R},\;\omega\,\mapsto\,\sup\hskip 1.00006pt\{-g_{\hskip 0.12291pt1}(\omega,x)\,\colon x\in F\}

is
\Sigma
-measurable.
Since
m=-\hskip 0.50003ptm_{1}
the claim overall follows.
∎

Proof of Lemma
4.3
.
.

First, we consider the extension map
\mathbf{D}^{\hskip-0.40974pt+}
.
By Lemma
B.3
we know that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
the map
\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974pt+}
is measurable as a correspondence and therefore
\Sigma
-measurable by Theorem
A
.
Hence,
\mathbf{D}^{\hskip-0.40974pt+}
itself is a stochastic process.
Furthermore, for each
x\in D
we can find
y\in\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)
with
\lVert\hskip 0.50003ptx-y\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert\leq d_{H}(\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega),D)
, such that

x\,=\,y+(x-y)\in\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)\oplus\mathbb{B}(0,d_{H}(\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega),D))\,=\,\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974pt+}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)

for all
\omega\in\Omega
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Thus,
\mathbf{D}^{\hskip-0.40974pt+}
satisfies Assumption
4.1
for
\delta=0
.
For the upper bound on the Hausdorff distance we use that the Hausdorff distance is a metric on
\mathscr{D}
to obtain that

d_{H}(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974pt+}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega),D)\,\leq\,d_{H}(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974pt+}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega),\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega))+d_{H}(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega),D)\,\leq\,2\hskip 0.50003ptd_{H}(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega),D)

for all
\omega\in\Omega
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
where in the second inequality we used that for any
y\in\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974pt+}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)
there exist
x\in\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)
,
u\in\mathbb{B}(0,1)
and
r\leq d_{H}(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega),D)
with
y=x+ru
, such that

h(y,\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega))\,\leq\,h(x,\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega))+\lVert\hskip 0.50003pty-x\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert\,=\,r\,\leq\,d_{H}(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega),D)\hskip 1.00006pt.

Now, we consider the contraction map
\mathbf{D}^{\hskip-0.40974pt-}
.
As before, by Lemma
B.3
we know that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
the map
\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974pt-}
is measurable as a correspondence and therefore
\Sigma
-measurable by Theorem
A
.
Therefore,
\mathbf{D}^{\hskip-0.40974pt-}
is a stochastic process.
Fix now
\omega\in A
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, where
A
is defined as the event corresponding to (
57
).
Let
x\in\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}^{\hskip 0.40974pt-}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)
. Then, due to the compactness of
D
, there exists
y\in D
with
h(x,D)=\lVert\hskip 0.50003ptx-y\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert
.
Defining
u=(x-y)/\lVert\hskip 0.50003ptx-y\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert
we have
y=x-\lVert\hskip 0.50003ptx-y\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert u
but since
x\in\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}^{\hskip 0.40974pt-}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)
we also have that
z=x+d_{H}(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega),D)u\in\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)
.
Hence, we find that

d_{H}(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega),D)\,\geq\,h(z,D)\,=\,\lVert\hskip 0.50003ptz-y\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert\,=\,\lVert\hskip 0.50003ptd_{H}(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega),D)u+\lVert\hskip 0.50003ptx-y\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert u\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert\,=\,d_{H}(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega),D)+\lVert\hskip 0.50003ptx-y\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert\hskip 1.00006pt,

such that
x=y\in D
.
Therefore,
\mathbf{D}^{\hskip-0.40974pt-}
satisfies Assumption
4.2
for
\delta=\gamma
since we restricted ourselves to sample points of the event
A\subseteq\Omega
with
\mathbb{P}[\hskip-0.50003ptA\hskip 0.50003pt]\geq 1-\gamma
.
For the upper bound on the Hausdorff distance, similar to before, we have

d_{H}(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974pt-}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega),D)\,\leq\,d_{H}(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974pt-}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega),\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega))+d_{H}(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega),D)\hskip 1.00006pt,

such that it suffices to bound
d_{H}(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974pt-}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega),\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega))
from above, where
\omega\in A
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
remain fixed.
By
[
42
, Theorem 20]
the Hausdorff distance between two nonempty compact and convex sets can be reduced to their boundaries.
Hence, we can consider
x\in\bd(\mathbf{D}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega))
and there exists
y\in\bd(\mathbf{D}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974pt-}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega))\subseteq\mathbf{D}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)
with
r\coloneqq h(x,\mathbf{D}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974pt-}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega))=\lVert\hskip 0.50003ptx-y\hskip 0.50003pt\rVert
.
Since by choice of
\omega
we have that
\mathbf{D}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)
is
\alpha_{n}
-strongly convex, writing
u=(x-y)/r
, analogously to (
86
), we obtain that

\left(\hskip-1.00006ptx-\frac{r}{2}u\hskip-1.00006pt\right)+\mathbb{B}\hskip-2.5pt\left(\hskip-1.49994pt0,\frac{\alpha_{n}r^{2}}{8}\hskip 0.0pt\right)\,\subseteq\,\mathbf{D}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)\hskip 1.00006pt.

If it would now hold that

h(x,\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega))\,=\,r\,\geq\,\sqrt{\frac{8}{\alpha_{n}}d_{H}(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega),D)}\hskip 1.00006pt,

(93)

then we would obtain

\left(\hskip-1.00006ptx-\frac{r}{2}u\hskip-1.00006pt\right)+\mathbb{B}(0,d_{H}(\hskip 0.35004pt\mathbf{D}_{\hskip-0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega),D))\,\subseteq\,\left(\hskip-1.00006ptx-\frac{r}{2}u\hskip-1.00006pt\right)+\mathbb{B}\hskip-2.5pt\left(\hskip-1.49994pt0,\frac{\alpha_{n}r^{2}}{8}\hskip 0.0pt\right)\,\subseteq\,\mathbf{D}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)\hskip 1.00006pt,

such that
x-(r/2)\hskip 0.50003ptu\in\mathbf{D}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974pt-}\hskip-0.50003pt(\omega)
.
However, this is a contradiction to the choice of
y\in\mathbf{D}_{\hskip 0.2048ptn}^{\hskip-0.40974pt-}(\omega)
, such that the negation of (
93
) must hold.
The claim now follows since we restricted ourselves to sample points
\omega\in A
and it holds
\mathbb{P}[\hskip-0.50003ptA\hskip 0.50003pt]\geq 1-\gamma
by assumption.
∎

B.4.  Proofs of Section 5

Simple Example: Quadratic Objective on Rectangular Domain.

To begin with the derivation of uniform concentration bounds for the domain approximation processes
\mathbf{D}^{\text{MB}}
and
\mathbf{D}^{\text{CH}}
defined as in Section
5
, we first cite a well-known concentration bound for independent bounded random variables
[
11
, Theorem 2.8]
.

Theorem 10  –
(Hoeffding’s Inequality)

Let
\mathbf{Y}_{\hskip-1.22911pt1},\ldots,\mathbf{Y}_{\hskip-1.0243ptn}
be independent random variables such that
a_{i}\leq\mathbf{Y}_{\hskip-1.22911pti}\leq b_{i}
almost surely for all
i\in[n]
.
Then, for all
\varepsilon>0
it holds that

\mathbb{P}\hskip-1.99997pt\left[\hskip 1.00006pt\sum_{\hskip 0.81949pti\hskip 0.40974pt=1}^{n}(\mathbf{Y}_{\hskip-1.22911pti}-\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{Y}_{\hskip-1.22911pti}]\hskip 0.50003pt)\,\geq\,\varepsilon\hskip 1.00006pt\right]\,\leq\,\hskip-0.50003pt\exp\hskip-1.99997pt\left(\hskip-1.99997pt-\frac{2\hskip 0.50003pt\varepsilon^{2}}{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{n}(b_{i}-a_{i})}\right)\hskip-1.99997pt.

(94)

In the following, we use Hoeffding’s inequality (
94
) to derive the constant seen in Proposition
5.1
and Proposition
5.1
.
Recall that for
x>0
the (complete) Gamma function
\Gamma
(see
[
3
, Section 1]
for more details) can be written as

\Gamma(x)\,=\,\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\hskip 0.40974ptx-1}e^{-t}\,dt\hskip 1.00006pt

and satisfies
\Gamma(n)=(n-1)!
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Furthermore, recall the definition of the empirical mean estimator
\mathbf{E}_{\hskip 0.12291ptn}\hskip-0.24994pt(\mathbf{Y})
and the empirical variance estimator
\mathbf{V}_{\hskip-0.81949ptn}\hskip-0.24994pt(\mathbf{Y})
for any random variable
\mathbf{Y}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
as given in (
59
).

Lemma 19

Let
\mathbf{Y}
be a random variable such that
a\leq\mathbf{Y}\leq b
almost surely.
Then, for all
r\in[\hskip 0.24994pt0,1/2)
and for all
\beta\in(0,1]
there exists a constant
c>0
such that

\mathbb{P}\hskip-1.99997pt\left[\hskip 0.50003pt\lvert\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{E}_{\hskip 0.12291ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\mathbf{Y})\hskip-1.00006pt-\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{Y}]\hskip 0.50003pt\rvert\,\leq\,c\hskip 0.50003ptn^{\hskip-0.81949pt-r}\text{ for all }n\in\mathbb{N}\hskip 0.50003pt\right]\,\geq\,1-\beta\hskip 1.00006pt.

In particular, writing
s=1-2\hskip 0.50003ptr
, we can choose

c\,=\,(b-a)\hskip-1.49994pt\left(\hskip-1.00006pt\frac{\Gamma(1/s)}{s\hskip 0.50003pt\beta}\hskip-1.00006pt\right)^{\hskip-2.45836pts/2}\hskip-2.5pt.

Proof.
.

Since we know that
\mathbf{Y}
has values in
[\hskip 0.24994pta,b\hskip 0.50003pt]
, we can use Hoeffding’s inequality (
94
) to obtain that

\mathbb{P}[\hskip 0.50003pt\lvert\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{E}_{\hskip 0.12291ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\mathbf{Y})-\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{Y}]\hskip 0.50003pt\rvert\geq\varepsilon\hskip 0.50003pt]\,\leq\,2\hskip-0.50003pt\exp\hskip-2.5pt\left(\hskip-1.99997pt-\frac{\hskip-1.00006pt2\hskip 0.50003pt\varepsilon^{2}}{(b-a)^{2}}\hskip 1.00006ptn\hskip-1.99997pt\right)

for all
\varepsilon>0
.
Hence, for any
r\in\mathbb{R}
and any constant
c>0
this implies

\mathbb{P}[\hskip 0.50003pt\lvert\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbf{E}_{\hskip 0.12291ptn}\hskip-0.50003pt(\mathbf{Y})-\hskip 0.50003pt\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{Y}]\hskip 0.50003pt\rvert\geq c\hskip 0.50003ptn^{\hskip-0.40974pt-r}\hskip 0.50003pt]\,\leq\,2\hskip-0.50003pt\exp\hskip-2.5pt\left(\hskip-1.99997pt-\frac{\hskip-1.00006pt2\hskip 0.50003ptc^{\hskip 0.40974pt2}}{(b-a)^{2}}\hskip 1.00006ptn^{1-2\hskip 0.2048ptr}\hskip-1.99997pt\right)\hskip-1.00006pt,

such that using the union bound we can derive that
